This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain <note>The "public domain" refers to creative works to which no exclusive intellectual property rights apply; they are owned by the public rather than an individual author or estate.</note>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other marginalia <note>original: "marginalia"; refers to marks or comments written in the margins of a book</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ **Make non-commercial use of the files** We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ **Refrain from automated querying** Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>OCR; the mechanical or electronic conversion of images of typed, handwritten or printed text into machine-encoded text</note> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ **Maintain attribution** The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ **Keep it legal** Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About this Book

This is a digital copy of a book that has been preserved on library shelves for generations before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project intended to make the world's books available online.

The book has outlived its copyright and can now be made available to the public. A public domain book is a book that was never subject to copyright or for which the copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary from country to country. Public domain books are our gateway to the past and represent a historical, cultural, and scientific wealth that is often difficult to discover.

Marks of use, annotations, and other marginalia <note>original: "Randbemerkungen"</note> contained in the original volume are also found in this file—a reminder of the long journey the book has taken from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage Guidelines

Google is proud to work in partnership with libraries to digitize public domain material and make it accessible to a broad audience. Public domain books belong to the public, and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive. To continue providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties. This includes technical restrictions on automated queries.

We ask that you comply with the following guidelines:

+ *Use of the files for non-commercial purposes* We designed Google Book Search for end users and want you to use these files only for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *No automated queries* Do not send automated queries of any kind to the Google system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <term>OCR: the process of converting images of text into editable digital text</term>, or other areas where access to large amounts of text is useful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain material for these purposes and may be able to help you.

+ *Retention of Google branding elements* The Google "watermark" found in every file is important for informing users about this project and helping them find further material via Google Book Search. Please do not remove the watermark.

+ *Stay within the law* Regardless of your intended use, you must be aware of your responsibility to ensure that your use is legal. Do not assume that a book we believe to be in the public domain for users in the USA is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still subject to copyright varies from country to country. We cannot provide advice on whether a specific use of a specific book is legally permitted. Do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any form or anywhere in the world. A copyright infringement can have serious consequences.

->Organist and Music Director, Teacher of Singing at the Cathedral Music School, as well as of Plainchant and Liturgical Song at the Episcopal Seminary, President of the Diocesan Caecilian Society of Trier and of the Plainchant Society for the Research of Ancient Plainchant Manuscripts.<-

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page serves as the introduction to a treatise on Guido of Arezzo, establishing the significance of his various writings and identifying the Micrologus as his primary theoretical work.</meta>

<margin>Baron 81/66 <unclear>Naumburg</unclear></margin>

# Introduction.

In the history of music, the name of Guido of Arezzo has attained such fame that it must be of the greatest interest for every musician to become more closely acquainted with his writings. Alongside the "Letter of Guido to the monk Michael concerning an unknown chant" <note>original: "Epistola Guidonis Michaeli monacho de ignoto cantu directa"</note>—which offers a particular interest insofar as it sheds the most light on Guido’s person and life circumstances apart from its musical content—the most significant of Guido’s writings is his *Micrologus*. Of his remaining works, the treatises "Guido's Rhythmic Rules of Music, presented in the prologue to his Antiphonary" <note>original: "Musicae Guidonis regulae rhythmicae in antiphonarii sui prologum prolatae"</note> and "Other Rules of Guido concerning unknown chant, likewise presented in the prologue to his Antiphonary" <note>original: "Aliae Guidonis regulae de ignoto cantu identidem in antiphonarii sui prologum prolatae"</note> merely reproduce the content of the *Micrologus* in a different form. Meanwhile, regarding the essays "Guido's Corrective Treatise on many errors which occur in many places in Gregorian chant" <note>original: "Tractatus Guidonis correctorius multorum errorum, qui fiunt in cantu gregoriano in multis locis"</note> and "How music proceeds from arithmetic" <note>original: "Quomodo de arithmetica procedit musica"</note>, the latter likely does not originate from Guido at all, while the former is frequently provided with additions from a later period. For us, however, gaining knowledge specifically of the *Micrologus* holds a much higher interest, as Guido himself, at the conclusion of the aforementioned Letter, directs his students toward the study of it as a more detailed manual on the theory of music <term>music theory: the study of the practices and possibilities of music</term>; we therefore find in it a reliable explanation of the...

<summary>This introductory page argues for the historical importance of Guido of Arezzo and identifies the Micrologus as his most essential and authentic theoretical manual for musicians.</summary>
<keywords>Guido of Arezzo, Micrologus, music theory, Gregorian chant, medieval treatises, music history</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page serves as the author's preface, establishing the historical context of the 11th-century musical treatise and documenting the specific manuscripts used for this edition.</meta>

The state of the <term>musical art</term> <note>original: "Tonkunst"; refers to the science and practice of music</note> in the 11th century has been preserved by a man who was regarded by his contemporaries as the most prominent and significant expert and promoter of the musical arts. As such, he was as much honored and praised as he was, on the other hand, envied and persecuted. — For our translation, we have used the text as it was first published by Prince-Abbot Gerbert in his work: *Ecclesiastical Writers on Sacred Music Above All* <note>original Latin: "Scriptores ecclesiastici de musica sacra potissimum"</note>. This was based on manuscripts from the library of his monastery, St. Blaise, compared with two manuscripts from St. Emmeram, one from Admont, and fragments from Ottobeuren and Monte Cassino. Through the kindness of our highly esteemed and dear friend, the Ecclesiastical Councilor Mr. R. Schlecht in Eichstätt, we have been enabled to add the variant readings from the Latin Codex 9921 (Ottobeuren), Codex 14,663 of the Munich Court and State Library, and Codex 14,965a and b in the footnotes. These variants contribute significantly in many places to the clarification of the meaning, and we therefore feel deeply indebted to the aforementioned gentleman.

->The Author.<-

<summary>The author introduces an 11th-century musical treatise, citing Prince-Abbot Gerbert's 18th-century collection as the primary source while acknowledging new manuscript variants provided by R. Schlecht.</summary>
<keywords>musical art, music theory, Martin Gerbert, St. Blaise Monastery, manuscript variants, 11th century music, Raymund Schlecht</keywords>

<meta>This is the title page of the Micrologus, the foundational treatise on music theory by Guido of Arezzo, written around 1026.</meta>

# A BRIEF DISCOURSE BY GUIDO

## **Concerning the Discipline of the Art of Music.**

---

<note>original: "Micrologus Guidonis de Disciplina Artis Musicae". The title "Micrologus" translates from Greek as "brief discourse" or "short account." Guido of Arezzo was a medieval music theorist whose work revolutionized how music was written and taught.</note>

<summary>The title page of Guido of Arezzo's influential medieval treatise on music theory, outlining the fundamental rules and discipline of the musical arts.</summary>
<keywords>Guido of Arezzo, Micrologus, music theory, medieval education, musical discipline</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal text of Guido of Arezzo's "Micrologus," following the title page. It includes an introductory acrostic poem and the start of his dedicatory letter to Bishop Theodald of Arezzo.</meta>

<header>— 4 —</header>

# THE MICROLOGUS OF GUIDO
## CONCERNING THE DISCIPLINE OF THE ART OF MUSIC.

---

I decided to bring back the straying Muses to the school,
So they might be accessible to the little ones, having been barely understood by the great until now.¹)
Let love destroy the blind weapon of envy:
Indeed, the plague of anger has stolen all blessings from the lands.
I, who fashioned these poems, have written my name in the first letters.
<note>Guido uses an acrostic here: the first letter of each line in the original Latin (G-U-I-I-O) spells out his name, GUIDO. This was a common signature technique in medieval manuscripts.</note>

### The Letter
#### Of Guido, monk and Musician, to his Bishop Teudaldus, concerning the Discipline of the art of music.²)

To the most brilliant and sweet Father and most reverend Lord Theodaldus, shining with the light of divine fear and all wisdom.

---

¹) <note>original German: "Cod. lat. 9921 Mon. Ottenbur. hat die beiden ersten Zeilen..."</note> The manuscript Codex lat. 9921 from the Ottobeuren Abbey has the first two lines of the acrostic written with musical notes in letter form as follows:
->d e ♮ c d d f c d g d f c d d d a c d<-
->It pleased [us] to recall the loose Muses to the school,<-
->d e f g a d d g e f d f c d d a c d<-
->So that they may be open to the fearful, having been held scarcely until now by the great.<-

²) <note>original German: "Cod. 14965 b ließt hier..."</note> Manuscript 14965 b reads here: Here begins the Micrologus of Guido on music, or the letter of G. to Bishop T.

³) <note>original German: "Nach der Sitte der damaligen Zeit..."</note> Following the custom of the time, the author introduces himself in an acrostic, which gives subtle hints regarding the main features of his musical life and work. Ever since the time of Hucbald and even earlier, efforts had been made to develop a <term>musical notation (Tonschrift)</term>—a way of writing music—through which the substance of melodies could be definitely and securely established. In a noble competition, various attempts had been made to solve this problem...
<note>The text cuts off here, but it refers to the historical shift from learning music solely by ear to using a visual system of lines and spaces, which Guido famously perfected.</note>

<summary>This page presents the opening acrostic poem of the Micrologus, where Guido of Arezzo encodes his name and states his goal: to make music theory accessible to beginners. It also begins his formal dedication to Bishop Theodald.</summary>
<keywords>Guido of Arezzo, Micrologus, musical notation, acrostic, Bishop Theodald, medieval music theory, Muses</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory section of Guido of Arezzo's "Micrologus." It features a poem written as an acrostic and begins the formal dedication to his patron, Bishop Theodaldus of Arezzo, while providing historical context for the work's composition around the year 1022-1024.</meta>

# A Brief Treatise by Guido
## on the Rules of the Musical Art.

---

> Through spiritual struggle, it was possible to renew the unrestrained chants,
> And those things are now clear to the little ones, which until now the elders barely grasped,
> At any rate, may love destroy the arrows of envy;
> For this terrible plague has driven all happiness from the earth.
> Ordering himself in the first place, he wrote his name, who composed these lines.³)

# The Letter
## of Guido, the Monk and Musician
### to Teudaldus, ⁴) his Bishop,
## concerning the Rules of the Musical Art.

To the Father, most illustrious and most beloved, and most Reverend, shining with the brilliance of the fear of God and all wisdom—

<note>The following text appears to be a continuation of note 3, explaining the poem above:</note>
...sought to solve; finally Guido succeeded in emerging as the victor in this contest and in representing the "unrestrained"—that is, chants not previously fixed by any stable <term>musical notation|Tonschrift</term>—in writing such that they could be immediately understood clearly and correctly by boys. These were chants which, until now, could scarcely be held securely in memory even by respectable, capable singers after years of traditional practice. The author reflects here on the envy and persecution that his invention brought him and wishes that love might finally destroy the poisonous arrows of this blind passion, so that the advantages of his invention might become the common property of all. <note>original: "cf. Epist. Guid. de ign. cantu." — Referencing Guido's "Letter on Unknown Chant."</note>

⁴) <note>Theudaldus, Theodaldus, or Theobaldus, was the Bishop of Arezzo from the year 1014 to 1037. According to the historian Baronius (annals for the year 1022, no. 22), Guido is said to have written the *Micrologus* in his 34th year of life under Pope John XX (historically identified as John XIX), who sat on the papal throne after Benedict VIII from 1024 to 1038. <note>original Latin: "Joanne XX gubernante ecclesiam. micrologum suum edidit Guido" — While John XX was governing the Church, Guido published his Micrologus.</note></note>

<vocab>Guido of Arezzo, Micrologus, Teudaldus, Musical notation, Music theory, Acrostic, Arezzo, Pope John XX, Baronius</vocab>

<summary>This page contains an introductory acrostic poem where Guido hides his name in the first letters of the verses and begins his formal dedication to Bishop Theodaldus. The accompanying notes explain that Guido's new system of musical notation allowed children to learn chants that previously took professional singers years to memorize.</summary>
<keywords>Guido of Arezzo, Micrologus, Bishop Theodaldus, musical notation reform, acrostic poem, medieval music education, Pope John XIX, Arezzo history</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication of Guido’s treatise to Bishop Theodaldus of Arezzo. The text transitions from the introductory acrostic poem to the main body of the prefatory letter, where Guido discusses the Bishop's patronage and the practical goals of his musical innovations at the Cathedral of St. Donatus.</meta>

To the most worthy of priests and prelates, from Guido—of your monks would that I were the least—whatever a servant and a son <note>Guido uses standard medieval expressions of humility here, addressing his patron as both a spiritual father and a lord.</note> [can offer].

While I desire to follow, at least in some small measure, the path of a solitary life, the condescension of your kindness wished my smallness to be joined to yourself in the study of the sacred word: <note>In this context, the "sacred word" likely refers to the study of the liturgy and the musical setting of scripture.</note> not because your Excellence lacked many great spiritual men, most abundantly strengthened by the fruits of virtue and fully adorned with the studies of wisdom—men who might competently instruct the people committed to your care together with you, and who might assiduously and frequently cling to Divine contemplation—but rather so that the weakness of my smallness, in both mind and body, having been pitied, might be supported and protected by the refuge of your piety and fatherly care. Thus, if any useful thing should come to me through divine grace, God may attribute it to your merit.

For this reason, when matters of ecclesiastical utility were being discussed, your authority commanded that the practice of the musical art—in which, by God's favor, I remember I have not labored in vain—be brought into the public view. Just as you have completed the church of the most blessed Donatus, bishop and martyr (over which, by God’s authority, you preside with delegated power), in a most wonderful <term>scheme|architectural style or design</term>, so too you might make the ministers of that same church more distinguished than almost all other clerics throughout the world through a most honorable and decent privilege. And indeed, it is quite a miracle and a thing to be desired, when even the boys of your church, in the study of <term>modulation|modulandi; the art of singing and creating melodies</term>, [surpass] the perfected [singers] of other places everywhere...

---

...in the thirty-fourth year of his age), so around the year 1024 or 1025, and specifically at the command of the Bishop of Arezzo, at whose church Guido had already for a considerable time [conducted] music instruction according to this, his new method <note>The German commentary notes that the "new method" refers to Guido's invention of the musical staff, which allowed singers to learn melodies they had never heard before.</note>

<summary>Guido of Arezzo addresses Bishop Theodaldus, explaining that he has published his musical theories at the Bishop's command to improve the skill of the singers at the Cathedral of St. Donatus. He claims that his new method is so effective that even young boys can now outperform experienced singers from other regions.</summary>

<keywords>Theodaldus of Arezzo, St. Donatus, music education, modulation, medieval liturgy, musical notation, 11th century</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication of Guido’s treatise to Bishop Theobaldus of Arezzo. The text transitions from the introductory acrostic poem to the main body of the prefatory letter, where Guido discusses the Bishop's patronage and the practical goals of his musical innovations at the Cathedral of St. Donatus.</meta>

To Lord Theobaldus, the most worthy of priests and bishops, from Guido, the least of his monks, his servant and son.

While I long to carry out, at least in some small measure, the exercises of a solitary life, the favor of your Grace has deigned to join my smallness to the study of the divine Word. This was not because your Highness lacked many highly respected, spiritual men—men fully strengthened in the exercise of every virtue and most richly adorned with the treasures of all wisdom, who might appropriately instruct the people entrusted to you and constantly and untiringly devote themselves to divine contemplation—but rather so that the mental and physical weakness of my humble person, for whom you have pity in your goodness and whom you surround with your fatherly protection, might receive a firm support. Thus, if I should be of any use in anything through the grace of God, God may credit it to your merit. 

Therefore, when consultations were held concerning the best interests of the Church, your Grace issued the command that a work on the musical art <note>Guido is referring to his famous treatise, the *Micrologus*, written around 1026.</note>—in which, God willing, I believe I have not labored in vain—should be made public. You intended that, just as you have continued the Church of the holy bishop and martyr Donatus <note>Saint Donatus of Arezzo (d. 362), the patron saint of the city; the "church" refers to the Cathedral of Arezzo.</note> (over which you now preside as his representative by the grace of God) with truly wondrous majesty and stability, you might also present the ministers of this same church as objects of admiration to the clergy of the whole world, granting them a very honorable and fitting preeminence. And indeed, it already possesses enough that is admirable and worthy of imitation, in that the boys of your church surpass even the most capable men, grown gray in this art in all other places without exception, in their skill in singing; the

---

...must have taught, since, as is mentioned further below, the Church of Arezzo had already attained great fame above all other churches, partly because the singing of the boys was performed with more certainty and excellence than by singers grown gray in this art elsewhere. <note>This commentary highlights a key achievement of Guido's: his new system allowed children to learn in months what used to take adult singers years to master.</note>

<vocab>Theobaldus, Guido of Arezzo, Bishop, Monk, Music theory, Donatus, Arezzo, Chant, Church music</vocab>

<summary>Guido of Arezzo dedicates his musical treatise to Bishop Theobaldus, crediting the Bishop's patronage for the work's publication. He highlights the extraordinary success of the choirboys at Arezzo Cathedral, who have mastered singing more effectively than experienced singers elsewhere.</summary>
<keywords>Guido of Arezzo, Bishop Theobaldus, Saint Donatus, music pedagogy, choirboys, Arezzo, music theory, Micrologus</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues Guido of Arezzo's letter to Bishop Theobaldus. Guido is concluding the formal dedication and transitioning into the prologue of his musical treatise, where he explains his revolutionary teaching methods.</meta>

- 8 -

...may outshine the elders, and the height of your honor and merit will be greatly increased, when after the former fathers such great and such illustrious studies for the Church shall have come forth through you. Therefore, because I neither wished nor was able to go against your so beneficial command, I offer to your most skillful fatherhood the rules of the **musical art** <term>Musicae artis: In the Middle Ages, music was considered one of the four mathematical sciences (the Quadrivium), alongside arithmetic, geometry, and astronomy</term>, explained as clearly and briefly as I could by the philosophers; yet not in the same way to the full, nor by following in their same footsteps. I have taken care only for what may be of benefit to the needs of the church and what may assist our **young ones** <note>Guido is referring to the young choirboys and students at the cathedral school in Arezzo</note>. For this study has hitherto lain hidden and concealed for this reason: because, while it was truly difficult, it was not explained by anyone in a humble way. On what occasion I once undertook this, and with what usefulness and intention, I shall explain in very few words.

->Here ends the letter.<-

## Here begins his Prologue to Music. ¹) <note>original: "Incipit Prologus ejusdem in Musicam."</note>

Since both my natural disposition and the imitation of good men made me diligent for the common good, I began, among other studies, to teach music to the boys. At last, divine grace was with me, and some of them—practiced in the imitation of the string <note>referring to the **monochord**, a single-stringed instrument used to teach the mathematical ratios of musical pitches</note> and in the use of our notations—within the space of one month were singing **unseen and unheard chants** <note>original: "invisos et inauditos cantus"</note> at first sight without hesitation, so that it provided a great spectacle for many; ²) what however, those who...

<column-break/>

¹) In Codex 14965 b, the heading "Here begins the prologue, etc." is missing.

Guido's fame also spread... <note>This commentary by the 19th-century editor highlights why Guido's invention of the musical staff was so revolutionary: it allowed singers to read music they had never heard before.</note>

- 9 -

The height of your honor and your merit is significantly increased by the fact that, after all the striving of our ancestors, such a high and excellent brilliance of knowledge has emerged for the Church specifically through you. Therefore, since I neither wished nor was able to go against your so important request, I dedicate to your most art-loving, fatherly affection these rules of the musical art, which I have developed as clearly and briefly as possible following the philosophers <note>Guido is likely referring to ancient authorities like Boethius, whose complex mathematical theories dominated music education at the time</note>, without, however, following them completely in general or in detail; for my main focus was only to be of use to the Church as well as to be helpful to our **little ones** <note>Guido’s "little ones" were the young boys in the cathedral choir school whom he was tasked with teaching</note>. Only for this reason was this science previously hidden and unknown: because, being indeed difficult in itself, it was not presented by anyone in a simple manner. On what occasion I undertook this work, with what intention, and with what success, I wish to explain here in a few words.

->End of the letter.<-

## Preface.

Since both my natural inclination and the encouraging example of good men made me a particular lover of activity for the common good, I began, alongside other favorite occupations, to also teach boys music. In this, divine grace came to my aid, so that some among them—through the use of the **monochord** <term>Monochord: A musical instrument with a single string and a sliding bridge, used in the Middle Ages to demonstrate the mathematical ratios between different musical pitches</term> and through diligent practice in the use of our notation—were able, before the end of a single month, to sing at first sight, with such certainty, songs they had never seen or heard before, that they became an object of the highest admiration to most people. ²) Moreover, I do not understand with what...

---

...soon it was of such a nature that even Pope John XX <note>Historically, this is believed to be Pope John XIX (reigned 1024–1032), as the numbering of Popes named John was inconsistent in medieval records</note> sent three messengers to summon him and have his method explained, and did not rest until he himself had learned a verse which he had never heard sung before, as Guido himself reports in his letter addressed to the monk Michael **On the Unknown Song** <note>original title: *de ignoto cantu*</note>.

<note>Vocabulary: Music, Monochord, Guido of Arezzo, Pope John XX, Preface, On the Unknown Song, Music Theory, Chant</note>

...[if a person] cannot do this, I do not know with what face he dares to call himself a musician or a singer. Therefore, I have felt the greatest sorrow for our singers who, even if they persist in the study of singing for a hundred years, are never able to perform even the shortest **antiphon** <term>Antiphon: A short chant or sentence sung before or after a psalm; in Guido's time, these had to be memorized by ear because notation was not yet precise enough to read at sight</term> by themselves. They are "always learning," as the Apostle says, "and never arriving at the perfect knowledge of this art" <note>original: "semper discentes... et numquam ad perfectam hujus artis scientiam pervenientes." Therefore, desiring to devote our useful study to the common good, I have summarized as briefly as possible certain musical principles which I collected over various times with God's help, and which I believed would benefit singers. For I have judged that those aspects of music which are less useful for performance, or those which are too difficult to understand, are not even worth mentioning. I care little if some hearts turn green with **envy** <note>original: "livescat invidia"</note>, so long as the **training** <note>original: "disciplina"</note> of others progresses.

->The prologue ends.<-

¹) Therefore, it has also always deeply saddened me to see how our singers, even if they were to persist in the study of singing for a hundred years, would nonetheless never be able to produce even the smallest **antiphon** <term>Antiphon: A short liturgical chant; in Guido’s time, these were mostly learned by rote memory rather than by reading notation.</term> on their own—always learning, as the Apostle says, without ever arriving at a perfect mastery of this art <note>A reference to the biblical verse 2 Timothy 3:7: "Always learning and never able to come to the knowledge of the truth."</note>. Specifically, I have not considered those things which are of lesser significance for singing, or which are not easily understood, to be worth further mention. In this, I am not concerned about what some squint-eyed souls, eaten away by envy, might judge, as long as my "Rules of the Musical Art" bring some advantage to others.

->End of the Preface.<-

...waste such a long time in singing that they could have perfectly learned all worldly and spiritual science." — <term>Guido</term> <note>Guido of Arezzo (c. 991–1033), the monk credited with inventing the staff notation that remains the basis of modern music writing.</note> introduces his **rhythmic rules** <note>original: "regulae rhythmicae"</note> as follows:

> Between musicians and singers, there is a great distance;
> The latter perform, the former know what Music composes;
> For he who does what he does not understand is defined as a beast.
> Furthermore, if they praise the heights of a thundering voice,
> Then a braying ass will surpass the nightingale.
<note>original Latin:
"Musicorum et cantorum magna est distantia,
Isti dicunt, illi sciunt, quae componit Musica;
Nam qui facit, quod non sapit, diffinitur bestia.
Caeterum tonantis vocis si laudent acumina,
Superabit philomelam vel vocalis asina."</note>

That is: "Between musician and singer there is a great distance. These only perform, but those understand what music achieves. But he who does what he does not understand, one calls a beast. If, moreover, one praises the high roar of a thundering voice as beautiful, then a braying donkey might well carry off the prize over the nightingale." — The phrasing is coarse, but in terms of substance, these pronouncements still have a certain justification in our own time.

## The Chapters Begin. ¹)

**Chapter I.** What should one do, who is preparing himself for the discipline of Music?
**Chapter II.** Which or of what kind are the notes, and how many are there?
**Chapter III.** Concerning their arrangement on the **monochord**. <term>Monochord: A scientific instrument with a single string and a movable bridge, used in the Middle Ages to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</term>
**Chapter IV.** In which six ways are the **tones** <note>original: "voces" — in this context, it refers to the individual steps or syllables of the musical scale.</note> joined to one another?
**Chapter V.** Concerning the **octave**, and why there are only seven notes? <term>Octave: Referred to in the text as the "diapason," the interval between one musical pitch and another with double its frequency.</term>
**Chapter VI.** Likewise concerning the divisions and their interpretation.
**Chapter VII.** Concerning the affinity of tones through the four **modes**. <term>Modes: The traditional melodic scales of medieval church music.</term>
**Chapter VIII.** Concerning other affinities and the **soft B** and **hard B**. <note>original: "♭ et ♮" — these represent B-flat (soft) and B-natural (hard), the only "accidental" notes used in this era.</note>
**Chapter IX.** Likewise concerning the similarity of tones, of which only the octave is perfect.
**Chapter X.** Likewise concerning the modes and the recognition and correction of false melody.
**Chapter XI.** Which tone holds leadership in a chant, and why?
**Chapter XII.** Concerning the division of the four modes into eight.
**Chapter XIII.** Concerning the recognition of the eight modes, their high pitch, and their low pitch.
**Chapter XIV.** Likewise concerning the **tropes** and the power of Music. <term>Tropes: Musical or textual additions to standard liturgical chants; also another term for musical modes.</term>
**Chapter XV.** Concerning suitable or well-composed melody.
**Chapter XVI.** Concerning the manifold variety of sounds and **neumes**. <term>Neumes: The early symbols used in musical notation to indicate the shape and direction of a melody before the modern staff was fully developed.</term>
**Chapter XVII.** That everything which is spoken is reduced to song.

**Chapter I.** What must he do, who wishes to learn music?
**Chapter II.** Which or what kind of notes are there, and how many?
**Chapter III.** On the arrangement of these on the **monochord**. <term>Monochord: A single-stringed instrument used as a scientific tool to measure and demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</term>
**Chapter IV.** In what sixfold way are the **tones** joined among themselves? <note>original: "Töne" — here referring to the six musical intervals: the semitone, whole tone, minor third, major third, perfect fourth, and perfect fifth.</note>
**Chapter V.** On the **octave**, and why there are only seven notes? <term>Octave: The interval between one musical pitch and another with double its frequency; in this system, the scale repeats after seven distinct letter names.</term>
**Chapter VI.** On the ratios of division and their explanation.
**Chapter VII.** On the relationship of tones according to the four **modes**. <term>Modes: The sets of scales (Dorian, Phrygian, Lydian, and Mixolydian) that formed the basis of medieval chant.</term>
**Chapter VIII.** On other relational connections (of the tones) and **♭** and **♮**. <note>original: "♭ und ♮" — these symbols represent 'soft' B (B-flat) and 'hard' B (B-natural), the first accidental notes in Western music history.</note>
**Chapter IX.** On the similarity of tones, which is a perfect similarity only in the octave.
**Chapter X.** On the modes and the recognition and correction of a false melody.
**Chapter XI.** Which tone maintains precedence in song and why?
**Chapter XII.** On the division of the four modes into eight. <note>This refers to the transition from the four "authentic" modes to the system of eight "church modes" which include "plagal" versions.</note>
**Chapter XIII.** On the characteristics of the eight tones, and their range according to height and depth.
**Chapter XIV.** On the **tropes** and the influence of music. <term>Tropes: Here used to mean the emotional character or "flavor" of the different musical modes and their power to affect the listener's soul.</term>
**Chapter XV.** On the well-formed or to-be-formed **proportion** of a melody. <note>original: "Ebenmaaß" — referring to the balance and symmetry required in a beautiful musical composition.</note>
**Chapter XVI.** On the manifold variety of tones and groups of tones.
**Chapter XVII.** That everything which is spoken can be brought into song.

## Chapter I.

### **What he should do, who prepares himself for the discipline of the art of music. ¹)**
<note>1) The Latin Manuscript 9921 from Ottobeuren includes a title before Chapter I: "Here begins the Micrologus of Guido." The title for Chapter I itself in that version reads: "What he should do, who prepares himself for the discipline of music."</note>

Therefore, he who seeks our discipline should learn a number of chants written in our notation, exercise his hand in the use of the **monochord**, and frequently meditate upon these rules until—once the power and nature of the notes are understood—he can sweetly sing unknown songs just as easily as those he already knows. But because we can better observe the notes (which are the primary foundations of this art) on the monochord, let us first see how art, imitating nature, has distinguished them there. <note>original: "discrevit" — MS 9921 reads "discreverit," a minor grammatical variation.</note>

# Chapter I.

## **What he must do, who wishes to learn the rules of the musical art.**

Whoever desires our musical instruction should first learn a significant number of chants written in our notation, practice his hand in the use of the **monochord**, and frequently review the rules provided here until he has perfectly grasped the essence and nature of the tones and is able to sing unknown chants—just like those already known—with a sweet and pleasant delivery. <term>Monochord: A scientific and musical instrument consisting of a single string stretched over a soundbox with a movable bridge, used to measure and demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</term> However, since we can better observe the tones—as the primary foundation of this art—on the monochord, let us first see how art, in imitation of nature, has demarcated them there.

---

# Chapter II.

## **Which or what kind of notes are there, and how many?**

The notes on the monochord are as follows: In the first position, the Greek G (**Gamma**) is placed, which has been added by the moderns. <margin>4) The Γ <note>original: "Gamma"</note> was therefore not added to the system by Guido himself or in his honor—as has often been claimed—but was already in use before his time. Furthermore, Guido could not do without the tone Γ, as his two methods for dividing the monochord described in Chapter III are impossible to execute without the tone Γ. Specifically, starting from A, the tone C in particular cannot be found through simple direct division. **Very**</margin> It is followed by the seven low—

<margin>4) The Γ <note>original: "Gamma"</note> was therefore not added to the system by Guido himself or in his honor, but likely because the "moderns" added the pitch Gamma only to allow a simple division of the **monochord** to be performed from this note. Guido at least (and likely the "moderns" before him) still considers the pitch Gamma as **not** belonging to the system. In his *Letter on Unknown Song* <note>original: "Epistola de ignoto cantu"</note>, he explicitly calls **A** the "first letter" (the first letter or pitch), **B** the "second," **C** the "third," and so on, and **a** the "second first," and so forth. The pitch Gamma has found practical use (as far as we can recall at the moment) in **plainchant** <note>original: "Choralgesange"</note> only in the Responsory for Palm Sunday: "The high priests gathered" <note>original: "Collegerunt principes"</note>.</margin>

# Chapter III.
## Concerning their arrangement on the monochord.

---

Therefore, having first fixed **Γ** <note>Gamma: the Greek letter 'G', used by Guido d'Arezzo to represent the lowest note of the scale, hence the term "Gamut" (Gamma-Ut)</note>, divide the space of the string beneath it into nine parts as far as the end, and at the boundary of the first ninth part, place the letter **A**, from which all the ancient writers made their beginning. Likewise, having measured a ninth part from **A** to the end, join the letter **B** in the same manner. After this, returning to **Γ**, measure as far as the end by four <note>This refers to the mathematical ratio of a perfect fourth (4:3)</note>, and at the boundary of the first part you will find **C**. By the same division of four, just as **C** was found from **Γ**, you will find **D** from **A** in a similar way and in order; you will find **E** from **B**; **F** from **C**; **G** from **D**; the high **a** ⁴) from **E**; and the round **b** ⁵) <note>b-flat</note> from **F**. As for the notes that follow, the midpoints <note>i.e., halving the string to produce an octave</note> of all these same and similar notes are easily gathered in order.

---

¹) Latin Manuscript 9921: "and already."
²) Latin Manuscript 9921: "made clear."
³) The contents of Chapter II are found again in the "rhythmic rules" <note>original: *regulae rhythmicae*</note> in the following verses:
> Therefore, having cast aside those notes used by the common folk,
> Who like the blind go nowhere without a guide,
> Learn these seven notes with their seven characters.

> For the other sets of seven that follow after
> Are not different, but are repeated by the same rule.
> Because a week <term>Hebdomada: A group of seven; here referring to the seven-note octave cycle.</term> of voices occurs just like the days.
> If you wish to see these with your eyes just as they are heard,
> While the mouth is for a moment silent,
> Let a proof of manual labor be made, defined by certain numbers.

...having been given, it shall now be explained here briefly and in a manner completely intelligible even to boys ³).

## Chapter III.
### On the arrangement of the same on the Monochord.

After first establishing the letter **Γ** <note>Gamma: the Greek letter 'G', used to represent the lowest note of the medieval musical system</note>, divide the entire space lying beneath the string from that point to the end into nine parts, and at the boundary point of the first ninth, place the letter **A**, with which all the ancient theorists began. After having separated a ninth part again in the same way from **A** to the end point, add the letter **B** in the same manner. Then return to **Γ** and divide the space to the end into four parts; at the boundary point of the first part, you will find **C**. Through the same division into four, just as **C** was found from **Γ**, so also find in sequence **D** from **A**, **E** from **B**, and **F** from **C**. From **D** one finds **G**, from **E** the high **a** ⁴), and from **F** the round **b** ⁵) <note>b-flat</note>. The letters that follow are easily obtained in order by halving the space of the preceding similar ones; for example: from **B** to the—

---

³) That is: "Setting aside those notes used by the masses, who are accustomed to proceeding like a blind man without a guide, learn the following seven notes with their signs: **Γ A B C D E F G a b c d e f g a**. For the other notes that follow the first seven are no different, but are grouped under one rule. Among the tones, just as with the days, there is only a number of seven (a <term>Hebdomas</term> <note>original: *Hebdomas* (Greek); a group of seven, like the seven days of the week</note>). If you wish to see the tones with your eyes just as they are heard with your ears, then while the mouth keeps silence, the following arrangement, constructed according to certain numerical ratios, must be made by hand." — (the division of the monochord follows).
⁴) Latin Manuscript 9921: "and with **E** you will find **a**."
⁵) Latin Manuscript 9921: "**b** rotundum" <note>round b; the origin of the flat sign (♭), used to indicate the "soft" B or B-flat</note>.

...for example, from **B** to the end, place another **♮** <note>The 'square b' or 'hard B', which we now call B-natural</note> in the middle space. And similarly ^1) **C** will mark another **c**, and **D** another **d**, and **E** another **e**, and **F** another **f**, and **G** ~~another~~ **g** ^2), and **a** another <gloss>a</gloss><gloss>a'</gloss> and **b** another <gloss>♭</gloss><gloss>b</gloss> <note>The 'round b' or b-flat</note>, and **♮** another <gloss>♮</gloss><gloss>♮</gloss>, and **c** another <gloss>c</gloss><gloss>c,</gloss> and **d** another <gloss>d</gloss><gloss>d.</gloss> In the same way, you could proceed infinitely upwards or downwards, unless the rules of the art restrained you by their authority.

Of the many and diverse divisions of the monochord, I have provided this one, so that while many focus on one without—

---

^1) Latin Manuscript 9921: And similarly **c**.
^2) Latin Manuscript 9921 reads: "another **g** and the rest in the same way" and then continues: "You could proceed infinitely in this way," etc.
^3) The division of the <term>monochord</term> <note>A single-stringed instrument used to teach musical intervals through mathematical ratios</note> carried out here is also specified in the *Letter of Guido to the Monk Michael*. It is based on division by nine, by four, and by two. Its purpose was to make the series of tones established in Chapter II practically audible to the student, thereby providing him with a means by which he could train his musical ear and dispense with the assistance of a teacher. It is based on the general principle of changing the tone of a stretched string by shortening it, whether by simple pressure of the finger (as with our modern stringed instruments) or by placing a bridge beneath it (as with the frets of our guitars, zithers, mandolins, lutes, etc.). The student is meant to learn to determine, through the mentioned method of division, at which points the stretched string should be stopped with the finger or shortened with bridges to produce the tones listed above. If one therefore tunes a string **S T** stretched over a hollow resonance box, which runs over two bridges (see Plate I. B.) at **y** and **x**, and designates the tone of the free-vibrating part **y x** of the string **S T** as **Γ** <note>Gamma, the lowest note</note>, then one finds the point at which the tone **A** is stopped by dividing the entire string **y x** (or **Γx**, or rather the space lying beneath it) very precisely (*studiosissime*, as Guido says in his *Letter*) into nine equal parts. Where the first division point falls, there is the point where the tone **A** is produced; that is, if I cause only the part **A x** of the string **S T** to vibrate by pressing down the string or by placing a bridge, then this part of the string gives the tone **A**. In the same way, the division point **B** for the tone **B** is found by the division into nine of the string **A x**. The division point **C** for the—

- 21 -

Finally, place the other **♮** <note>The "square b," known as B-natural</note> in the middle of the space. In a similar way, **C** determines the other **c**, **D** the other **d**, and **E** the other **e**, **F** the other **f**, **G** the other **g**, and **a** the other **aa**, **b** the other **bb**, **♮** the other **♮♮**, **c** the other **cc**, and **d** the other **dd**. <note>The notation using double letters (like aa or cc) indicates the higher octave.</note> Thus, one could proceed infinitely upwards and downwards, if the laws of the art <note>of music</note> did not forbid it. ^3)

Of the multiple and diverse ways of dividing the <term>monochord</term> <note>A single-stringed instrument used by medieval musicians to calculate the exact mathematical pitches of the scale</note>, I have set down only one, so that one might—

***

The note **C** is found if one divides the entire string **Γx** into four equal parts. Likewise, one finds the division point **D** if one divides the string section **Ax**, **E** if one divides **Bx**, **F** if one divides **Cx**, **G** if one divides **Dx**, **a** if one divides **Ex**, and **b-flat** <note>original: b rotundum, the "round b"</note> if one divides **Fx** into four equal parts. The remaining division points **♮**, **c**, **d**, **e**, **f**, **g**, **aa**, **bb**, **♮♮**, **cc**, and **dd** are obtained by halving the respective string sections **Bx**, **Cx**, **Dx**, **Ex**, **Fx**, **Gx**, **ax**, **bx**, **♮x**, **cx**, and **dx**. If one calculates the length of the string sections for each tone based on the divisions performed, and designates the total length **Γx** as **1**, then the resulting lengths are: **Ax** = 8/9, **Bx** = 64/81, **Cx** = 3/4, **Dx** = 2/3, **Ex** = 16/27, **Fx** = 9/16, **Gx** = 1/2, **ax** = 4/9, **bx** = 27/64, **♮x** = 32/81, **cx** = 3/8, **dx** = 1/3, **ex** = 8/27, **fx** = 9/32, **gx** = 1/4, **aa x** = 2/9, **bb x** = 27/128, **♮♮ x** = 16/81, **cc x** = 3/16, **dd x** = 1/6 of the string length **yx** or **Γx**.

From these measurements of length, the following ratios result for the intervals included in the series of tones: 
**Minor Second** <note>original: Semitonium</note> **B—C**, **E—F** = 243 : 256; 
**Major Second** <note>original: Tonus</note> **Γ—A**, **A—B** etc. = 8 : 9; 
**Minor Third** <note>original: Semitiditonus</note> **A—C**, **D—F** etc. = 27 : 32; 
**Major Third** <note>original: Ditonus</note> **C—E**, **F—a** = 64 : 81; 
**Perfect Fourth** <note>original: Diatesseron</note> **Γ—C**, **C—F** etc. = 3 : 4; 
**Perfect Fifth** <note>original: Diapente</note> **Γ—D**, **C—G** etc. = 2 : 3; 
**Minor Sixth** <note>original: Diapente cum semitonio</note> **E—C** = 81 : 128; 
**Major Sixth** <note>original: Diapente cum tono</note> **Γ—E**, **C—a** etc. = 16 : 27; 
**Minor Seventh** <note>original: Diapente cum semiditono</note> **Γ—F** = 9 : 16; 
**Major Seventh** <note>original: Diapente cum ditono</note> **C—♮** = 128 : 243; 
**Octave** <note>original: Diapason</note> **Γ—G** = 1 : 2; 
furthermore, the **Augmented Fourth** <note>original: Tritonus</note> **F—♮** = 512 : 729; 
the **Diminished Fifth** <note>as the inversion of the augmented fourth</note> = 729 : 1024.

...be seized by doubt. Especially since it is of such great utility that it is both easily understood and, once understood, is hardly forgotten. ¹) Another method of division follows which, although it is less easily memorized, allows the <term>monochord</term> <note>A scientific and musical instrument consisting of a single string stretched over a soundbox, used to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note> ²) to be constructed with even greater speed, in this way:

When first you make nine steps <note>original: passus; these are equal mathematical units or "intervals" of the string length</note> ³) from **Γ** <note>Gamma, the lowest note of the medieval scale</note> to the end of the string—that is, you create nine small sections—the first step will end in **A**; the second is skipped; the third ends in **D**; the fourth is skipped; the fifth ends in **a**; the sixth in **d**; the seventh in **aa**; and the remaining steps are skipped.

Likewise, when you divide from **A** all the way ⁴) to the end into nine steps, the first step will end in **B**; the second is skipped; the third in **E**; the fourth is skipped; the fifth in **♮** <note>The "square-b," which we know as B-natural</note>; the sixth in **e**; the seventh in **♮♮**; and the rest are skipped.

In the same way, when you divide from **Γ** to the end into four parts, the first step will end in **C**, the second in **G**, the third in **g**, and the fourth marks the end of the string. From **C** to the end, similarly in four steps, the first will end in **E** ⁷), the second in **c**, the third in **cc**, and the fourth marks the end. From **F**, however, in four steps, the first will end in **round b** <note>original: b rotundum; the note B-flat</note>, the second in **f**, and the third in **c** ⁸). From **round b** in four...

...direct one’s whole attention to just one [method] instead of many, and grasp it without any confusing doubts; especially since it is of the greatest importance that the **method of division** be clearly understood and, once understood, never forgotten. Another method of division shall nevertheless follow here, which, while not as easily impressed upon the memory, allows the division of the **monochord** <note>A one-stringed instrument used by medieval scholars to measure the mathematical proportions of musical intervals.</note> to be carried out much more rapidly, in the following manner:

If one first makes nine equal parts from **Γ** <note>Gamma, the lowest note of the scale.</note> to the end point, the first part ends in **A**, the second remains empty, the third ends in **D**, the fourth is empty, the fifth ends in **a**, the sixth in **d**, the seventh in <gloss>a</gloss>a <note>The double letter indicates a higher octave.</note>, and the rest remain unoccupied. Likewise, if one makes nine equal parts from **A** to the end, the first part ends in **B**, the second remains empty, the third ends in **E**, the fourth is empty, the fifth ends in **♮** <note>The "square-b" or B-natural.</note>, the sixth in **e**, the seventh in <gloss>♮</gloss>♮, and the rest remain empty.

In the same way, if one divides the space from **Γ** to the end into four equal parts, the first part ends in **C**, the second in **G**, the third in **g**, and the fourth reaches the end. But if one makes four parts from **C** to the end, the first ends in **F**, <note>original footnote 7: The division into four from **C** clearly gives the fourth, **F**, in the ratio of 4:3, and not the third, **E**, as the text in Gerbert suggests.</note> the second in **c**, the third in <gloss>c</gloss>c, and the fourth reaches the end. The text in Gerbert’s edition, "third in **c**," is incorrect because in the division into four parts from **F**, the first quarter clearly gives the fourth, **round b**, in a 4:3 ratio; the second quarter (the half-way point) gives the octave of **F**, which is **f**; and the third quarter (the half-way point from the small **f**) must give the octave of small **f**, namely **ff**.</note>. 1) This statement in Gerbert’s text <note>Martin Gerbert (1720–1793), a monk who published a famous collection of medieval music treatises.</note> is also incorrect, since in a fourfold division starting from **♭** *rotundum* <note>The "round B" or B-flat.</note>, at the second quarter (which is to say, at the halfway point), one does not obtain **♮** *quadratum* <note>The "square B" or B-natural.</note>, but only **♭♭**. The result of this second method of monochord division is, regarding the <term>interval ratios</term> <note>The mathematical relationship between the pitch of two notes.</note>, the same as the first method. From **F** (= <sup>9</sup>/<sub>16</sub> of the whole string), the fourfold division at the first division point (= <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> of <sup>9</sup>/<sub>16</sub> = <sup>27</sup>/<sub>64</sub> of the whole string) yields the **fourth**, **b** <note>B-flat.</note>; at the 2nd division point (= <sup>2</sup>/<sub>4</sub> of <sup>9</sup>/<sub>16</sub> = <sup>18</sup>/<sub>64</sub> or <sup>9</sup>/<sub>32</sub>) the **octave**, **f**. From **b** (= <sup>27</sup>/<sub>64</sub> of the whole string), the fourfold division at the 2nd division point (= <sup>2</sup>/<sub>4</sub> of <sup>27</sup>/<sub>64</sub> = <sup>54</sup>/<sub>256</sub> or <sup>27</sup>/<sub>128</sub>) yields the **octave**, **bb**. From **aa** (= <sup>2</sup>/<sub>9</sub> of the whole string), the fourfold division at the first division point (= <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> of <sup>2</sup>/<sub>9</sub> = <sup>6</sup>/<sub>36</sub> or <sup>1</sup>/<sub>6</sub>) yields the **fourth**, **dd**. 

If one arranges the found ratios in order, they are:
**Γ** = 1; **A** = <sup>8</sup>/<sub>9</sub>; **B** = <sup>64</sup>/<sub>81</sub>; **C** = <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub>; **D** = <sup>2</sup>/<sub>3</sub>; **E** = <sup>16</sup>/<sub>27</sub>; **F** = <sup>9</sup>/<sub>16</sub>; **G** = <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub>; **a** = <sup>4</sup>/<sub>9</sub>; **b** = <sup>27</sup>/<sub>64</sub>; **♮** = <sup>32</sup>/<sub>81</sub>; **c** = <sup>3</sup>/<sub>8</sub>; **d** = <sup>1</sup>/<sub>3</sub>; **e** = <sup>8</sup>/<sub>27</sub>; **f** = <sup>9</sup>/<sub>32</sub>; **g** = <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub>; **aa** = <sup>2</sup>/<sub>9</sub>; **bb** = <sup>27</sup>/<sub>128</sub>; **♮♮** = <sup>16</sup>/<sub>81</sub>; **cc** = <sup>3</sup>/<sub>16</sub>; **dd** = <sup>1</sup>/<sub>6</sub>, 

exactly as in the first method of division (see <note>original: "cf. Anmerkung 3 Seite 20"</note> Note 3, page 20).

## Chapter IV.

### In which six ways notes are joined to one another ¹).

---

->Once the notes have been arranged, a larger space is seen between some notes, such as between **Γ** <note>Gamma, the lowest note of the medieval scale.</note> and **A**, or between **A** and **B**; whereas a smaller space is seen between others, such as between **B** and **C**, and so on.²) This larger space is called a **tone** <term>A whole step, the basic unit of distance in the musical scale.</term>; the smaller is called a **semitone** <term>A half step.</term>, which means "half," that is, not a full tone. Likewise, between a note and the third note from it, there is sometimes a **ditone** <term>A major third, consisting of two whole tones.</term> (that is, two tones, such as from **C** to **E**) and sometimes a **semiditone** <term>A minor third, consisting of a tone and a semitone.</term>, which contains only a tone and a semitone (such as from **D** to **F**, and so on). A **diatessaron** <term>A perfect fourth.</term> occurs when there are two tones and one semitone between two notes in any arrangement, such as from **A** to **D** and from **B** to **E**, and so on. A **diapente** <term>A perfect fifth.</term> is larger by one tone, occurring when there are three tones and one semitone between any two notes, such as from **A** to **E** and from **C** to **G**, and so on. Thus, you have six **concordances** ³) of notes: the tone, semitone, ditone, semiditone, diatessaron, and diapente.<-

[To these concordances, two other types of modes are added by some singers: namely, the **diapente with a semitone** <note>A minor sixth.</note>, as from **E** to **c**; and the **diapente with a tone** <note>A major sixth.</note>, as from **C** to **a**. The **diapason** <term>An octave.</term> is also added. Because these are rarely found <note>Guido refers here to melodic leaps; while octaves and sixths exist as distances, they were less common as direct melodic intervals in the chant of his time.</note>, they are not counted by us among the six. However, the diligent seeker will find the origin and nature of the diapason in the following chapters.⁴)]

---

## Chapter IV.
### In which six ways notes are joined to one another.

Once the notes have been arranged (on the monochord) <term>A one-stringed instrument used to teach musical intervals and mathematical proportions.</term>, one can see that the space from one note to another is sometimes larger, for example from **Γ** <note>Gamma, the Greek letter used to represent the lowest note of the medieval scale.</note> to **A** and from **A** to **B**, but other times smaller, such as between **B** and **C**, and so on. *) The larger interval is called a **tone** (*tonus*); the smaller is called a **semitone** (*semitonium*). *Semis* (half), in this context, refers to an incomplete tone. In a similar way, the distance from one note to the third note counted from it is sometimes a **ditone** (*ditonus*) <term>A major third, consisting of two whole tones.</term> (that is, two tones, such as from **C** to **E**), and sometimes a **semiditone** (*semiditonus*) <term>A minor third.</term>, which comprises only one tone and one semitone, such as from **D** to **F**, and so on. A **fourth** (*quart*) <term>Historically called a diatessaron.</term> occurs when two tones and one semitone lie between two notes in any arrangement, such as from **A** to **D** and from **B** to **E**, etc. A **fifth** (*quint*) <term>Historically called a diapente.</term> is larger by one tone—specifically, when three tones and one semitone lie between any two notes, such as from **A** to **E** and from **C** to **G**, etc. Therefore, there are six combinations of notes ³): namely, the tone, the semitone, the major third, the minor third, the fourth, and the fifth.

[To these combinations, two others are added by some singers: namely, the fifth with a semitone <term>A minor sixth.</term>, such as from **E** to **c**, and furthermore the fifth with a whole tone <term>A major sixth.</term>, such as from **C** to **a**. The **octave** is also sometimes included. However, because these combinations are rarely found <note>In the context of early medieval chant, wider leaps like sixths and octaves were less common in melodic construction than smaller intervals.</note>, we do not count them among the six. The origin and specific nature of the octave will be recognized in what follows by anyone who follows the matter with interest ⁴).]

---

³) ... be joined. <note>original: "Non aliter, quam his sex modis voces junctae concordant vel moventur"; translates to: "Notes joined together harmonize or move in no other way than these six modes."</note>

⁴) According to the observation of Gerbert <note>Martin Gerbert (1720–1793), the musicologist who edited the standard collection of medieval music treatises used here.</note>, the words enclosed in brackets are found only in the manuscripts from Admont and Ottobeuren <note>Two influential Benedictine monasteries with significant musical libraries.</note>.

# Chapter V.
## Concerning the Octave and why there are only seven notes.⁴)

The **octave** is that connection in which a **fourth** and a **fifth** are joined together; for if there is a fourth from **A** to **D**, and from that same **D** up to the high **a** there is a fifth, then from **A** to the second **a** is an octave. Its particular nature is that it has the same letter on each side, such as from **B** to **♮** <note>The symbol ♮ here denotes B-natural, often called "square B" or "hard B" in medieval music, as opposed to B-flat.</note>, from **C** to **c**, from **D** to **d**, and so on. Just as each of the two tones with—

---

—the same high tone, as for example from the first to the first (**A** to **a**) or from the second to the second (**B** to **♮**), it is called a <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "diapason"; from the Greek phrase *dia pason khordon*, meaning "through all the strings."</note>, which literally means "from all."

³) "Let them be felt and known" <note>original Latin: "Sentiantur et cognoscantur"</note>. The first expression refers to the inner mental representation of the interval—the grasping of it in thought before it is even sung. The latter expression refers to the reliable recognition of the interval once it has been sung. The singer must master both if they wish to perform a piece with composure and ease. They must be able to mentally imagine every interval represented by the notes—whether more or less consciously—before singing it, and must immediately recognize it as correct once it has been sung in order to proceed calmly.

⁴) Latin Manuscript 9921 <note>original: "Cod. lat. 9921"</note>: "and why there are only seven notes." In the margin, this manuscript reads: "Of the first voice" <note>original Latin: "Vocis primae"</note>.

<summary>This page defines the octave as the combination of a fourth and a fifth, emphasizing its mathematical and practical role in the "structure of tones." It stresses the need for singers to both mentally hear and aurally recognize intervals to achieve musical mastery.</summary>

# Chapter V.
### Concerning the Octave and why there are only seven notes.⁴)

The **octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note> moreover is that in which a **fourth** <note>original: "diatesseron"</note> and a **fifth** <note>original: "diapente"</note> are joined; for since there is a fourth from **A** to **D**, and from that same **D** up to the high **a** there is a fifth, from **A** to the other **a** there is an octave: the power of which is to have the same letter on both sides, such as from **B** to **♮** <note>The symbol ♮ here denotes B-natural, often called "square B" or "hard B" in medieval music, as opposed to the rounded "soft" B-flat.</note>, from **C** to **c**, from **d** to **d** and so forth. For in no musical composition is one pitch joined to another in any other regular way ¹), whether ascending or descending. To these, a seventh connection—the **octave**—is added. ## Chapter V.
### On the Octave and Why There Are Only Seven Notes. ⁴)

The **octave**, however, is that connection in which a **fourth** and a **fifth** are joined together; for if there is a fourth from A to D, and from that same D to the high 'a' there is a fifth, then from A to the other 'a' is an octave, whose peculiar nature is that it has the same letter on each side, such as from B to ♮ <note>The "square B," indicating B-natural in early music notation.</note>, from C to c, from D to d, and so on. For just as each of the two tones with

---

the same high tone, such as from the first to the first (A to a) or from the second to the second (B to ♮), is called the <term>diapason</term> <note>The Greek term for an octave, used extensively in medieval music theory.</note> (octave), which literally means "through all."

³) <note>original: "Sentiantur et cognoscantur"</note>. The first expression refers to the inner mental conception of the interval—to the grasping of it in thought before it is even sung—while the latter expression refers to the reliable recognition of the interval once it has been sung. The singer must possess both if he wishes to perform a chant with composure and ease. He must be able to mentally imagine every interval represented by the notes before he sings it (whether more or less consciously) and must immediately recognize it as correct once he has sung it, so that he can proceed with confidence.

⁴) Codex Latin 9921: "and why there are only seven notes." <note>original: "et cur septem tantum sint notas"</note> In the margin, this Codex reads: "Of the first voice" <note>original: "Vocis primae"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the octave, specifically focusing on the concept of pitch identity—why an 'A' and a higher 'a' are considered the same note despite their different pitch levels.</meta>

all are of the same quality, and both are held and believed to have the most perfect similarity. For just as we repeat the same seven days once they are finished, so that we always call the first and eighth the same day, so we always symbolize the first and eighth notes with the same character <note>original: "eodem charactere"; referring to the letter names like A, B, C.</note> ¹) and name, because we feel them sounding together in a natural harmony, such as D and d. For each is lowered by a tone, a semitone, and two tones, and likewise each is raised by a tone, a semitone, and two tones ²). Therefore, when singing, if two, three, or more singers—as many as possible—begin and sing any <term>symphony</term> <note>From the Greek "symphonia," meaning a concordant interval or sounding together, rather than a modern orchestral work.</note> using this method of differing pitches, ³) you will marvel that you have the same voices in different places, yet they are not different at all ⁴), the same—

---

¹) *Cod. 9921* reads: "thus we always represent the eighth notes as the same."
²) What **Guido** <note>Guido of Arezzo (c. 991–1033), the monk and music theorist credited with inventing modern staff notation.</note> brings forward here as proof for the unison of notes standing in an octave is only a highly superficial point, having no relation to the inner essence of the matter. Both notes have the same position in the series of notes insofar as they occupy the same relationships to the notes surrounding them. *D* and *d* both have below them first a tone that stands a major (whole) step away, *C* or *c*; then follows a minor (half) step *B* or ♮ <note>The "natural" sign, representing the note B-natural in the medieval system.</note>; after this another major step *A* or *a* and then another major step *Γ* <note>Gamma, the lowest note of the medieval system.</note> or *G*. The same pitch ratios are encountered when ascending from *D* or *d*, as the following comparison shows:

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of octave equivalence—the principle that notes an octave apart are functionally identical. It completes the analogy between the seven-day week and the seven-note musical scale.</meta>

is designated by the same letter, both are also held and regarded as being of the same quality in every respect and possessing the most perfect similarity. For just as we begin again from the start after the passing of seven days, so that we always name the first and eighth days as the same, we likewise always designate and name the first and eighth tones with the same letter <gloss>1)</gloss>, because we feel that they harmonize into one sound in a natural harmony, as for example D and d. For both tones descend through a whole tone, a semitone, and two whole tones, and likewise ascend through a whole tone, a semitone, and two whole tones <gloss>2)</gloss>. From this it also follows that in practical singing, when two, three, or more singers—as many as possible—begin and sing <gloss>3)</gloss> any melody with the notes differing in this way, one hears to one’s astonishment the same notes <gloss>4)</gloss> in different registers, but by no means different notes; thus, the same song in low, high,

---

or unity is out of the question; rather, one can only speak of an identity of position, where for example every Wednesday has a Thursday before it and a Tuesday behind it. More important for proving the essential unity and equality of notes standing in an octave is what **Guido** <note>Guido of Arezzo (c. 991–1033), the medieval monk who developed the staff notation we use today.</note> cites in the following passage, where he points out that one and the same song can be performed at different pitch levels <note>This refers to singing in octaves.</note> without undergoing any essential change. From this, it clearly follows that essentially identical notes must repeat themselves at different pitch levels.

<gloss>3)</gloss> *incipient et decantent* <note>Latin: "they shall begin and sing out."</note>, meaning to perform simultaneously, which is important to note here. Melodies can be <term>transposed</term> <note>Moving a piece of music so it starts on a different note while keeping the same relationships between the notes.</note> to completely different notes without essential change—for example, a melody in D minor can also be sung in A minor—but not at the same time. Therefore, Guido says there exists a *similitudo* <note>Latin: similarity.</note> between the notes D and A, but not a *perfecta* <note>Latin: perfect.</note> similarity, which is only found in the octave (see Chapter IX).

<gloss>4)</gloss> Manuscript *Cod. 14663* reads: *diversas voces habere* <note>Latin: "to have different voices."</note>.

<summary>This page explains why notes an octave apart are considered identical, comparing the repeating musical scale to the seven-day cycle of the week. It argues that the identical pattern of whole and half steps surrounding these notes creates a "perfect similarity" that allows singers to perform the same melody in different registers.</summary>
<keywords>octave equivalence, Guido of Arezzo, whole tone, semitone, transposition, musical harmony, pitch registers, medieval music theory</keywords>

# Chapter VI.
### Concerning the divisions of pitches and their interpretations. <gloss>1)</gloss>

---

To briefly summarize much concerning the division of the <term>monochord<note>A scientific instrument with a single string and a movable bridge, used in the Middle Ages and Renaissance to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note></term>: the <term>diapason<note>The Greek term for an octave, literally meaning "through all [the strings]."</note></term> always runs in two equal steps toward the end <gloss>2)</gloss>; the <term>diapente<note>The Greek term for a perfect fifth, literally "through five."</note></term> in three; the <term>diatesseron<note>The Greek term for a perfect fourth, literally "through four."</note></term> in four; and the <term>whole tone<note>The standard interval between two adjacent notes, such as C to D.</note></term> in nine. The more numerous these steps are <gloss>4)</gloss>, the shorter they are in distance. You will not be able to find any other divisions besides these four. 

Furthermore, "diapason" is interpreted as *from all* <gloss>5)</gloss>, either because it contains all the pitches within itself <gloss>6)</gloss>, or because in ancient times, <term>citharas<note>An ancient Greek stringed instrument, similar to a lyre.</note></term> were constructed with eight strings across that interval. In this specific interval, the first and lower pitch possesses two spaces...

| Note 1 | Interval | Note 2 | Interval | Note 3 | Interval | Note 4 | Interval | Note 5 | Interval | Note 6 |
| :--- | :---: | :--- | :---: | :--- | :---: | :--- | :---: | :--- | :---: | :--- |
| **Γ** | 1 | **A** | 1 | **B** | 1/2 | **C** | 1 | **D** | 1 | **E** |
| | | | | | | **D** | 1 | **E** | 1/2 | **F** | 1 | **G** | 1 | **a** | 1 | **♮** |

(cf. Chapter IX.)

<note>The diagram above illustrates the intervals between notes. The top row starts on Gamma (the lowest note), while the bottom row shows a similar pattern of whole steps (1) and half steps (1/2) starting from D.</note>

<margin>1) Codex 9921 reads: Likewise, concerning division and their interpretations.</margin>
<margin>2) Codex 9921 reads: runs in steps toward the end.</margin>
<note>3) The octave spans two equal parts. That is, if one wishes to find the octave of any given tone, the string length of that tone must be divided into two equal parts; the lower tone then claims both parts, while its octave (the higher tone) claims only one part (a 2:1 ratio). Likewise, to obtain the fifth of a tone, the string length must be divided into three equal parts: the lower tone claims all three, while the fifth (the higher tone) claims two (a 3:2 ratio). Similarly for the fourth, the lower tone takes all four parts, while the higher fourth takes three (a 4:3 ratio). For the whole tone, the lower note claims nine parts and the higher note eight (a 9:8 ratio).</note>

# Chapter VI.

### Concerning the division of pitches and their further explanation.

To summarize all points regarding the division of the <term>monochord<note>A scientific instrument with a single string and a movable bridge, used in the Middle Ages and Renaissance to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note></term> in a few words, let it be noted that the **octave** always extends across two equal parts from beginning to end; the **fifth** across three; the **fourth** across four; and the **second** <gloss>3)</gloss> across nine. These parts are shorter the more numerous they are <note>A variant in Manuscript 9921 reads: "which (the octave, fifth, and fourth) are shorter in distance the more numerous they are in steps."</note>. One will not be able to discover any other divisions besides these four. 

<term>Diapason<note>The Greek term for an octave, literally meaning "through all [the strings]."</note></term> (octave) is derived from "all," either because it encompasses all pitches within itself <gloss>6)</gloss> <note>"Within itself" is missing in Manuscript 9921.</note>, or because in ancient times the <term>citharas<note>An ancient Greek stringed instrument, similar to a lyre but with a larger wooden soundbox.</note></term> were strung with eight strings spanning this same octave. In this type of tonal connection, the first and lower pitch comprises two parts,

---

<note>The following notes refer to the source text's scholarly commentary:</note>

<gloss>5)</gloss> The phrase "from all" <note>original Latin: "de omnibus"</note> probably cannot be taken as a literal translation of "diapason," as the Greek *dia* (through) does not correspond to the Latin *de* (from/concerning). While the scholar Gerbert highlights the phrases "from all," "from five," and "from four" with special formatting, the specific reasoning for the term <term>diatesseron<note>The Greek term for a perfect fourth.</note></term> shows that the author is primarily interested in the number "four." This is because the interval includes four pitches, and the lower pitch always contains four mathematical parts, of which the higher pitch is assigned only three. Similarly, the phrases "diapente is said of five" and "tone takes its name from sounding" <note>original: "intonando"</note> support the view that we should read these as "concerning" the numbers five and four, rather than as direct translations of the Greek names.

<summary>This page explains the mathematical ratios of the four primary musical intervals (octave, fifth, fourth, and second) as measured on a monochord and discusses the etymology of the term 'diapason'. It emphasizes that the octave is the only perfectly harmonious interval and the basis for all others.</summary>

<keywords>monochord, octave, diapason, fifth, diapente, fourth, diatesseron, musical ratios, cithara, Gerbert, musical theory</keywords>
