...parts, whereas the higher pitch occupies one part, as from **A** to **a**. The <term>Diapente<note>The Greek term for a perfect fifth, literally meaning "from five."</note></term> is said to be "of five"; for there are five pitches within its span, such as from **D** up to **a**. But its lower ¹) pitch occupies three parts, and the higher pitch occupies two. The <term>Diatessaron<note>The Greek term for a perfect fourth, literally meaning "from four."</note></term> sounds "of four," for it both possesses four pitches, and its lower pitch occupies four parts, while the higher occupies three, such as from **D** to **G**. You should remember ²) that these three types are called <term>symphonies<note>In medieval music theory, a "symphonia" is a consonance—a "sweet" sounding-together of voices.</note></term>, that is, sweet couplings of voices, because in the <term>diapason<note>The octave.</note></term> diverse pitches sound as one. ³) However, the fifth (diapente) and the fourth (diatessaron) possess the laws of <term>diaphony<note>Literally "different sounding," here referring to the practice of early polyphony or "organum."</note></term>, that is, of the organum, and they produce pitches that are in some way similar. ⁴) The <term>Tone<note>A whole step or major second.</note></term> (whole step) however takes its name from "intoning," that is, from "sounding," which establishes nine steps for the larger ⁵) pitch and eight for the smaller; however, the <term>semitone<note>A half step.</note></term> and the <term>ditone<note>A major third, literally "two tones."</note></term> ⁶), although they join voices together in singing, they do not receive any specific mathematical division on the <term>monochord<note>A single-stringed instrument used by theorists to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of intervals.</note></term>. ⁷)

# Chapter VII.
## Concerning the four modes and the affinities of pitches ⁸)

Since there are seven pitches—because, as we have said, the others (that is, the octave pitches) are the same—it is sufficient to explain these seven, which are of different modes and different quali-

---

¹) Manuscript 9921: "But its lower pitch."
²) It is not apparent what this is meant to refer to in the preceding text.
³) i.e., two tones standing in an octave sound like one tone, even though they are in themselves two different tones existing independently.
⁴) "They produce pitches as similar as possible," i.e., through the fifth and fourth one obtains pitches which, with regard to their <term>property<note>original Latin: "proprietas"</note></term>—that is, their peculiar position in the musical system—have much similarity, such as **C** and **G**, **D** and **a**, **D** and **G**, **E** and **a**, etc. See Chapter IX and Chapter V above.
⁵) Manuscript 9921: "which indeed for the larger pitch."
⁶) Manuscript 9921 adds "and the <term>semiditone<note>A minor third.</note></term>."

...the higher pitch only one part, as from **A** to **a**. The <term>Diapente<note>original: "Diapente"; the musical interval of a perfect fifth.</note></term> (Fifth) is named from "five"; for within its span, "five" pitches are contained, such as from **D** to **a**. However, the lower tone of this interval occupies three parts, and the higher tone two. The <term>Diatessaron<note>original: "Diatessaron"; the musical interval of a perfect fourth.</note></term> (Fourth) comes from "four"; for on one hand it encompasses "four" pitches, and on the other, its lower tone has four parts while the higher has three, such as from **D** to **G**. These three types—as you will recall ²)—have been called <term>symphonic<note>Symphonic here means "sounding together" in harmony; these were the only intervals considered "perfect" consonances in the Middle Ages.</note></term> consonances, that is, pleasant combinations of sound, because in the octave, different pitches sound as if they were a single one. ³) The **Fifth** and **Fourth**, however, possess the privilege of **diaphony**, that is, of <term>organum<note>The earliest form of polyphony where a second voice is added to a plainchant melody.</note></term>, and they produce tones that are as similar as possible. ⁴) The <term>Tonus<note>original: "Tonus"; a whole tone or major second.</note></term> (whole step) takes its name from "tones," that is, "sounds"; in this interval, the lower pitch is assigned nine parts and the higher pitch eight. The <term>semitone<note>A half step.</note></term> (the minor second) and the <term>ditone<note>original: "Ditonus"; a major third, literally "two tones."</note></term> (the major third) ⁶) receive no specific division points on the monochord, even though they also occur as melodic combinations during singing. ⁷)

# Chapter VII.
## On the four modes and the affinity of pitches ⁸).

---

Since there are now seven pitches—because, as has been said, the others (the eighth pitches) are essentially the same—it is sufficient to explain groups of seven pitches, which belong to different modes and are of different qualities.

---

⁷) The major and minor thirds, which could only have been found in their perfect purity through division by five and six, were therefore—as is explicitly emphasized here—completely excluded from the monochord division; these pure intervals and their inversions, the major and minor sixths, were thus entirely unknown to the ancients. However, the major and minor thirds and sixths that occur by chance in the monochord division described in **Chapter III** between the pitches **A-C**, **B-D**, **C-E**, **D-F**, and so on, are so altered in their vibration ratios that not only the ancients but even today, every musical ear must declare such thirds and sixths to be horrible dissonances. Anyone can convince themselves of this by practically producing the series of tones **Γ A B C D** <note>The letter 'H' in the German source refers to B-natural.</note> etc. with perfectly pure fourths and fifths on an instrument according to the instructions given in **Chapter III**.

⁸) **Codex 9921** reads: "**On the affinities of voices and the four modes**" and...

...completed. ¹) The first mode of pitches is when a pitch is lowered by a tone and raised by a tone, a semitone, and two tones, such as **A** and **D** ²). The second mode is when a pitch is raised by two tones, such as **B** and **E** ³). The third is the mode which descends by a semitone and two tones, but ascends by two tones and a semitone, as in **C** and **F** ⁴). The fourth, however, is lowered by a tone, but rises through two tones and a semitone, as in **G** ⁵). And note that they follow one another in order, so that the first is at **A**, the second at **B**, the third at **C**. Likewise, the first is at **D**, the second at **E**,

***

Manuscript 14663: "On the various modes and qualities of the art of music." <note>original: "De modis et qualitatibus diversis musicae artis."</note> It is striking that the headings themselves do not even agree with those listed in the table of contents provided earlier. There, the heading for Chapter VII is: "On the affinity of pitches through four modes," <note>original: "De affinitate vocum per quatuor modos"</note> which gives a significantly different sense—that is, concerning the relationship of the tones insofar as they are grouped according to four "musical modes."

¹) The sense is: Since the pitches situated in octaves are always essentially the same, one does not need to consider every single pitch throughout the entire system to develop the modes and the different qualities of the tones; rather, one only needs to consider seven at a time, because the eighth pitch is always identical to the first.

...are ¹). The first **mode** <term>Tonart|In this context, the text refers to 'modes' or 'qualities' of pitches based on their surrounding intervals, a concept central to medieval music theory.</term> is that where a pitch descends by a whole tone, and ascends by a whole tone, a semitone, and again two whole tones, such as **A** and **D** ²). ...are lowered or raised according to the arrangement of tones and semitones: for example, the first pitch **A** and the fourth **D** are said to be similar and of one mode, because both have a whole tone in descent, but in ascent have a whole tone, a semitone, and two whole tones. And this is the first similarity in pitches, that is, the first mode." <note>original Latin: "tur vel deponuntur secundum dispositionem tonorum et semitoniorum: utputa prima vox A et quarta D similes et unius modi dicuntur, quia utraque in depositione tonum, in elevatione vero habent tonum et semitonium et duos tonos. Atque haec est prima similitudo in vocibus, hoc est, primus modus."</note>

In English: "All pitches are similar and form similar melodic progressions and consistent groups of tones to the extent that they allow similar movements upward and downward according to the arrangement of whole tones and semitones: just as, for example, the first pitch **A** and the fourth pitch **D** are called similar and of one kind, because both have a whole tone when descending, and a whole tone, a semitone, and two whole tones when ascending. And this is the first similarity among the pitches, that is, the first (musical) mode."

³)

# Chapter VIII.
## Concerning other affinities of pitches and the flat (♭) and natural (♮)³.)

If there are any other affinities, they [the theorists] likewise created them through the fourth and the fifth⁴). For since the octave <term>diapason|An interval of an octave, which in medieval theory was seen as the sum of a fourth and a fifth.</term> contains within itself the fourth and the fifth, and contains the same letters at both ends <note>For example, from low A to high a.</note>, there is always a letter in its middle space that corresponds to both sides of the octave in such a way that the letter to which it produces a fourth from the lower notes also corresponds to it in the higher notes by a fifth, as is noted in the figure above.

---

<note>The following section translates a commentary in German that critiques the preceding Latin text.</note>

"...and **G** according to the following." This, however, is not correct, as the specified intervals are only found at **G**:

**F** —(1)— **G** —(1)— **a** —(1)— **♮** —(½)— **c**
<note>The numbers above represent the distance between notes: 1 for a whole tone, ½ for a semitone.</note>

On the other hand, they are not found at **D**:

**C** —(1)— **D** —(1)— **E** —(½)— **F** —(1)— **G**

In his **Letter** <note>original Latin: "Epist." Referring to Guido d'Arezzo’s 'Epistola de ignoto cantu' (Letter on Unknown Chant), written c. 1028.</note>, **Guido** also says explicitly: "Only the seventh, **G**, makes the fourth mode, which in its descent has one whole tone and a semitone..."

# Chapter VIII.

## Concerning other affinities of the pitches and the flat (♭) and the natural (♮) ³)

If there are any other affinities, they are established in a similar way through the fourth and the fifth. Since the octave <term>octave|The interval between one musical pitch and another with double its frequency; in medieval theory, it is seen as the perfect union of a fourth and a fifth.</term> contains within itself a fourth and a fifth and has the same letter-names on both sides, there will always be a letter found in its middle space that stands in such a relationship to both sides of the octave that it forms a fourth from the lower side and is joined with the same letter from the upper side within the span of a fifth—

---

<note>original: "et duos tonos, in elevatione vero duos habet tonos et semitonium." — "The seventh pitch G alone, on the other hand, forms the fourth mode, which has one whole tone, one semitone, and two whole tones when descending, but two whole tones and one semitone when ascending."</note>

¹) Manuscript 9921 reads: *Again* <note>original: "Iterum"</note>

²) Manuscript 9921 provides, instead of the table above, the representation drawn in the autograph supplement.

³) Manuscript 9921 reads: *Likewise concerning affinities and the natural (♮) and flat (♭).* <note>original: "Item de affinitatibus et ♮ et ♭."</note>

⁴) Manuscript 9921: *they made.* <note>original: "fecere."</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical "affinities" and the mathematical structure of octaves, specifically how a middle note relates to the notes an octave apart via the intervals of a fourth and a fifth. ...and to whichever note [the middle note] provides a fifth <term>fifth|The interval of a *diapente*, spanning five scale degrees.</term> from the lower notes, it will give a fourth <term>fourth|The interval of a *diatessaron*, spanning four scale degrees.</term> from the higher notes, such as with **A**, **E**, and **a**. For **a** <note>The source text uses "A", but Codex 9921 more correctly reads "a" here to represent the note an octave higher.</note> and **E** agree in their descent <note>original: *depositione*</note>, both of which are formed of two whole tones and a semitone <note>original: *in utraque*</note>. Likewise, since **G** resonates with **C** and **D** through these same interval types <note>namely, the fourth and the fifth</note>, it takes the descent of one and the ascent of the other. For both **C** and **G** rise by two tones and a semitone, while **D** and **G** fall by a tone and a semitone.

<note>The core concept here is that every octave—such as A to a, C to c, or D to d—contains a middle note that splits the span into a fourth and a fifth. For example, in the span A-E-a, E is a fifth above A and a fourth below a.</note>

The "round b" <note>original: *b rotundum*, the ancestor of our modern flat symbol ♭</note> is less regular; they call it "added" or "soft" <note>original: *b molle*, the origin of the term "B-flat"</note>. It has a concord with **F**, and it was added specifically because **F** could not have a concord with the note a fourth above it—the "square natural" <note>original: ♮ or *b quadratum*</note>—because they differ by a tritone <term>tritone|An interval of three whole tones (like F to B-natural) that was considered harsh and difficult to sing in medieval music.</term>. You should not, however, combine both types of "b" (the flat ♭ and the natural ♮) within the same musical phrase <term>neuma|A short melodic sequence or the sign used to represent it.</term>.

| Intervals | 1 | 1 | 1/2 | 1 | 1 | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Upper Letters** | **C** | **D** | **E** | **F** | **G** | [flourish] |
| **Lower Letters** | **Γ** (Gamma) | **A** | **B** | **C** | **D** | **E** |
| **Intervals** | 1 | 1 | 1/2 | 1 | 1 | |

In chant, we primarily use the "soft b" where **F** or **f** is frequently repeated in the low or high range. In such cases, it seems to cause a certain confusion and transformation of the sound: **G** begins to sound like the *protus* <term>protus|The first mode or "tone" in the medieval system, characterized by a specific arrangement of steps.</term>, **a** sounds like the *deuterus* <term>deuterus|The second medieval mode.</term>, while the "soft b" itself sounds like the *tritus* <term>tritus|The third medieval mode.</term>. Because of this shifting effect, many authors have not even mentioned it. Another...

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and harmonic analysis of how specific notes (like G or E) function as "midpoints" within octaves. It then transitions into the historical and practical reason for B-flat (the soft b) in medieval and Renaissance music theory.</meta>

...matches, as indicated in the figure above, and that with the same letter by which it forms a fifth <term>fifth|The interval of a *diapente*, spanning five scale degrees.</term> from the lower notes, it gives a fourth <term>fourth|The interval of a *diatessaron*, spanning four scale degrees.</term> from the higher notes, as in for example **A**, **E**, **a**. For **A** <note>1) The author likely means the relationship between the low A and the E above it.</note> and **E** agree in their descending movement <note>original: *absteigenden Bewegung*</note>, which for both <note>2) original: *bei beiden*</note> is completed through two whole tones and one semitone. In a similar way, when **G** sounds with **C** and **D** in those same intervals <note>3) namely, the fourth and the fifth</note>, it takes on the descending movement of one and the ascending movement of the other. For both **C** and **G** rise equally through two whole tones and one semitone, and **D** and **G** likewise move downward equally through one whole tone and a semitone. 

The "round b" <note>original: *das runde b*, the ancestor of the modern flat symbol ♭</note>, however—which is also called the "added" or "soft" <note>original: *b moll*, the origin of the musical term "B-flat"</note> because it is less regular—stands in a familial (harmonic) relationship to **F**. It was added specifically because **F** could not find a harmonic connection with the fourth note calculated from it—the "square b" <note>original: ♮ or *b quadratum*, the modern "natural" sign</note>—from which it is separated by three whole tones <note>4) This refers to the tritone, a famously dissonant interval in early music.</note>. One should not, however, combine both versions of "b"—the round (**b**) and the square (**♮**)—within one and the same musical phrase. One primarily makes use of the "soft b" <note>original: *b moll*</note> in that style of singing in which **F** and **f** are used frequently in succession, whether in the high or low register; in such cases, it then seems to cause a certain mixing and transformation, so that **G** sounds like the first tone, **a** like the second tone, and the **soft b** itself like the third tone. For this reason, many authors make no mention of it [the B-flat] at all. In general—

---

Accordingly, every note can also be the middle of an octave; it forms a fifth with the lower note and a fourth with the higher note—or it can be the middle of another octave, where it represents a fourth with the lower note and a fifth with the higher note. Thus, **G** is the middle of the octave **C–c**, and also the middle of the octave **D–d**:

->&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Fourth&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Fifth<-
->C _ G _ c&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;D _ G _ d.<-
->&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Fifth&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Fourth<-

With one note it shares the descending movement, and with the other, the ascending movement:

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between specific notes, specifically addressing the practical application of B-flat (the soft b) versus B-natural. It explains how to transpose melodies to avoid the "irregular" B-flat and begins a discussion on the similarity of musical modes and their "differences" (endings).</meta>

-> — 44 — <-

Indeed, the "square" B <note>original: ♮; the natural sign</note> was generally accepted for common use. But if you wish to avoid using the "soft" B <note>original: ♭; the flat sign</note> entirely, you must adjust the neumes <term>neumes|Early musical notation signs used before the invention of the modern five-line staff</term> in which it appears. You should temper them so that instead of the sequence **F**, **G**, **a**, and that **♭**, you use **G**, **a**, **♮**, and **c**. 

Alternatively, if there is a neume that requires two whole tones and a semitone when rising after **D**, **E**, **F**—which is what the "soft" B provides¹) —or one that requires two whole tones when falling after **D**, **E**, **F**, then you should use **a**, **♮**, **c** in place of **D**, **E**, **F**. These notes belong to the same mode and naturally possess those same patterns of rising and falling. By clearly distinguishing between these movements in the sequences **D**, **E**, **F** and **a**, **♮**, **c**, one removes the most troublesome confusion.

We have touched briefly on the similarity of these musical tones because the more we seek out similarity among different things, the more we reduce the very diversity that causes a confused mind to struggle. Things that are unified are always easier to grasp than things that are divided. Therefore, all musical modes <term>modes|The medieval precursors to modern scales, such as Dorian or Phrygian</term> and the distinctions between them are adapted to these three notes: **C**, **D**, and **E**.²) By "distinctions," I mean those which many call "differences" <term>differences|Specifically the *differentiae*, the various melodic endings of a psalm tone used to transition back to an antiphon</term>—that is, the *seculorum amen* <note>original: *seculorum amen*; the final words of the "Gloria Patri" prayer, used in chant to categorize melodic endings</note>. A "difference" is named as such because it distinguishes...

---

<note>German commentary below:</note>
...but it should not be used interchangeably with the "square" B <note>original: ♮ (si)</note> within one and the same musical phrase. It should only be used in pieces where **F** occurs frequently and appears more as a fundamental tone, even if only in passing cadences <term>cadence|A melodic or harmonic configuration that creates a sense of resolution or 'resting point'</term>. Furthermore, the "round" B <note>original: ♭ rotundum</note> can lead to a mixing of the modes and thus cause confusion, which is why many theorists reject it entirely. Even Guido <note>Guido of Arezzo (c. 991–1033), a pivotal figure in medieval music theory</note> speaks against it in his *Epistle* and wants it avoided as much as possible. One should especially write everything on **a**, **♮**, **c** instead of on **D**, **E**, **F** with the "round" B.

-> — 45 — <-

However, it was generally thought best to have a second natural sign <note>original: ♮; the symbol for the "hard" or "square" B</note>. But if you wish to avoid using the soft B <note>original: ♭ molle; B-flat</note> altogether, you should rewrite the neumes <term>neumes|The graphic symbols used in medieval music notation to indicate the shape of a melody before the five-line staff was standardized</term> in which it occurs. You must change them so that instead of the sequence **F**, **G**, **a**, and the round B <note>original: ♭; B-flat</note>, you obtain **G**, **a**, **♮**, and **c**. 

Alternatively, if the neume is constructed so that after **D**, **E**, **F**, it requires two whole tones and a semitone when ascending—which is exactly what the soft B <note>original: ♭ moll</note> creates¹)—or if it requires two whole tones when descending after **D**, **E**, **F**, then you should use the tones **a**, **♮**, and **c** in place of **D**, **E**, **F**. These are of the exact same nature and naturally include the aforementioned patterns of rising and falling. By clearly distinguishing between the rising and falling intervals of the tones **D**, **E**, **F** and **a**, **♮**, **c** in this manner, the highly disadvantageous confusion will resolve itself.

Something more should be said here regarding the similarity of the tones. The more we explore the similarity between different things, the more we reduce the apparent contradiction that often causes a confused mind to struggle in vain. Indeed, things that agree are always more easily grasped than things that differ. All musical modes <term>modes|The specific patterns of notes and intervals that characterized medieval chant, similar to modern scales</term> and the distinctions between them are therefore linked to these three tones: **C**, **D**, and **E**.²) I use the term "distinctions" for what most people call a "difference"—that is, the "unto ages of ages, amen" <note>original: *saeculorum amen*; the final words of the Gloria Patri, the ending of which determined how a psalm tone would transition back to the antiphon</note>. It is called a "difference" for this reason,

---

¹) ...so that any sequence of tones proceeding from **D**, **E**, or **F** using the round B <note>original: ♭ rotundum; B-flat</note> can be replaced by the natural sequence of **a**, **♮**, and **c**.

---

²) Since all tones in their rising and falling movement agree with one of these three tones, **C**, **D**, or **E** (see Chapter IX), the essence of all modes and their differences can be made somewhat clear through these three tones. However, as has been noted frequently, perfect similarity across all relationships of the entire octave is only found in those tones that stand an octave apart.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical intervals and their relationships. # Chapter X. <note>original: "Caput X." Manuscript 9921 omits Chapters 9 and 10, jumping from the end of Chapter 8 to Chapter 11, which it labels as Chapter 9.</note>
## Concerning the similarity of voices in song, of which only the octave is perfect.

The aforementioned voices, insofar as they are similar—some in <term>ascent|elevatione; the upward movement of a melody</term>, such as **C & G** or **D & a**; others in <term>descent|depositione; the downward movement of a melody</term>, such as **a & E** or **G & D**; and others in both directions, such as **C & G** <note>The editor notes this is incorrect and contradicts Chapter 8. C and G are only similar in ascent. Below G lies a whole tone, but below C lies a half-tone. E and ♮ (B-natural) share two whole tones descending, but differ in their ascending intervals.</note> and **E & ♮**—create similar <term>neumes|melodic phrases or groups of notes</term>. Thus, understanding one neume reveals the nature of the other. However, where no similarity is shown, or where they belong to different <term>modes|the medieval precursor to modern musical scales or keys</term>, one note cannot accept the neume or melody of the other. If you force them to do so, you will <gloss>transform</gloss> <note>Meaning you will change the very nature of the song.</note> the music. For example, if someone wished to begin an <term>antiphon|a short liturgical chant</term> that starts on **D** instead on **E** or **F**, which belong to a different mode.

---

# Chapter IX <note>original: "Kapitel IX." Footnote 1 refers to the editor's notes on numbering discrepancies between various manuscripts of this treatise.</note>
## On the Similarity of Tones in Song, Which is Perfect Only in the Octave.

Just as the aforementioned tones are similar—some in **ascent**, such as **C** and **G**, or **D** and **a**; others in **descent**, such as **a** and **E**, or **G** and **D**; and others in both, such as **C** and **G**, or **E** and **♮** <note>The symbol ♮ refers to B-natural, also known as "B durum" or "hard B." Footnote 2: The editor notes that the author's claim here is slightly flawed; C and G are only truly similar in ascent because the intervals below them (a half-step vs. a whole step) differ.</note>—they likewise form similar groups of tones, so that understanding one reveals the other. However, in the case of tones where no similarity is proven, or which belong to different <term>modes|the medieval system of scales that preceded modern major and minor keys</term>, one pitch cannot take on the <term>neume|a melodic phrase or group of notes</term> and melodic shape of the other. If one attempts to force the melody of one tone onto another by violence, they will ³) <gloss>completely transform it</gloss>. If, for instance, someone wanted to begin an <term>antiphon|a short liturgical chant used as a refrain</term> that starts on **D** with the tones **E** or **F** (which belong to a different mode), he would:

voices, begin, and would soon perceive by ear how great a transformation of diversity would occur. # Chapter X.
## On the Modes, and the Recognition and Correction of False Melody.

These are the four <term>modes</term> or <term>tropes</term>—which people improperly call "tones" <note>In medieval theory, a "tone" strictly refers to a specific interval distance, whereas a "mode" refers to the entire scale or melodic category. Calling a mode a "tone" was considered a common technical error.</note>—which are separated from each other by such a natural diversity that one does not grant a place to the other in its own seat, and one never receives or transforms the melodic gesture of another ²); dissonance also creeps into singing through error in such a way ³),

1)

In the top row, the letters B, C, and D are connected by curved lines above them; the interval between B and C is labeled '1/2' (a semitone), and the interval between C and D is labeled '1' (a whole tone). In the bottom row, the letters F, G, and a are connected by curved lines below them; the interval between F and G is labeled '1', and the interval between G and a is labeled '1'. This shows that while D has a whole tone below it, 'a' has two whole tones (a ditone) below it reaching down to F.</image-desc>

# Chapter X.
### On the Modes and the Recognition and Correction of a False Melody.

These are the four modes or <term>tropes|another term for the medieval church modes</term>, which are also improperly called "tones." These are separated from one another by a natural difference in kind, such that none allows the other a place in its own position, and one either transforms the melodic flow of the other or does not accept it at all ²). A discord also "creeps" ³) in...

---

...cannot make [a pitch] the fundamental tone of the other without completely changing the melodic flow, as demonstrated in Chapter IX.
³) Note here the use of the present tense: "it creeps in" <note>original Latin: "subrepit"</note>, which clearly indicates that the author is not speaking here of specific historical falsifications of certain ways of singing, but of improprieties in song that are brought about at any time by poorly educated singers with bad voices. Therefore, it is not necessary to think of "lowering by subtracting" <note>original Latin: "demunt gravantes"</note> and "adding by stretching" <note>original Latin: "intendentes adjiciunt"</note> as an intentional raising and lowering of the intervals on the part of singers who...

<summary>This page explains why different pitches in the same musical mode (like D and A) are not interchangeable due to the different patterns of whole and half steps surrounding them. --- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter X regarding how singers can mistakenly distort a melody. The previous page introduced the four modes and warned that a discord can "creep" into the music when the boundaries between these modes are not respected.</meta>

...when they either take away from well-measured notes by lowering them slightly, or add to them by raising them; this is what the crooked voices of men do when, by raising or lowering the aforementioned proportion beyond what is proper, they either pervert the <term>neume|a group of notes sung to a single syllable</term> of any mode into another mode, or begin in a place that does not accept the pitch, or make certain subtractions in the <term>tritus|the third 'maneria' or grouping of modes, comprising the 5th and 6th modes</term> ¹),

---

...in anticipation of pure harmony, [some might argue that singers] would have deepened the <term>major third|the interval between two notes spanning four semitones</term> of the <term>monochord|a scientific instrument with a single string used to measure musical ratios</term> from the Pythagorean ratio (64 : 81) to the pure ratio of 4 : 5, and raised the too-low <term>minor second|the smallest interval in the medieval scale</term> (243 : 256) to the ratio of 15 : 16. However, Guido would certainly have expressed himself more precisely regarding such an important fact—which could only have been inspired by highly educated singers—than with the vague words "beyond what is proper" <note>Latin: "plus iusto"</note>. Furthermore, the phrase "they pervert the neume of any mode into another mode" finds no explanation in that mathematical interpretation.

In general, the whole of Chapter X does not provide what one might expect from reading the heading, as there is basically no talk of specific rules for recognizing and correcting faulty melodies. Instead, it merely points out individual improprieties in practical singing, such as the impure delivery of intervals, the insertion of half-steps, and the mixing of modes by bad singers.

Our passage, therefore, only intends to say that disharmony enters the singing because singers with "crooked" <note>Latin: *pravae*</note> voices do not deliver the intervals purely at all; they drive some notes too high beyond what is proper, take others too low, and thus often turn whole tones into half-tones and half-tones into whole tones. By doing so, they lose the normal scale and wander from one mode into another, or reach places where, according to the logic of the system, a specific note does not exist at all—for which the <term>Communion|a chant sung during the distribution of the Eucharist</term> *Diffusa est* is cited as an example at the end of the chapter.

In his *Letter on an Unknown Chant* <note>Latin title: *Epistola de ignoto cantu*</note>, Guido also touches upon these grievances insofar as he provides his friend Michael with a means to avoid them. He advises him to memorize certain short melodic phrases in which the characteristics of the mode are particularly sharply defined. With the help of these, he could immediately recognize the mode of any song he heard by comparison, and on the other hand, easily convince himself whether he had intoned a melody correctly and sung it through without error. If he found that at the end or during the course of the song it did not align with the melodic phrase of the mode in question, he would know that he had fallen out of the mode—that is, he had committed the error being discussed here.

<summary>This page contains a commentary on Guido d'Arezzo's warnings against poor singing, clarifying that his "rules" were practical advice for maintaining modal integrity rather than complex mathematical tuning adjustments. It highlights Guido's pedagogical method of using mnemonic melodic phrases to help singers stay within a specific mode.</summary>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical errors, specifically focusing on how singers deviate from the precise mathematical ratios of the monochord. It addresses the corruption of the modes and the philological history of certain terms in Guido d’Arezzo's manuscripts.</meta>

...[creeps] in through falsehood in singing in such a way, when one takes away a little from the **well-measured tones** 1) by lowering them, or adds to them by raising them. The crooked voices of men do this by driving the prescribed tone-proportion excessively high or letting it sink, and thus pervert the melodic flow of any given mode into another mode, or by beginning at a place that does not permit a tone, or by making 2) certain <term>subductiones|subtractions or lowering of pitch</term> 3)—

---

1) "Well-measured," namely measured exactly on the <term>monochord|a scientific instrument with a single string used to measure musical ratios</term>, thus perfectly pure, as required by Guido, who urged his students to practice the tones according to the monochord.

2) The passage from "or certain subtractions" <note>original: "vel quasdam subductiones"</note> to "they add a tone" <note>original: "apponunt tonum"</note> is not found in the manuscripts of the 11th century, neither in Codex 9921, which does not contain Chapters IX and X at all, nor in Codex 14663, nor in Codex 14965 a. and b.; it therefore does not seem to originate from Guido. It first appears in a Codex from the former Benedictine monastery of St. Emmeram, now Codex lat. 14523 of the Royal Court and State Library in Munich, as well as in Codex 13021 from the 12th–13th century, and in a Codex written by Hartmann Schedel in Nuremberg in the year 1493 (cf. Schlecht, *History of Church Music*, p. 36; *Monthly Journals for Music History* 1872, p. 140). Although we are therefore dealing only with an interpolation from the era of <term>measured polyphonic music|music with multiple independent parts and a fixed rhythm</term>, which had already begun to exert its influence on <term>plainsong|traditional unison church chant</term>, the explanation given therein regarding the use of the <term>diesis|the smallest interval in the scale</term> is nevertheless very interesting. — According to the manuscripts of the 11th century, the context of the text is therefore: *or they begin in a place which does not receive the voice; as may be shown by the example in the Communion: Grace is poured out . . . . .* <note>original: "aut in loco, qui vocem non recipit, inchoant; quod ut exemplo pateat in Communione: Diffusa est gratia . . . . ."</note>

---

3) *Subductio* is the Latin expression for the Greek **diesis** <note>original: διεσις</note>. We see this clearly from the treatise of Abbot Engelbert of Admont: *On Music*, chapter XVIII (Gerbert *Scriptores*, page 312), where it is taught that the whole tone cannot be divided into two equal harmonic proportions, but only into two unequal ones. The larger part is called the **apotome** <note>original: αποτομη</note>, that is, the "section amounting to more than half" (*superdivisa*), and forms the **major semitone** <note>original: semitonium majus</note>; the smaller part is called **diesis**, that is, the "remaining part" (*subductio*, from *subducere*: to take away, subtract, or take into account) and forms the **minor semitone** <note>original: semitonium minus</note>. The entire passage reads: *because of this it is divided into* <note>original: "propter hoc dividitur in"</note>

...which are called <term>dieses|very small musical intervals, often smaller than a semitone</term>, since it is not appropriate to admit them into use except in certain specific places that arise. So that this may be easily clear, we present an example:²)

<image-desc>A musical diagram consisting of three horizontal lines with alphabetical note designations. Above the top line are letters 'a' (some joined by dashes as 'a-a'). ->Longing.<- <note>original: "Desiderium"</note>

For it is not permitted to occur on any sound³) except for the third and sixth; for even if it is found in another, it must be thoroughly corrected; and not only the thing itself, but also the root from which it uselessly proceeded must be eradicated.⁴)

---

[The whole tone is divided into] two unequal parts, namely into a larger one called <term>Apotome|the 'leftover' part of a whole tone after a smaller semitone is subtracted</term>, that is, the "over-divided," and this is the major semitone; and into a smaller one called <term>diesis|in this context, the minor semitone</term>, that is, the subtraction from the major semitone; and this is the minor semitone. <note>The size-ratio of the "subtraction" (diesis) or the minor semitone is determined in Chapter XIX with the following words: <note>original: "Diesis est spatium, quo sesquitertia proportio alicujus consonantiae major est duobus tonis; et haec diesis, id est, subductio dicitur semitonium minus."</note> “The diesis is the space by which the <term>sesquitertia|the 4:3 ratio, characteristic of a perfect fourth</term> proportion of any consonance is greater than two tones; and this diesis, that is, subtraction, is called the minor semitone.” This is that interval by which the consonance standing in the ratio of 3:4 (the perfect fourth) is larger than two whole tones (a major third in the ratio 64:81); and this diesis, i.e., subtraction, is called the minor semitone. According to this, the diesis (subtraction, minor semitone) has the ratio 3/4 minus 64/81 = 3/4 times 81/64 = 243/256, thus 243:256. Among the ancients, the diesis formed the smaller half of the minor semitone, thus 499:512. Today, the diesis is the difference between the large (15:16) and small semitone (24:25), thus 15/16 divided by 24/25 = 15/16 times 25/24 = 375/384 = 125/128. This ratio (125:128), moreover, comes very close to that of the ancients (499:512), for 125/128 = 500/512, thus only 1/512 larger.</note>

<note>¹) "Trito" (the third), a word otherwise used only for the musical mode, stands here evidently for "on the third tone," i.e., on the note C (cf. Note 4 on page 15 or 16). The following sentence: "For it is not permitted to occur on any sound, except for the third and sixth" is clearly just a further elaboration of what is said here.</note>

<note>²) The historian Gerbert does not have the notes C and D at the beginning of his edition; however, they are found in all other manuscripts.</note>

It warns that these specific pitch adjustments should only be used on the third and sixth notes of a scale to avoid corrupting the melody.</summary>

As the later discussions show, it is first a matter of ensuring that *C-sharp D* is not sung at the beginning, although this would hardly occur to anyone. Schedel writes in his codex written in the year 1493 (Munich State Library *No. 1500*) over *a g a*: “<term>Diesis</term>,” by which he likely intends to indicate that here, too, a semitone is sung by some.

³) *Sonus* [sound] designates for Guido, as for the ancients in general, the “tone” as a sound determinable by height and depth; the term *vox* [voice] is synonymous. *Tonus* [tone], on the other hand, primarily means a “tone-step”; it also stands in the sense of *modus* [mode] and *tropus* [trope] for a “musical mode,” especially for the “eight tones” (*octo formulae*, *octo toni*) obtained by dividing the four main modes into the authentic and plagal forms. Here, then, it is not a matter of modes, but only of the third and sixth sounds of the musical system, namely *C* and *F* (see Note 4 on page 15 and page 16 respectively).

⁴) From this passage, it is evident that the *diesis-effect* took place not only at *C* and *F*, but also at other sounds. The “subtractions in the third” (<term>subductiones in trito|a technical error where a singer lowers a pitch in the third musical position or mode</term>) criticized above do not, therefore, refer only to the raises on the third sound (*C*); for this reason, the term “in the third” (*in trito*) is not used simply for “in the third” (*in tertio*), but was likely chosen intentionally as a designation for an entire category of sounds. Such a category of sounds results if one notes that Guido and the medieval theorists first...

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of medieval music theory, specifically focusing on the division of modes and the corruption of traditional rules through the misuse of semitones and the 'diesis'.</meta>

->— 56 —<-

It should be noted that because the <term>diesis</term> <note>the smallest interval in ancient Greek music theory, often smaller than a semitone</note> is admitted by some in place of a semitone, they produce a harmony in the manner of a wagon tilting along a rocky path. For this reason, the rules of music are more loosened than those of all other arts; for while some were never able to keep themselves to the [standard] path—indeed, like a full river which, when its own channel is not enough, overflows through the crossroads—so they themselves, in every place where semitones have increased, have chosen another path, namely

---

...accept only four <term>modes</term> <note>the predecessor to modern musical scales</note>, which encompass high (<term>authentic</term>) and low (<term>plagal</term>) melodies and can only be divided into eight tones (<term>formulae</term>) with regard to this. The range of these four modes is accordingly:

1. **Protus** (Dorian) A B C **D** (Tonic) E F G a ♮ c d.
2. **Deuterus** (Phrygian) B C D **E** (Tonic) F G a ♮ c d e.
3. **Tritus** (Lydian) C D E **F** (Tonic) G a ♮ c d e f.
4. **Tetrardus** (Mixolydian) D E F **G** (Tonic) a ♮ c d e f g.

Here the <term>tonic</term> <note>the "home" note or ground tone of the scale</note> always appears as the fourth, and the <term>leading tone</term> <note>the note that naturally "leads" or pulls toward the tonic</note> lying below the tonic as the third of the series. The tonic can therefore also be designated as the "fourth tone" (<term>tetrardus</term>, to distinguish it from the note D, which is the fourth of the system in general) or also as the "first" (<term>protus</term>), if one only considers the authentic form. The "third tone" (<term>tritus</term>, to distinguish it from the note C) refers to all leading tones C, D, E, F, and in transposition also G, a, and ♮. By "lowerings in the tritus" <note>original: subductiones in trito</note> the author means the raising of all leading tones, about which the <term>interpolator</term> <note>an ancient or medieval editor who added notes to a text</note> first remarks that they can only occur in certain cases, and even then only with C and F and on no other tone. —

<margin>¹) Gerbert <note>Martin Gerbert, an 18th-century music historian</note> incorrectly writes here: "because *a* by some," <note>original Latin: "quia a a quibusdam"</note> instead of "because by some," as the old manuscripts read. This reading certainly arose through a misinterpretation of the abbreviations in the old manuscripts. For instance, Manuscript 13021 writes: "because <note>original: q'a</note> by some," where "q'a" stands for "because" <note>original: quia</note> everywhere, such as in the following passage: "The rules are dissolved, because, while nowhere..." The subject here is to be supplemented from the earlier text as "diesis," and the sense is: "Because some use the diesis itself instead of the semitone obtained through chromatic alteration, they produce a song similar to the creaking of a heavy freight wagon whose wheels slip over the stones lying in the path."</margin>

<margin>²) Note here the perfect tenses: *they were able, they have increased, they have chosen*, which point to old, established abuses that make the musical rules appear "loosened" or "dissolved."</margin>

<summary>This page critiques the "dissolved" state of musical rules caused by the misuse of intervals, famously comparing dissonant harmony to a wagon rattling over a rocky road. It defines the four primary medieval modes (Protus, Deuterus, Tritus, and Tetrardus) and their technical structures regarding tonics and leading tones.</summary>

<keywords>modes, Dorian, Phrygian, Lydian, Mixolydian, tonic, diesis, semitones, music theory, Martin Gerbert, leading tone, medieval scales</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical critique of contemporary musical practice, specifically how singers deviate from established rules by substituting the "diesis" for standard semitones.</meta>

->— 57 —<-

It should be noted that some, because they use the <term>diesis</term> <note>original: "Diësis"; the smallest interval in ancient Greek music theory, often treated here as a quarter-tone or smaller</note> in place of the semitone, perform a song as if a heavy wagon were sliding back and forth on a stony road. For this reason, however, the rules of music are more loosened than in any other art, because many—who could nowhere keep to the prescribed path, quite in the manner of a swollen river whose bed is no longer sufficient and which pours over the roads—at every point where semitones have established themselves, have [taken] another path

---

³) <term>accresco</term> <note>Latin: "to grow toward" or "to increase"</note>, "to grow in addition," "to be added increasingly," also "to enlarge by growing," according to which it could be translated as: "at every point where the semitones have enlarged." Namely, by using the semitones obtained through <term>diesising</term> <note>the process of altering a note by a very small interval</note> in a ratio of 224:243, instead of the <term>minor semitone</term> <note>original: "semitonium minus"; a standard Pythagorean interval with a ratio of 243:256</note> (see later). This translation, however, would force the assumption that semitones were already present beforehand and had enlarged afterward. But there is clearly no talk of enlarging the existing semitones B–C and E–F, but rather of the semitones "being added" through the diesising of C and F. It is therefore simpler and more natural to take the word <term>accreverunt</term> <note>Latin: "they have increased/accrued"</note> in its first meaning. 

The passage then states that everywhere that semitones have "been added" to the existing semitones established in the system—E–F<gloss>1/2</gloss>, C♯<gloss>1/2</gloss>, and A♭–B♭<gloss>1/2</gloss>—namely, having established themselves through usage over time (such as the diesising of the <term>leading tones</term> <note>notes that "lead" to the tonic, like F♯ leading to G</note> discussed here, especially on the third and sixth steps [C and F] in Modes I, II, VII, and VIII [D–C♯–D; G–F♯–G]), those people who cannot stick to the rules at all (even those prescribed by tradition) go their own way. This is done, as the <term>interpolator</term> <note>a person who inserted later comments or corrections into the original manuscript</note> mockingly adds, as if they were afraid to enter such a small interval because of their "sublime, imposing figures." 

But where they cannot avoid such semitones at all, they merely scurry past them so fleetingly, singing the diesis itself—scarcely a quarter-tone—instead of the semitone to be obtained through diesising (224:243). They are like smelters who, no matter how cold it is, only scurry quickly past the glowing opening (Latin: <term>ante os</term> <note>"before the mouth"</note>) of the furnace, and at most, with great effort, poke into the embers once if the fire must be stoked. — The entire passage is thus only an explanation of the <term>notandum</term> <note>Latin: "thing to be noted"</note> as to why the diesis is taken by "certain people" in place of the semitone. The interpolator, moreover, wishes to intervene defensively on two fronts. While on the one hand he [advocates] for the application of...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the "diesis," specifically comparing those who use it poorly to people huddled fearfully by a fire, before moving into a more formal definition of when this specific musical interval should be applied within the modes.</meta>

->— 58 —<-

...fearing to enter a narrow cave, lest the greatness of their body, in which they excel, be constricted by either a smaller width or a shorter height of the cover. But when they cannot <note>The Latin text suggests *queunt* (they are able) rather than *quærunt* (they seek).</note> entirely escape, they use the <term>diesis</term> <note>In this context, a very small interval, often a quarter-tone, used to adjust a pitch.</note>, doubtless imitating those who, while they fear the power of the cold, exert themselves by huddling all at once before the mouth of the fireplace.

Therefore¹) this diesis, which, as we said above, takes the place of a semitone ²), is never to be used except in this manner: when the <term>tritus</term> ³) <note>A term referring to the third grouping of musical modes, often associated with the note F.</note> is sung, and the <term>tetrardus</term> <note>The fourth grouping of modes, associated with the note G.</note> is to be extended into the <term>proto</term> <note>The first grouping of modes, associated with the note D.</note>, and again must be lowered into itself, or in the same tritus, or even further down. Then the tritus, which precedes the tetrardus or proto, must be lowered slightly ⁵); this lowering is called a <term>diesis</term> <note>original: "diesis"</note>,

[The commentator notes that the author] stands up for the raised <term>leading tones</term> <note>notes that "lead" the ear toward a resolution, usually a half-step below the target note</note> introduced by custom in his time and wishes to teach their correct application against abuse; he turns in the "Note" against those who, relying on the theoretical system, reject them and, where they cannot avoid them (perhaps when singing together with others), only touch upon them fleetingly through a small <term>inflection</term> of the voice (1/4 tone, diesis), but in doing so disfigure the song in another way (the harmony sounding like a wagon, etc. . .)

---

¹) With this "therefore," the <term>interpolator</term> <note>A later scribe who adds comments or corrections into a text</note> leads back to the previous points by moving to discuss the "certain applicable places" where the diesis or <term>subductio</term> <note>Latin: "lowering" or "withdrawal" of a pitch</note> can occur.

²) "Which takes the place of a semitone" cannot mean "which is a semitone," but only "which represents the place of a semitone," without being an exact semitone. The phrase "as we said above" can only refer to the previous mention of it being "admitted by some in place of a semitone." By this, it was already indirectly said and conceded that the diesis represents the place of a semitone; however, it was criticized that it is actually put in the place of a semitone by some and used practically, whereas it should only be considered a semitone theoretically for calculating the size of the leading tone, as is evident below.

³) <term>Tritus</term> is to be understood as a specific note, not as a mode, as shown in note 4 on page 55; likewise <term>tetrardus</term>. In any other sense, the following "then the tritus must be lowered slightly" would make no sense.

<meta>This page continues the metaphorical comparison of singers to people seeking shelter from the cold, before transitioning into a technical explanation of the "diesis" and its specific application in creating leading tones within the medieval modal system.</meta>

...have taken, namely because they fear entering the narrow cavity, so that their physical stature—for which they are distinguished—might not be constricted by the all-too-narrow width or too-low height of the shell. But where they cannot avoid it at all, they apply the <term>diesis</term> <note>In this context, a small upward adjustment of a pitch to create a leading tone.</note>, imitating the example of those who, though they fear the severity of the cold, nevertheless force themselves to huddle just for a moment into the opening of a smelting furnace. —

This diesis, therefore ¹), which, as stated above, takes the place of a <term>semitone</term> <note>A half-step, the smallest interval in standard Western music.</note> ²), is to be applied nowhere except in the case where the third tone ³) <note>The <term>Tritus</term>, or the third grouping of musical modes.</note> is sung and the fourth tone is to follow in the first mode and should conclude again ⁴) in itself, or in that third tone, or even in a still lower tone. In that case, the third tone, which precedes the fourth or the first, must be drawn upward slightly ⁵). This raising of the pitch is called a diesis and—

---

³) "In the first" <note>original: "proto"</note> must, by contrast, be referred to the <term>mode</term> <note>The scale or "key" of the music.</note>: "The fourth in the first" <note>original: "Tetrardus in proto"</note> is therefore "D in the first mode" (I and II tones).

⁴) "And again" <note>original: "Iterumque"</note> indicates that the fourth tone (D or G) must have already preceded the third, which it then follows, in order to close once more in itself or in the third (C or F) or in a still lower tone. This would result in the following <term>cadences</term> <note>Melodic patterns that bring a phrase to a close.</note>: D C# D; — D C# D C; — D C# D C A; or respectively G F# G; — G F# G F; — G F# G F E; — G F# G F D.

⁵) Here it is expressly stated that in "the mentioned cases" the third tone (C or F) is to be raised, specifically "a little" <note>original: "modicum"</note>, and that precisely this raising of the C (to approximately C#) is called a <term>diesis</term>. The extent of this raising is shown by the accompanying measurement. According to this, the diesis C to C# forms an <term>interval</term> in a ratio of 27:28, which is not quite a <term>minor semitone</term> <note>original: "semitonium minus"</note>, since 27:28 = 243:252, while the minor semitone forms the ratio 243:256. If one subtracts this diesis ratio of 27:28 from the <term>whole tone</term> C to D (8:9), the ratio 224:243 remains for C# to D, (8/9 divided by 27/28 = 8/9 ✕ 28/27 = 224/243) which is slightly larger than the <term>apotome</term> or the <term>major semitone</term> <note>original: "semitonium majus"</note> = 2048:2187; (since 224/243 = 2016/2187). Nevertheless, the ratio 224:243 can

...and is the half of the following semitone ¹), just as the semitone is the half of the following whole tone. It is measured in this way: When you have made nine steps from G to the end and you find 'a', then divide from 'a' to the end by seven, and at the end of the first part you will find the first <term>diesis</term> <note>A very small musical interval, here defined mathematically.</note> between ♮ <note>The symbol for B-natural.</note> and c ²). — Soon the second and third steps will be empty, but the fourth will hold the third diesis.

---

(C-sharp — D) should be considered as a semitone, just as the diesis 27/28 takes the place of a semitone <note>original: "locum semitonii sumit"</note>. In practice, the diesis can find no use in the <term>Chorale</term> <note>Plainchant or Gregorian chant.</note>, as the chromatic interval C — C-sharp cannot occur in the Chorale. It serves only to establish the semitone C-sharp — D by pulling the C up by a diesis. The fact that the semitone C-sharp — D is somewhat large, and that when singing from D to C-sharp the voice must lean even more towards C, corresponds entirely to the "horror of the ancients" regarding semitones; they even sought to avoid the semitone E — F = 243/256 in the 5th and 6th <term>tones</term> <note>The Lydian and Hypolydian modes.</note>, even though it was given in the system.

¹) "and half of the following semitone;" from the following, "is" <note>original: "est"</note> should be added: "The raising is called a diesis and is the half of the following semitone (namely C-sharp — D = 224 : 243), just as in the series of tones the <term>semitonium</term> <note>The minor semitone in Pythagorean tuning, ratio 243:256.</note> (e.g., E — F) is the half of the following whole tone (F — G)." The semitone 243 : 256 is, however, in reality very close to half of the whole tone 8 : 9; for if one subtracts the semitone 243 : 256 from 8 : 9, there remains the <term>apotome</term> <note>The "major" semitone, ratio 2048:2187.</note> 2048 : 2187, which is only about one <term>comma</term> <note>A tiny interval representing the difference between two near-identical notes in different tuning systems, ratio 80:81.</note> larger than the semitone 243 : 256 itself, thus coming very close to our semitone 15 : 16. (The semitone 15 : 16 plus the semitone 243 : 256 equals 3645/4096; the whole tone 8 : 9 is equal to 3640/4095, so that the difference is highly insignificant). Likewise, the diesis 27 : 28 is now very close to half of the semitone 224 : 243; for if one subtracts the diesis 27 : 28 from the semitone 224 : 243, there remains the ratio 6272 : 6561, which is only about half a comma larger than the diesis 27 : 28 itself;

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivation of musical intervals, specifically focusing on the diesis. — 61 —

...represents half of the semitone that follows $^1$), just as the <term>semitone</term> <note>A half-step, the smallest interval in most Western music.</note> is half of the following <term>whole tone</term> <note>An interval consisting of two semitones, like C to D.</note>. It is measured, however, in the following way: When one has created nine parts from G to the end and found 'a', one then divides from 'a' into seven parts, and at the end point of the first part, one will find the first <term>diesis</term> <note>In this context, a very small interval used to "sharpen" a note, mathematically defined here as the ratio 27:28.</note> between ♮ <note>The symbol for B-natural.</note> and c $^2$). The second and third division points that follow remain empty; but the fourth division point marks the place...

---

$^1$) (If one subtracts the diesis $^{27}/_{28}$ again from the ratio $^{6272}/_{6561}$, the small ratio $^{6504}/_{6561}$ (approximately half a <term>comma</term> <note>An extremely small interval, barely perceptible to the ear, resulting from the difference between two types of tuning.</note>) remains. The whole tone C—D = 8 : 9 is thus divided by the diesis 27 : 28 into approximately three parts, of which one part falls on the diesis C—C-sharp and two parts on the semitone C-sharp—D, similar to how the minor third E—G (27 : 32) is divided by the <term>minor semitone</term> <note>original: "semitonium minus"; a specific small semitone in Pythagorean tuning with the ratio 243:256.</note> $^{243}/_{256}$ into three parts, of which one part falls on the semitone E—F and two parts on the whole tone F—G.)

$^2$) Thus, the string length 'a' = $^8/_9$ is to be divided into seven parts to obtain the diesis at the first division point. The string length of this diesis amounts to $^6/_7$ of $^8/_9$ = $^8/_9 \times ^6/_7 = \frac{48}{63} = \frac{16}{21}$. However, if the string length of 'a' = $^8/_9$, then that of ♮ (as seen earlier in Chapter III) is $^{64}/_{81}$ and that of 'c' = $^3/_4$. Therefore, the relationship of ♮ : diesis = $^{64}/_{81}$ : $^{16}/_{21}$ = $\frac{81}{64} \times \frac{16}{21} = \frac{27}{28}$ and diesis : c = $^{16}/_{21} : ^3/_4 = \frac{21}{16} \times \frac{3}{4} = \frac{63}{64}$; thus ♮ : diesis : c = 243 : 252 : 256. The diesis $^{27}/_{28}$ developed here by the <term>interpolator</term> <note>A later editor or commentator who inserted these explanations into the original text.</note> is thus by no means equal to the minor semitone, as the previous citation of Engelbert of Admont teaches; nor is it equal to the diesis of the ancients (i.e., the smaller half of the semitone, 499 : 512), for the semitone $^{243}/_{256}$ divided arithmetically gives the ratios 486 : 499 : 512, while our semitone ♮—c, divided by the diesis developed here, is split into the ratios 486 : 504 : 512. The explanation provided here by our interpolator is therefore highly noteworthy. He teaches first that the <term>subductio</term> <note>original: "subductio"; a term for "drawing under" or lowering, used here as a technical term for the diesis.</note> or diesis is an upward pull of the lower tone toward the higher one, specifically by the ratio 27 : 28, which only then produces the raised <term>leading tone</term> <note>A note that creates a strong musical pull toward the note above it (e.g., C-sharp pulling toward D).</note> (C-sharp—D) used in practice in the ratio of 224 : 243.

It must, therefore, be a matter of the singer's skill to know in what place or manner he should begin any <term>neume</term> <note>A symbol representing a musical note or group of notes in medieval notation.</note>, so that he may either restore it to its proper mode, or if a change is needed, seek out the related pitches. Moreover, we call these modes or tropes in Greek: <term>protus</term>, <term>deuterus</term>, <term>tritus</term>, and <term>tetrardus</term> <note>These are the four primary classifications of the medieval church modes, corresponding to modes on D, E, F, and G.</note>.

# Chapter XI. ¹)

## Which note, and why it holds the mastery in song.

While any song may be composed of various notes and modes²), it is the note that ends the song that holds the mastery³); for it sounds both longer and more deliberately⁴). And the preceding notes,⁵) which are clear only to the practiced, are so adapted to it that...

---

¹) Namely, below F stands E, which forms only a semitone with F. A whole tone below F would be our modern "E-flat," which does not exist in the <term>Choral system</term> <note>The traditional system of Gregorian chant notation and scales.</note>. This would therefore be an example of what was criticized at the beginning of the chapter as "beginning in a place that does not admit the note" <note>original: "inchoant in loco, qui vocem non recipit."</note>. —
²) Manuscript 14965 b does not include the words from "wherever" up to "and therefore," and thus reads: "and so it happens that the end of the same Communion arrives in a place where there is no note."
³) That is, they place the occurring semitone below F; because they have intoned the passage a whole tone too low, this has become E-flat, so that the semitone <term>mi—fa</term> <note>The standard syllables used in solmization to denote a half-step or semitone.</note> now actually sounds as D—E-flat. This should not be, as such a semitone D—E-flat does not exist in chant; in this way, they also end on the pitch E-flat with the final note F (*fa*), which does not exist in the system of chant.

...should begin, a whole tone lower, even though no whole tone step exists below **F**¹); thus it happens that ²) wherever a semitone occurs, they place it below **F**³), which can in no way be done, and accordingly, the conclusion of the entire <term>Communion</term> <note>A chant sung during the distribution of the Eucharist.</note> lands where no note exists. Therefore, the singer must know from practical experience on which step and in what manner he must intone each musical phrase in order to set it in its correct <term>mode</term> <note>original: "Tonart" — the medieval system of organizing scales.</note>, or, if a <term>transposition</term> <note>Shifting a piece of music to a different pitch level than originally written.</note> is necessary, to transfer it accurately to the related tones.

## Chapter XI.¹)

### Which note in the song maintains the primacy, and why.

Although every song is formed from all the various notes and <term>intervals</term> ²), the note that closes the song nonetheless maintains the primacy³); for this note sounds longer and more persistently ⁴). Even the preceding notes ⁵) are related to it in such a way that

²) <note>Since there are only seven essentially different notes, but every chant in the <term>Chorale</term> (Gregorian chant) has an octave range, it can indeed be said that every song contains all the notes and the intervals contained within them.</note>

¹) <note>original: "d. h. alle Töne..." — That is, all notes, even during a song, are understood by trained musicians (and by others as well, though more unconsciously) in a constant relationship to the final note or tonic, in which relationship the notes always present themselves in a peculiar light. For example, the same note **a** (*la*) appears in a different light if the final or tonic note of the song is **D**, and in another if it is **E** or **F**. In the first case (the Dorian mode), the trained singer always perceives the note **a** as a fifth; in the Phrygian, as a fourth; in the Lydian, as a third; which gives it a different "coloring" or "physiognomy" each time.</note>

<meta>This page continues the argument that the final note of a chant is the most important element for determining its musical mode. It transitions from discussing how a melody begins to how its conclusion defines its structure and relationship to other liturgical elements like psalms.</meta>

...[semi]tones <note>The text likely begins mid-word with "semitonia."</note> or other species follow, we do not know. But when the song is finished, we clearly recognize the mode from the final note. For when a song begins, you are ignorant of what follows; but when it is finished,¹) you see what has preceded. Therefore, the final note is that which we may better contemplate. Furthermore, if you wish to join a verse, a psalm, or anything else to that same chant, it is most necessary to adapt it to the final note, rather than returning to inspect the first note or any others.

To this is also added the fact that skillfully composed chants place their <term>distinctions</term> <note>original: "distinctiones"—these are musical pauses or cadences that mark the end of a phrase.</note> mostly upon the final note. Nor is it a wonder that music takes its rules from the final note, since even in the study of **Grammar**, we discern the force of meaning almost everywhere in the final letters or syllables through cases, numbers, persons, and tenses. Therefore, because "all praise is sung at the end,"²) we rightly say that every chant is subject to its mode in this way, and takes its rule from that mode which sounds last.³) From the final note, therefore, a descent to the fifth⁴) and an ascent to the octave is considered proper in any chant, although it often happens against this rule that we may proceed to the ninth, tenth, or eleventh note. For this reason, they established the final notes as **D, E, F,** and **G**, because the layout of the <term>monochord</term> <note>A one-stringed ancient instrument used to teach the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note> first provided the aforementioned ascent or descent to these; for these notes have one <term>tetrachord</term> <note>A scale of four notes.</note> of "low" notes below them, and two of "high" notes above them.⁵)

³) Manuscript 9921 reads "which also sounds last."

⁴) This likely does not mean down to the lower fifth, but to the note which is to be considered the fifth in the respective mode. ...heard, because we do not know whether whole tones or semitones or the other types of intervals follow. But when the song is finished, then the <term>mode</term> <note>The medieval precursor to the modern musical key; a system of organizing scales.</note> of the last tone becomes perfectly clear to us from what has preceded. For at the beginning of the song, one does not know what follows, but at the conclusion ¹) one sees what has gone before. Therefore, it is the final tone that we must consider more closely. Furthermore: if one wishes to add a verse or psalm or anything else to this song, it is necessary above all to link it to the final tone, and not so much to turn one's gaze back to the first or other tones.

It must also be added that carefully composed songs mostly let even the smaller sections end on the final tone. Moreover, it cannot appear surprising that music derives its rules from the final tone, since in grammar, too, the decisive meaning is recognized in the last letters and syllables according to case, number, person, and tense. Thus we rightly say—since "all praise is indeed sung at the end" ²)—that every song belongs to the mode and takes its regular form from that mode which sounds at the conclusion ³). Now, in every song, the regular descent is from the fundamental tone to the fifth ⁴) and the ascent to the octave, although one often ascends to the ninth, tenth, and eleventh in contradiction to this rule. Therefore, the tones **D, E, F, G** are established as the final tones, because their position on the <term>monochord</term> <note>A single-stringed instrument used to measure musical intervals and teach theory.</note> allows the prescribed rise and fall to these first; for these have the <term>tetrachord</term> <note>A series of four notes; the building block of medieval scales.</note> of the low notes below them and the two tetrachords of the high notes above them ⁵).

## Chapter XII.
### On the division of the four modes into eight.

---

Meanwhile, since the songs of a single <term>mode</term> <note>The medieval precursor to the modern musical key; a system of organizing scales.</note>—such as the <term>protus</term> <note>Greek for "first." In this system, Protus corresponds to the notes ending on D.</note>—when compared to their final note, are sometimes low and level¹) and at other times sharp and high, it followed that verses and psalms (and whatever else needed to be fitted to the final note, as we have said) could not be adapted to different songs if they were all produced in one and the same way. For if the part that was added was low, it did not harmonize with the high notes; if it was high, it clashed with the low notes.

Therefore, the plan was that each mode should be divided into two: namely, the high and the low. With rules being distributed, high parts would agree with high notes, and low parts with low notes. Thus, each high mode would be called **authentic**, <note>original: "autenticus"</note> meaning authoritative and principal; while the low mode would be called **plagal**, <note>original: "plaga"</note> meaning collateral and lesser. For he who is said to "stand at my side" is lesser than I; whereas if he were greater, it would be more fitting to say I stood at his side.

When we speak, then, of the **authentic protus** and the **plagal protus**, and similarly for the rest, the four modes that were naturally divided by their pitches were made into eight for the purpose of singing. However, common usage <note>original: "abusio," literally "misuse" or "improper habit," referring to how names change over time.</note> has led the Latins to say "first and second" instead of authentic and plagal *protus*; "third and fourth" instead of authentic and plagal <term>deuterus</term>; <note>Greek for "second," ending on E.</note> "fifth and sixth" instead of authentic and plagal <term>tritus</term>; <note>Greek for "third," ending on F.</note> and "seventh and eighth" instead of authentic and plagal <term>tetrardus</term>. ²) <note>Greek for "fourth," ending on G.</note>

---

¹) That is, a portion of the chants move with their melody below the fundamental tone (low) and do not move far from it at all (uniformly), moving instead around it. —

<summary>This page explains the historical evolution from four Greek-named pitch categories to the eight-mode system of medieval chant, distinguishing between higher "authentic" and lower "plagal" versions of each mode.</summary>
<keywords>modes, authentic, plagal, protus, deuterus, tritus, tetrardus, church music theory, chant range</keywords>

# Chapter XII.

## On the Division of the Four Modes into Eight.

Meanwhile, because songs of one and the same <term>mode</term> <note>A system of melodic organization, similar to a modern musical key but based on different scales.</note>—such as the first—when compared to their final note <note>original: "Schluß"; the concluding pitch that defines the mode.</note> are at times low and uniform,¹) and at others high and wide-reaching, it was not possible for verses and psalms (or whatever else, as previously stated, was to be joined to the end) to be added to these various chants in one and the same manner of performance. For if the part that was added was low, it did not fit with the high chants; if it was high, it stood in contradiction with the low ones.

It was therefore advisable to divide each mode into two: namely, into a high one and a low one. According to certain rules, the high was brought into harmony with the high, and the low with the low. Each high mode was designated as **authentic**, <note>original: "authentische," from the Greek for "authoritative."</note> that is, the original and principal mode; while the low one was designated as **plagal**, <note>original: "plagalische," from the Greek for "lateral" or "sideways."</note> that is, a collateral and subordinate mode. For of him whom one says stands "at my side," he is lesser than I; otherwise, if he were greater, it would be better to say that I stood at his side.

Since one must therefore say: "first authentic mode" and "subordinate mode of the first," and similarly for the others, the modes—which are naturally divided into four according to their pitches—become eight in the chants. A misuse <note>original: "Mißbrauch"; the author refers to the common Latin naming convention as an "abuse" of the original Greek-derived logic.</note> however, led the Latins to say, instead of "first authentic" and "first plagal" tone, "first" and "second" tone; instead of "second authentic" and "second plagal," "third" and "fourth"; instead of "third authentic" and "third plagal," "fifth" and "sixth"; and instead of "fourth authentic" and "fourth plagal," the "seventh" and "eighth tone"²).

---

²) In his *Letter on Unknown Chant* <note>original: "Epistola de ignoto cantu"</note>, <term>Guido</term> <note>Guido of Arezzo (c. 991–1033), a monk and highly influential music theorist who developed modern staff notation.</note> designates the subdivision of the four principal modes <note>original: "modi"</note> as **formulas of the modes** <note>original: "formulae modorum"</note> (stating, "therefore you have two formulas in each mode"), which would also be called the **eight tones** <note>original: "octo toni"</note>.

<summary>This page explains the evolution of music theory from four basic modes to the system of eight church tones. It defines the hierarchical relationship between "authentic" (high) and "plagal" (low) modes, noting how common Latin usage simplified their names into the numbered tones 1 through 8.</summary>
<keywords>music theory, authentic modes, plagal modes, church tones, Guido of Arezzo, medieval chant, octo toni, formulae modorum</keywords>
