# Chapter XIII. <note>Manuscript 9921 labels this as Chapter X.</note>
## On the recognition of the eight modes, their highness, and their lowness.

Therefore, there are eight modes, just as there are eight parts of speech and eight forms of beatitude. Through these modes, every song <note>original: "cantilena"; refers to any melodic composition or chant.</note>—as it ranges about—is varied by eight different sounds and qualities. To distinguish these modes within chants, certain melodic formulas <note>original: "neumae"; in this context, these are specific mnemonic melodies used to identify a mode's character, often associated with the "Noeane" syllables.</note> were devised. From the "fit" of these melodies, we recognize the mode of a song, just as we often identify which tunic belongs to whom by how it fits a person's body: for example:

->D a c &nbsp; a &nbsp; a &nbsp; G F E &nbsp; G F E &nbsp; D D &nbsp; C F G a G a G F E F G a G F E F E D<-
->*First seek the kingdom of God* <note>original Latin: "Primum quaerite regnum Dei." This text was commonly used as a mnemonic for the first mode.</note><-

For as soon as we see that this melodic formula fits well with the end of some <term>antiphon</term> <note>A short chant sung before and after a psalm.</note>, there is no need to doubt that it belongs to the <term>authentic protus</term> <note>The first mode (Mode 1). "Protus" is the Greek word for "first."</note>; and so it is with the rest of the modes.

To recognize this, the verses of the nightly <term>responsories</term> <note>Chants where a soloist alternates with a choir, used in the night office of Matins.</note> and the psalms of the <term>offices</term> <note>The daily cycle of prayer services.</note> are also very useful, and all...

---

<note>
1. As noted above, Manuscript 9921 identifies this as Chapter X.
2. "Every song" refers to everything that is sung. "Ranges about" (discurro) means to move in all directions or across the scale.
3. The meaning is likely that all song is composed of the eight different tones (A B C D E F G) in their various qualities (low, high, and very high). It receives its specific nuance within a piece of music by moving within one of the eight modes. Just as previously mentioned, every note receives its own "color" through its relationship to the fundamental tone; this color changes whenever the fundamental tone or the mode itself changes.
4. These short melodies, found in musical guidebooks called "tonaries," were specifically created for this purpose and do not usually appear in the actual liturgical chants.
</note>

# Chapter XIII. <note>Manuscript 9921 identifies this as Chapter X.</note>
## On the recognition of the eight modes and their range in height and depth.

There are, therefore, eight modes, just as there are eight parts of speech <note>A reference to classical grammar, which was a standard part of the medieval education system (the Trivium).</note> and eight types of beatitudes. Within these, every song <note>original: "Gesang"; likely referring to Gregorian chant melodies.</note> moves and is nuanced by eight different tones and unique characteristics. To distinguish these modes within musical compositions, certain melodic formulas <note>original: "Tonsätze"; these are mnemonic melodies, often called 'neumae', used to teach the 'sound' or 'flavor' of a mode.</note> were invented. By observing how well a chant connects to these formulas, one can recognize its mode, just as one often sees from how a garment fits the body to whom it belongs. For example:

<note>This chant notation depicts a melody for identifying the first mode.</note>
->First seek the kingdom of God. <note>original Latin: "Primum quaerite regnum Dei."</note><-

For just as one sees that this melodic formula connects well with the ending of any <term>antiphon</term> <note>A short chant sung before or after a psalm.</note>, one can immediately know without any doubt that the antiphon belongs to the first <term>authentic mode</term> <note>The "authentic" modes are those with a higher range, starting on the final note of the scale.</note>; the same applies to the remaining modes. For this recognition, the verses of the <term>responsories</term> <note>A type of chant where a soloist and choir alternate.</note> of the <term>Nocturns</term> <note>The night-time services of the Divine Office.</note> and the psalms in the <term>Offices</term> <note>The daily cycle of prayer services in the church.</note> are also very suitable.

---

<note>Footnote 5:</note> Manuscript 14965 b gives the example as follows:
| D a c a | a G | E F | G F E | D | D C F G a G F E E G a G E F E D |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| First | seek | - the | king- | dom | of God. |
Manuscript 9921 records the melody on lines <note>Refers to staff notation, which was a significant innovation in the 11th century.</note> (see the handwritten supplement). In his *Epistle on Unknown Chant*, <term>Guido</term> <note>Guido of Arezzo (c. 991–1033), a hugely influential music theorist who developed the staff and the precursor to modern "do-re-mi" syllables.</note> gives the same with a minor variation.

<note>Footnote 6:</note> Namely, because the phrase "First seek" <note>original: "Primum quaerite"</note> itself belongs to the first authentic mode.

<note>Footnote 7:</note> For the other modes as well, such melodic formulas are found in the <term>tonary</term> <note>A book used to categorize chants by their musical mode.</note> of Saint Bernhard, in the tonary of Berno, and others. For example:
- For the **II. Tone**: "The second is like unto this." <note>original: *Secundum autem simile est huic*</note>
- For the **III. Tone**: "It is the third day since these things were done." <note>original: *Tertia dies est quod haec facta sunt*</note> (see Chapter XI above).
- For the **IV. Tone**: "At the fourth watch He came to them." <note>original: *Quarta vigilia venit ad eos*</note>
- For the **V. Tone**: "Five wise virgins entered into the wedding." <note>original: *Quinque prudentes intraverunt ad nuptias*</note>
- For the **VI. Tone**: "At the sixth hour He sat upon the well." <note>original: *Sexta hora sedit super puteum*</note>
- For the **VII. Tone**: "There are seven spirits before the throne of God." <note>original: *Septem sunt spiritus ante thronum Dei*</note>
- For the **VIII. Tone**: "There are eight beatitudes." <note>original: *Octo sunt beatitudines*</note>

## Chapter XIV.
### On tropes and the power of music <gloss>4</gloss>.

Through practice, some are so accustomed to the use of these tropes that they recognize their unique qualities and distinct "faces"—if I may call them that—as soon as they hear them. This is [just as] an expert [on nations] can [observe] many people standing before him and say: this man is Greek, that one a Spaniard, this one a Latin, and that one a German; this one, however, is a Gaul.

The <term>plagal modes</term> <note>The "lower" versions of the musical scales (Modes 2, 4, 6, and 8).</note>, however, rise and fall to the interval of a fifth <gloss>1: From the treatise *On the Unknown Chant* <note>original: *de ignoto cantu*</note>, after providing the aforementioned musical patterns for recognizing modes: "Thus you can also recognize if you have sung a melodic phrase correctly if you find that it can be appropriately joined to melodies of the mode in which it is recorded."</gloss>. In terms of height, however, weightier authorities also grant them the sixth and even the seventh, just as the ninth and tenth are granted to the <term>authentic modes</term> <note>The "higher" versions of the musical scales (Modes 1, 3, 5, and 7).</note>. The plagal forms of the <term>Protus, Deuterus, and Tritus</term> <note>Greek-derived terms for the first, second, and third categories of modes, which are built on the notes D, E, and F respectively.</note> (Modes II, IV, and VI) are sometimes, by necessity, closed on the high notes a, b-natural, and c. —

The above-mentioned rules should be observed with particular care in <term>antiphons</term> and <term>responsories</term> <note>Two main types of chant in the church service; antiphons are usually refrains for psalms, while responsories are more elaborate choral answers.</note>, whose melodies must be based on these standard rules so that psalms and verses can be properly connected to them. For the rest, one finds many chants in which height and depth are so mingled that it cannot be determined to which mode—that is, the authentic or plagal form—they more likely belong. Furthermore, we are significantly supported in the search for unknown chants <gloss>2: This refers to the singer's ability to interpret written notation independently without a teacher's help.</gloss> by drawing upon the previously mentioned musical patterns and relationships; by identifying these, we can clearly perceive the specific character of each mode resulting from its characteristic structure <gloss>3: The word *vis* means "the effect" and *tropicus* means "pertaining to a turn"; thus, the effect resulting from the peculiar "turn" or melodic shape of the song—its characteristic structure.</gloss>. A <term>trope</term> <note>original: *tropus*</note>, however, is a specific quality of a song, which was also designated as a <term>mode</term> <note>original: *modus*</note>, about which we must still speak.

<meta>This page transitions from the technical classification of melodic ranges to a discussion on the psychological and physiological effects of music, specifically how different musical modes (tropes) appeal to different human temperaments.</meta>

And so the diversity of <term>tropes</term> <note>Also known as modes; these are the specific musical scales used in medieval chant, each thought to possess a unique emotional or moral character.</note> is matched to the diversity of minds, so that one person is delighted by the broken leaps of the <term>authentic deuterus</term> <note>Mode 3 (based on the note E), historically described as having a serious or even inciting character.</note>; another chooses the pleasure of the <term>plagal tritus</term> <note>Mode 6 (based on the note F), typically associated with devotion and gentleness.</note>; to one, the talkativeness of the <term>authentic tetrardus</term> <note>Mode 7 (based on the note G), often characterized as energetic or cheerful.</note> is more pleasing; another approves of the sweetness of its plagal form <note>Mode 8.</note>; and so on for the rest.

Nor is it a wonder if the hearing is delighted by a variety of sounds, since sight rejoices in a variety of colors, smell is nourished by a variety of scents, and the tongue delights in changing flavors. For in this way, through the window of the body, the sweetness of delightful things enters marvelously into the inner chambers of the heart. From this it follows that just as by certain flavors, colors, and smells—or even by the sight of colors—the health of both heart and body is either diminished or increased. Thus formerly, as it is read, a certain madman was recalled from insanity while <term>Asclepiades</term> ¹) the physician was singing <note>original: "Asclepiade." This refers to the ancient Greek belief in "musical therapy."</note>. And likewise, another man was incited to such great lust by the sweetness of the <term>cithara</term> <note>An ancient Greek stringed instrument similar to a lyre.</note> that, driven mad, he sought to break into a girl's bedroom; but soon, when the <term>citharœdus</term> <note>A performer who sings while playing the kithara.</note> changed the mode, he was led to repentance and withdrew in confusion. Likewise, David also used to soothe the demon of Saul with the <term>cithara</term>, and he would break the demonic ferocity by the powerful force and sweetness of this art. However, the full extent of this power is known only to divine wisdom. But let us use those things which we have perceived from there "through a glass darkly" <note>original: "in ænigmate," a biblical reference to 1 Corinthians 13:12, suggesting a partial or veiled understanding of divine truths.</note> in the performance of divine praises. But because we have tasted only a few small things concerning the power of this art, let us see what things are needed for singing well <note>original: "modulandum," referring to the art of measuring or performing music correctly.</note>.

---

¹) Asclepiades, a famous physician from Prusa in Bithynia. (Cicero, *On the Orator* 1, 14, 62.)

The Frenchman: <note>Likely referring to a specific authority or theorist of the French tradition being cited.</note> furthermore, the diversity of the **modes** <note>original: "Tropen"; refers to the church modes or scales.</note> corresponds to the diversity of temperaments in such a way that one person finds pleasure in the broken leaps of the **authentic Deuterus** (the 3rd Mode); another prefers the bliss of the **plagal Tritus** (the 6th Mode); to one, the "talkativeness" of the **authentic Tetrardus** (the 7th Mode) is more pleasing; another declares himself for the sweetness of its **plagal form** (the 8th Mode); and so it is with the remaining modes.

From this it comes that through certain delicacies, mixtures of colors, and sweet smells—and even through the close observation of colors—the well-being of both the spirit and the body is either diminished or increased. 

Thus it is said that once, as we read, a madman was healed of his frenzy by the singing of the physician **Asclepiades** ¹). <note>An ancient Greek physician famous for using music as a medical treatment.</note> Likewise, another man was so inflamed to carnal lust by the sweetness of playing the **cithara** <note>An ancient stringed instrument similar to a lyre.</note> that he, out of his mind, wanted to break into a girl's chamber; but soon, when the cithara player changed the voluptuous melody, he is said to have departed in shame, driven by remorse. In a similar way, **David** also soothed the evil spirit of **Saul** with the cithara and broke the demonic rage through the mighty power and sweetness of this art. 

This power, however, is fully revealed only to Divine Wisdom. But we wish to use what we have so far grasped only in an enigmatic way for the praise of God²). Since, however, we have tasted only a tiny bit of the influence of this art, let us see what is necessary for the creation of a good melody.

## Chapter XV.

### On the proper arrangement of melody. ¹)

<note>original: "modulatio"; from the word *modulor*, meaning "proportioned measure" or "symmetry."</note>

Therefore, just as in poetry there are letters and syllables, parts, feet, and verses, so too in harmony there are **phthongi** <term>original: "phthongi"; the Greek term for musical tones or individual notes.</term>, that is, sounds. One, two, or three of these sounds are joined together into "syllables." These syllables, either alone or doubled, constitute a **neume** <term>original: "neuma"; the basic melodic unit or "breath" of a chant, representing a single musical gesture.</term>, which is a part of the song. One or more of these parts then create a **distinction** <term>original: "distinctio"; a musical phrase or cadence that marks a logical ending point.</term>, which provides a suitable place for breathing. 

Regarding these elements, it should be noted that the entire "part" must be noted and performed with a certain compression, the syllable even more compressed, but the **tenor** ²) <note>original: "tenor"; usually meaning a continuous flow, but here used in the sense of a "hold" or a sustained duration of the voice.</note>—which is the lingering on the final note—is very slight in a syllable, larger in a part, and longest in a distinction. This sustain acts as a signpost for these divisions. 

Thus, it is necessary that a song be measured out as if by poetic feet ³), and that some notes have a **little pause** ⁴) <note>original: "morula"; a brief delay or rest.</note> twice as long or twice as short as others, or even a **trembling** effect ⁵) <note>original: "tremula"; likely referring to a vocal ornament or vibrato.</note>. This indicates a varying sustain, which is sometimes signified as long by a horizontal line placed over a letter. Great care must be taken in the distribution of neumes, so that whether the neumes are formed by the repetition of the same sound or by the connection of two or more different sounds, they always correspond to one another, either in the number of notes or in the proportion of the tones.

---

¹) **Proportioned measure:** from the word *modulor*, referring to symmetry and balance in composition.
²) **Tenor:** While this usually means "uninterrupted flow," here it should be taken in the sense of a "hold" or "halt." This halt should be as minimal as possible after a syllable—consisting of a nearly imperceptible pause without taking a breath, since a musical member consisting of several syllables should be performed as a cohesive unit. At the end of a "member" (part), the hold is more significant; it is longest and combined with a free breath at the "incisions" (distinctions), which, as previously stated, are the most appropriate places to breathe. We have, therefore, a three-fold division of pauses.

<summary>This page establishes a structural theory of music by comparing it to the components of grammar and poetry, defining the hierarchy of notes, syllables, neumes, and phrases. It introduces the concept of the 'tenor' or sustained note as a way to mark the ends of musical phrases and manage breathing.</summary>

<keywords>melody, neumes, distinction, tenor, musical rhythm, phthongi, breathing in chant, poetic feet in music</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical theory, moving from the emotional effects of the modes (discussed on the previous page) to the structural rules of how a melody should be composed, using the analogy of grammar and poetry.</meta>

# Chapter XV.
## On the Correct Proportion of a Melody ¹).

<note>The German "Ebenmaß" suggests symmetry, balance, or proportion in the construction of a musical line.</note>

Just as in poetic meter there are letters and syllables, parts, feet, and verses, so too in song there are sounds—that is, tones—of which one, two, or three are joined into syllables; these syllables, either individually or doubled, form a <term>neume</term> <note>original: "Neuma"; the basic melodic unit or "breath" of a chant.</note>, which is a member of the melody; and one or more members make a <term>phrase</term> <note>original: "Einschnitt," literally a "cut" or "incision," referring to a musical cadence.</note>, which is a suitable place to take a breath. Regarding this, it should be noted that a whole member is to be written and performed as a cohesive unit, but the syllable even more tightly. The **hold** ²)—that is, the pausing on the final tone, which is as slight as possible for a syllable, more significant for a member, and longest for a phrase—serves as a distinguishing mark for these divisions. 

Thus, it is also necessary that the melody be beaten or conducted ³) as if according to poetic feet, and that some tones receive a duration ⁴) twice as long or twice as short as others, or a <term>tremolo</term> ⁵) (vibrato)—that is, a different temporal value. A long duration is often indicated by a small horizontal line placed above the letter. Particular care must be taken in the distribution of the neumes, so that these neumes—which are formed either by the repetition of a single tone or by the joining of two or more tones—always stand in a mutual relationship, either in the number of tones or in the proportion of the tones...

---

corresponding to the today common terms <term>sigh</term> <note>original: "suspiratio"</note>, <term>breathing</term> <note>original: "respiratio"</note>, and <term>pause</term> <note>original: "pausa"</note>; the first should make the individual syllables distinguishable from one another through a slight lift of the voice without taking a breath; the second highlights the larger members against one another; the third separates the larger sections from each other.
³) To strike, clap, or beat with the hands or feet; thus, here, to indicate the beat or tempo.
⁴) <term>brief delay</term> <note>original: "morula," the diminutive of "mora," a delay</note>; this indicates that the duration serving as the unit of measure should be short, so that the tempo is not dragging.
⁵) <term>trembling</term> <note>original: "tremulam"</note> refers to the "brief delay" (<term>morula</term>); that is, a stretching of the tone combined with a quivering or <term>tremolo</term> of the voice, whereby the held tone gains a certain weight and seemingly increases in length because of its emphasis.

<summary>This page explains the structural analogy between music and grammar, defining how tones form syllables, neumes, and phrases. It also introduces the concept of musical "holds" and rhythmic measurement, likening musical conducting to poetic meter.</summary>
<keywords>neume, phrase, rhythm, musical meter, conducting, vibrato, breathing, musical structure</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page concludes the sentence regarding the rhythmic and proportional balance of musical units (neumes) and provides diagrams illustrating the Pythagorean mathematical ratios that underlay medieval music theory.</meta>

...and correspond to one another, sometimes equal to equal, sometimes double or triple to single units, and at other times in a <term>sesquialtera</term> <note>original: "sesquialtera"; a mathematical ratio of 3:2, where the larger value contains the smaller value plus half of it again.</note> or <term>sesquitertia</term> <note>original: "sesquitertia"; a ratio of 4:3, where the larger value contains the smaller value plus one-third of it again.</note> relationship.

### On the Comparison of Proportions ¹).

The musician should consider in advance in which of these **divisions** he should let the song proceed, just as the **versifier** considers in which feet he should form the verse; only with the difference that the musician does not bind himself to the law with such great severity, because on the whole, this art permits a **rational variety** in the arrangement of tones ¹). <note>original: "vernünftige Abwechselung." <meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical structure, moving from the philosophical justification of "rational variety" to the specific arrangement of melodic motifs and phrases. ...these or all **neumes** <note>original: "neumae." In this context, melodic motifs or short musical figures.</note>, since they sometimes begin from the same pitch and at other times from different ones, according to various qualities of lowering and sharpening. Likewise, almost all **distinctions** <note>original: "distinctiones." The structural phrases or "clauses" of a musical composition.</note> should flow toward the principal tone—that is, the <term>final</term> <note>The concluding pitch of a mode, acting as a tonal home.</note>—or whatever related tone they have chosen in its place <note>The word 'tes' in the original is a printer's error for 'est'.</note>. Sometimes the very same tone that ends all the neumes may also end or even begin several distinctions, as the diligent student will be able to find in the works of **Ambrose** <note>St. Ambrose (c. 340–397), the Bishop of Milan associated with the development of Ambrosian chant.</note>.

There are, however, what might be called **prosaic songs**, which observe these rules less strictly. In these, there is no concern if some parts and distinctions are found to be larger and others smaller, scattered throughout the piece without specific arrangement, in the manner of prose.

I call other songs **metrical**, however, because we often sing them in such a way that we seem to scan them with "feet," <note>Rhythmic units used in poetry to measure verse.</note> just as occurs when we sing actual poetic meters. In these songs, one must take care not to continue two-syllable neumes excessively without a mixture of three-syllable and four-syllable ones. For just as the **Lyric poets** joined various feet together, so too those who compose a song should arrange rationally distinct and diverse neumes.

A truly **rational distinction** exists when there is such a moderate variety of neumes and distinctions that they correspond to one another with a certain similarity—that is, a "dissimilar similarity" <note>original: "similitudo dissimilis." A rhetorical term suggesting a balance where elements are recognizable as related but not identical.</note> in the style of the most sweet **Ambrose**. Indeed, there is no small similarity between poetic meters and songs, since neumes take the place of feet and distinctions take the place of verses. Thus, one neume might run in a **dactylic** <note>A foot consisting of one long syllable followed by two short ones.</note> meter, another in a **spondaic** <note>A foot consisting of two long syllables.</note>, and another in an **iambic** <note>A foot consisting of one short syllable followed by one long one.</note> meter; and a distinction might be at one time a tetrameter, at another a pentameter, and at others as if a hexa-

one syllable may have one or more **neumes** <note>original: "Neumen." Melodic figures or groups of notes sung to a single syllable.</note>, and sometimes one neume is divided across several syllables. But I speak of **metrical songs** because we often sing in such a way that we seem to scan verses by **feet** <note>original: "Füße." For just as lyric poets have joined together now these feet and now others, so too those who compose a song assemble rationally selected and even completely different neumes. The selection is "rational" when there is a moderate variety of neumes and sections, such that the neumes still harmoniously correspond to neumes and sections to sections through a certain likeness—that is, that there exists a **dissimilar similarity** <note>original: "unähnliche Aehnlichkeit." A translation of the Latin "similitudo dissimilis," a principle where beauty is found in things that are balanced and symmetrical but not identical.</note> after the manner of the most delightful Ambrose. ---

...you will perceive meter and many other things, such as how the **elevation** and **position** <note>original: "elevatio et positio." These terms correspond to the upward and downward movements or stresses in music and poetry, often called *arsis* and *thesis*.</note> are placed—now similar to themselves, now one similar or dissimilar to another—whether placed before, under, next to, or between; sometimes joined together, sometimes separated, and sometimes mixed in this manner¹). In this context, it refers to the duration or time-value held for a syllable or note.</note> should not be used on short syllables, nor a short one on long syllables, for this produces a lack of harmony <note>original: "obscoenitatem." Meaning low, heavy, or serious.</note>; for peaceful things, they should be pleasant²); for prosperous things, exultant, and so on. Also, we often apply a low and high accent to the notes, because we frequently sing some with a greater impulse and others with a lesser one; indeed, the repetition of the same pitch often seems to be an ascent or a descent. Likewise, just as with a **galloping horse** <note>original: "currentis equi." A famous medieval metaphor for musical phrasing: a horse slows down as it approaches its destination; similarly, music should slow down at the end of a phrase.</note>, the notes should approach the point of breath more slowly at the end of sections, so that they arrive like weary travelers in a dignified manner to rest. Whether notes are grouped closely or spaced apart as required, these composite notes can often provide a sign of this. In many places, the notes **liquefy** <note>original: "liquescunt." Refers to liquescence, a vocal technique where one note glides into the next, typically occurring on specific "liquid" consonants like 'l', 'm', or 'n'.</note> in the manner of letters, so that the beginning of one note passes clearly into the next and does not seem to end³). Furthermore, we mark a liquescent note with a dot, as if spotting it, in this way:

->G DE Ga a G<-

# Chapter XVI.
## On the Manifold Variety of Sounds and Neumes.

Indeed, it should not seem surprising why such a great abundance of diverse songs is formed from so few **pitches** <term>vox (plural: voces): literally "voice," but in a musical context referring to the individual notes of the scale.</term>, which are joined to one another in no more than six ways, as we have said, through both **elevation** and **depression** <note>The rising and falling of pitch.</note>; just as from a few letters—even if not many—syllables are created (for the number of syllables can be counted), yet an infinite plurality of parts has grown out of those syllables; and in **poetic meters** <term>metrum: the rhythmic structure of verse.</term>, from a few **feet** <note>The basic rhythmic unit in poetry, such as a dactyl or an iamb.</note>, many types of meters are made, and a meter of a single type is found to be varied in many ways, such as the **hexameter** <note>A line of six poetic feet, common in epic poetry.</note>; how this happens, let the grammarians see to, since discussing that belongs to another field of study. Let us, if we can, see in what ways we are able to construct **neumes** <term>neuma: the signs used in medieval musical notation before the modern staff system was fully developed.</term> that are distinct from one another ¹).

Therefore, the **movement of the pitches** <note>original: "motus vocum."</note> ²), which we have said occurs harmoniously in six ways, happens through **arsis and thesis** <term>arsis & thesis: from the Greek for "lifting" and "setting down," referring to the rising and falling of the melody or rhythmic pulse.</term>, that is, by elevation and depression: by whose double movement—that is, arsis and thesis—every neume...

---

<note>¹) This section up to "Therefore, the movement" is missing in the Munich Manuscript (Cod. Mon. 14665).</note>
<note>²) The following is of particular interest insofar as we obtain here a precise insight into the nature of the neumes and can simultaneously recognize how the medieval masters thought, down to the smallest detail, about the construction of a melody.</note>

<summary>This page explores the diversity of melody, comparing the few notes of the musical scale to the few letters of the alphabet that create infinite language. It introduces the concept of arsis and thesis (rising and falling) as the foundation of all musical notation.</summary>
<keywords>neumes, arsis and thesis, pitch, musical theory, grammar analogy, hexameter, melody construction, liquescence</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the technique of "liquefaction" (softening transitions between notes) from Chapter XV and begins Chapter XVI. The author uses a grammatical analogy to explain how a complex musical system is built from a small set of basic intervals, much like how a vast language is built from a few letters and syllables.</meta>

If, however, you wish to provide the tone more fully, without **sliding it over** <note>original: "hinüberzuziehen." This refers to the practice of liquefaction, a subtle vocal technique in chant where a note is shortened or softened to facilitate the pronunciation of certain consonant combinations.</note>, it does no harm; but often this sliding pleases more. Everything we have mentioned so far should be done neither too rarely nor too often, but with careful judgment.

# Chapter XVI.
## On the Manifold Variety of Pitches and Neumes.

It should not surprise us that such a vast quantity of diverse songs is created using so few **pitches** <term>vox (plural: voces): literally "voice," but in medieval music theory, it refers to the individual notes of the scale.</term>, which—as previously stated—are joined together by only six intervals, both in ascending and descending. In the same way, syllables are formed from a few letters, though not in an exceedingly large quantity; for while the number of syllables can be calculated, an infinite number of parts of speech arise from those syllables. In the art of poetry, how many types of verse emerge from just a few **feet** <note>The basic rhythmic units of poetry, such as a dactyl or an iamb.</note>? Even a meter of the same kind presents itself differently through many variations, such as the **hexameter** <note>A classic poetic meter consisting of six feet per line, used in epic poetry like the Iliad or Aeneid.</note>. How this occurs is for the grammarians to consider, as it is their profession to speak on such matters. We, however, shall consider, if possible, the manner in which different **neumes** <term>neuma: the basic symbols of medieval musical notation, representing one or more notes in a melodic gesture.</term> may be composed ¹).

The **movement of pitches** ²), which, as mentioned, can appropriately occur in only six different interval steps, is carried out through **arsis** and **thesis**, that is, through rising and falling. Every neume is formed by this double movement—namely, by **arsis** and **thesis**—except for those that repeat or consist of a single—

---

seeking to give an account of themselves. — “**Movement of pitches**” <note>original Latin: "Motus vocum."</note> or more precisely: “movement of the voice,” since primarily the voice must move when producing the pitches that form a neume. In general, the voice moves in a harmonious <note>original: "consonanter."</note> manner—

...is formed, except for repeated or simple ones <note>original: "præter repercussas aut simplices." Then, **arsis** and **thesis** are joined sometimes to themselves—such as an arsis to an arsis, or a thesis to a thesis—and sometimes one to the other, such as an arsis to a thesis, and a thesis to an arsis. This joining itself occurs sometimes between similar movements and sometimes between dissimilar ones. There is dissimilarity, however, if among the aforementioned motions—that is, the **tones, semitones, major thirds, and the rest** <note>original: "tonis, semitoniis, ditonis & cæteris." Finally, even if the joining is made similarly, one motion will be placed before another, that is, in the higher pitches...

---

In choral chant <note>Specifically Plainchant or Gregorian Chant.</note>, there are only six ways, or six different steps, in which a melody can move: namely, the intervals of the minor and major second, the minor and major third, the fourth, and the fifth, which, as taught earlier, are the only ones that can occur in choral song. Aside from this—that is, aside from the size of the steps—the voice can move in only two directions: namely, upward and downward (**Arsis** and **Thesis**). Because of this, every **neume** <term>neuma: a symbol in medieval notation representing a single pitch or a group of pitches to be sung to a single syllable.</term>, every connection of tones, appears as an arsis or a thesis, unless it consists of a single tone or its repetition. The simplest form of an arsis is the **Podatus** <term>Podatus: a neume representing two notes, where the second is higher than the first.</term> <image-desc>symbol for a podatus neume resembling a thick tick mark or heavy 'J' shape</image-desc>, and the simplest form of a thesis is the **Clivis** <term>Clivis: a neume representing two notes, where the second is lower than the first.</term> <image-desc>symbol for a clivis neume resembling a rounded cap or '∩' shape</image-desc>.

> ¹) The following are to be considered "simple" neumes: the **Punctum** (.) <term>Punctum: a basic neume indicating a single short or lower note.</term>, the **Virga** (/) <term>Virga: a basic neume indicating a single higher or longer note.</term>, and the **Apostrophus** ('), as well as their doubling or tripling on the same pitch: the **Bipunctus** (..), **Bivirga** (//), **Bistrophus** (''), or the **Tripunctus** (...), **trivirga** (///), and **tristrophus** ('''). In these cases, no upward or downward movement of the voice occurs.
>
> ²) The composition of neumes can thus be fourfold: 1. Arsis with Arsis <image-desc>musical notation on a four-line staff showing two ascending square notes</image-desc> 2. Arsis with Thesis <image-desc>musical notation on a four-line staff showing an ascending note followed by a descending note</image-desc> 4. Thesis with Arsis <image-desc>musical notation on a four-line staff showing a descending note followed by an ascending note</image-desc>.
>
> ³) The aforementioned fourfold connection can again vary depending on whether similar or dissimilar elements are combined. ⁶) The term **Praepositus** <note>literally: "placed before" or "set over"</note> (referring to an overseer or superior in rank) is to be understood here in a spatial sense, as shown by the accompanying explanation "placed in the higher parts" <note>original: "in superioribus positus"</note>. This is also seen in Hothby <note>John Hothby (c. 1410–1487), an English composer and theorist who worked in Italy.</note> in his *The Rule of Calliopea* (Section 52): "Placed above according to the intensity, that is, positioned on top," <note>original: "Praeposita secondo la intensione, cio è di sopra posita"</note> which refers to a higher placement when ascending. This also indicates that the second movement stands higher than the first, not the reverse; this is self-evident, as in the ongoing movement of a song, only the later part can be compared with what preceded it. This **elevated movement** (motus praepositus) can, moreover, be applied to each of the four types of connections between the rise and the fall, as well as to the connection of similar and dissimilar motions. For the latter, it follows three viewpoints mentioned later: a. according to high and low <note>original: "secundum laxationis et acuminis"</note>, b. according to the number of notes <note>original: "augmenti et detrimenti," referring to adding or subtracting notes</note>, and c. according to the different types of intervals, i.e., major and minor seconds, thirds, etc. <note>original: "modorum varias qualitates"</note>. Thus:

<meta>This page continues the technical categorization of how melodic movements (rising and falling) are positioned relative to one another in musical notation. i.e., standing in higher tones, now **placed under** ¹), now **placed next to**, i.e., in such a way that the end of one movement and the beginning of the other fall on the same tone ²), now **placed between**, i.e., when one movement is positioned within the range of the other and is both less deep and less high ³); it is sometimes **mixed**, i.e., partly placed between, partly placed under or placed over or placed next to; and again these divisions can be broken down according to the various properties of depth and height ⁴), increase and decrease ⁵), and also the intervals ⁶). The **neumes** <term>neume: the basic element of medieval Western musical notation</term> can also be varied through all these types ⁷) of **rising** <term>arsis: the upward motion of the voice</term> and falling <note>The text cuts off here, but refers to "thesis": the downward motion of the voice.</note>

In this way, one obtains 16 different types of "placed over" movement. —

¹) *suppositus* <note>Latin: "placed under"</note>, "**placed under**," is the opposite of the previous term. (*Subposita secondo la remissione cioè di sotto posita* <note>Italian: "Placed below according to the lowering [of pitch], that is, positioned underneath"</note> (Hothby, *Calliopea* § 52) e.g. <image-desc>Musical notation using square neumes on a four-line staff, showing an ascending and descending melodic line.</image-desc> etc. Here, too, 16 different combinations are possible, as mentioned before.

²) Thus, for example: <image-desc>Musical notation with square neumes on a four-line staff, showing two groups of notes meeting at a common pitch level.</image-desc> etc. Here, too, the mentioned 16 combinations are to be distinguished.

³) e.g. : <image-desc>Musical notation with square neumes on a four-line staff, showing a small group of notes positioned within the vertical range of another group.</image-desc> etc. with 16 different combinations as before.

⁴) Unequal according to height and depth, i.e., that the range of one movement is greater than that of the other, e.g. : <image-desc>Musical notation with square neumes on a four-line staff; the first neume group spans a fifth, the second spans a third.</image-desc> Here the first movement stands in the range of a **fifth** <term>fifth: a musical interval encompassing five staff positions</term>, the second a **third** <term>third: a musical interval encompassing three staff positions</term>. This would therefore be a "placed over" connection of rising with falling in unequal (dissimilar) movement regarding height and depth.

⁵) Unequal in increase and decrease, i.e., that one movement contains more notes than the other, e.g. : <image-desc>Musical notation with square neumes on a four-line staff; the first group contains two notes, the second group contains three notes.</image-desc> The first movement has two notes, the last has three. This is therefore a "placed between" connection of rising with falling in unequal movement of increase and decrease.

⁶) Unequal according to the type of intervals, i.e., that both movements take place within the scope of equal intervals, but differ in their specific type, e.g. : <image-desc>Musical notation with square neumes on a four-line staff showing two-note steps.</image-desc> Both movements are steps of a **second** <term>second: the interval between two adjacent scale notes</term>, but the first is a **major second** <term>major second: a whole-step interval</term>, and the second is a **minor second** <term>minor second: a half-step interval</term>. —

⁷) Codex Munich 14663 reads *per omnes eosdem modos* <note>Latin: "through all the same ways"</note>.

# Chapter XVII.
## That everything which is written is reduced to chant.

Having briefly explained these matters, I will provide you here with another very clear argument, most useful in practice, though unheard of until now. Once the cause of all melodies becomes clear through this method, you will be able to apply what you find useful and, likewise, reject what seems discordant <note>original: "obscœna" (obscene/foul). A scholarly note by Gerbert suggests the reading "absona" (discordant/out of tune)</note>. Consider, therefore, that just as everything that is spoken is written, so everything that is written is reduced to chant. Thus, everything that is spoken is sung, and writing is represented by letters.

But so that our rule is not stretched out too long, let us take only five vowels from *these six letters* <note>original: "sex his de litteris"; the author likely refers to a specific set of letters used in his notation system</note>, without which no other letter or even a syllable can be proven to sound. It is chiefly because of these vowels that a sweet concord is produced whenever harmony is found in different parts—just as we often see in poetry where verses are consonant and respond to one another, so that you might admire a kind of "grammatical symphony." If a similar musical response is joined to this, you will be delighted by a double melody.

Let us therefore take these five vowels; perhaps, since they grant so much concord to words, they will provide no less to songs and **neumes** <term>neume: a medieval musical notation sign representing a melodic turn or a group of notes</term>. Let them be placed in order under the letters of the **monochord** <term>monochord: a single-stringed instrument used in the Middle Ages to teach the mathematical ratios of musical intervals</term>, and because there are only five, let them be repeated until a vowel is subscribed to every tone, in this manner:

---

<summary>In this chapter, the author introduces a pioneering method for generating musical melodies by mapping the five vowels (a, e, i, o, u) to the pitches of the monochord. He compares the structural harmony of language (the "symphony of grammar") to musical concord, suggesting that the natural resonance of vowels can dictate the shape of a chant.</summary>

<keywords>vowels, monochord, neumes, grammatical symphony, melodic composition, concord, Guido of Arezzo, Gerbert, musical notation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the practical instruction of Chapter XVII. # Chapter XVII.
## That everything which is written can be set to song.

After we have briefly fixed the preceding points in our minds, we wish to present to you here another very easily understood subject. This method is highly useful for practical application, even if it has not been made public until now. Since the foundation of all melodies becomes completely clear through this method, you may use from it for your own benefit whatever you consider appropriate; likewise, you may reject whatever appears unsuitable <note>The German translator notes that the Latin original likely meant "discordant" (*absona*) rather than "foul" (*obscœna*).</note> to you. Consider, then, that just as everything that is spoken can be written, so too everything that is written can be set to song. Accordingly, everything that is spoken can now also be sung; and that which is written is represented by letters. 

However, in order not to draw out our instruction too long, we shall take from these letters <note>Gerbert <note>Martin Gerbert (1720–1793) was a Swiss theologian and music historian who published major collections of medieval musical treatises.</note> notes: "Better: from the same letters," which is certainly more correct.</note> only the five vowels, without which no other letter, indeed not even a syllable, can be found to produce a sound. It is primarily through their effect that a lovely harmony is achieved whenever it occurs in various places [in speech], as can very frequently be observed in the rhyming and corresponding verses of metrical poems. One cannot help but marvel at a certain, as it were, harmonic pleasantness of the grammatical form itself. If music joins itself to this in a corresponding way, the listener will feel all the more delighted by the double harmonic effect of the sound.

We will therefore take these five vowels; perhaps, since they lend so much pleasant sound to words, they will offer no less to song and **neumes** <term>neume: a medieval musical notation sign representing a melodic turn or a specific pitch before the modern staff was invented</term>. They shall be placed in sequence beneath the letters of the **monochord** <term>monochord: a single-stringed instrument used by medieval theorists to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of intervals and to teach the notes of the scale</term>, and because there are only five of them, they are repeated until every tone has its vowel assigned to it, in the following manner:

->Saint John, I cannot worthily sing the abundance of your merits.¹)<-
<note>original: "Sancte Joannes meritorum tuorum copias nequeo digne canere." This is the text used for the musical example.</note>

Therefore, what has been done with this prayer can undoubtedly be done with all others. But lest a heavy necessity be imposed upon you—because in this manner five pitches hardly suffice for any symphony <term>symphoniae: here meaning a consonant melody or musical sequence</term>, and it may not be possible to exceed those five according to your wish—I subjoin another row of vowels so that you may wander a little more freely. However, let it be different so that it begins from the third position of the former row, in this manner:

---

<note>
1) In this example, the vowels are already arranged differently than indicated above, where the vowel **o** falls on the note **C**, whereas here the vowel **a** is set to the tone **C**. It is obvious that this arrangement is entirely arbitrary and left to each individual. In any case, it is advisable to place the vowel of the last syllable on the tone one wishes to choose as the final (tonic) tone of the whole, which seems to have been decisive here for the choice of tone **D** for the final vowel **e** in *canere* (to sing). At first glance, the whole matter appears as a pastime unworthy of art, and one might rightly wonder that Guido, the serious artist, would still concern himself with such a game after his discussions in Chapters XV and XVI. However, one must consider that Guido, who was never at a loss for practical tricks wherever it was necessary to advance his students as quickly as possible, also here [provided a tool for composition].
</note>

What has been performed here with this sentence can undoubtedly be done with any other. This row differs from the first in that it begins from the third position of the former, in the following manner:

---

<margin>
The author certainly only intended to lead his students by the shortest possible route to the independent composition of a chant. The means provided here were intended only to come to the aid of their initial clumsiness and helplessness, and to be a support and starting point for the free flight of their musical imagination, but by no means a seriously intended rule for the art of composition in general. Therefore, he says above that a melody found in this way should only provide a certain foundation, from which one can keep or discard what seems good; and in the following, he gives further instructions on how a greater scope for the imagination may be gained, and how later through correction and revision, a chant that is artistically perfect in all respects can be created. — Codex Monacensis 14663 <note>A 15th-century manuscript from the Munich State Library</note> has this example written on its side in another manner, as can be seen in the autograph supplement.
</margin>

Where with two sub-tones <note>lower pitches</note> available everywhere, in which five vowels are held—since indeed one vowel underlies each sound, yet another gives you a sufficiently freer ability—you are granted the power to vary and proceed with either a longer or shorter movement at your own pleasure. From this, let us now see what kind of harmony <note>original: *symphoniam*</note> its vowels have brought to this rhythm.

>*Restraining the tongue, may He temper it, lest the horror of strife sound, by—*

>*—guarding the sight, may He shield it, lest it drink in vanities:*
<note>original Latin: *—sum fovendo contegat, ne vanitates hauriat:*</note>

For we have left this proof for the final part alone, so that we might render the melody fitting to its <term>tetrardus</term> <note>The "fourth" category of musical modes in the medieval system, which typically ends on the note G</note>. Thus, when a certain song <note>original: *cantus*</note> claims for itself a suitable melody <note>original: *cantilenam*</note> based only on its own vowels, there is no doubt that it becomes most fitting if, being well-practiced in many examples, you choose only the better and more aptly corresponding parts from the many options; you should fill in any gaps, loosen what is compressed, shorten what is too long, and stretch what is too short, so that you produce a single, carefully crafted work.

->Γ A B C D E F G  a b ♮ c d e f g  a b ♮ c d<-
<note>This represents the medieval "Gamut" or full range of notes. The Γ (Gamma) is the lowest note, and the ♮ symbol indicates a "hard" B-natural.</note>
->a e i o u. a e i o u. a e i o u. a e i o<-

<note>This repeating sequence shows how the five vowels (a, e, i, o, u) are cycled across the notes of the scale.</note>

Here, a sufficiently free scope is granted to the composer. Since two underlying tones are available for each of the five vowels—given that every note has a second vowel assigned to it alongside the first—one may choose to move and progress between wider or narrower musical intervals as desired. We shall therefore attempt this now and see what kind of melody the vowels of the corresponding verse produce: <note>The following diagram maps the syllables of a Latin hymn to specific pitches based on their vowels.</note>

->**Therefore:**<-

<image-desc>Musical notation for a Gregorian chant on two four-line staves using square neumes and C-clefs. The lyrics underneath the notation are: "Linguam refrenans temperet, ne litis horror insonet, visum fovendo contegat, ne vanitates hauriat."</image-desc>

We have abandoned our rule only in the final part here, in order to keep the melody more in line with its <term>fourth mode</term> <note>Original German: *vierte Tonart*. In medieval music, the "fourth mode" or Hypophrygian mode has a specific emotional character and range.</note> ²). Since every song ³) acquires a more or less fitting melody in this manner simply through its vowels, there is no doubt that the melody will become perfectly appropriate if, after many attempts, one selects only the most excellent and suitable passages from several options. One can then fill in awkward leaps, expand congested sections, contract steps that are too wide, and separate those that are too close together, so that a unified and as near-perfect a work as possible is obtained.

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of vowel-based melodic composition to the psychological reception of music and the technical foundations of early polyphony. It concludes the section on "cantus" (monophonic song) and begins Chapter 18, which introduces "diaphonia" or "organum."</meta>

I also want you to know this: that just like pure silver, every song <note>original: "cantus"</note> becomes more "colored" <note>in this context, "polished" or "refined"</note> the more it is used. What might displease at first, after being polished by use as if by a file, is later highly praised. Because of the diversity of peoples and minds, what displeases one person is embraced by another; one finds delight in harmony <note>original: "consona"</note>, while another prefers diverse sounds. One person seeks continuity and softness according to the playfulness of his mind; another, being more serious, is soothed by sober songs. Yet another, like a man possessed, feeds on complex and winding arrangements <note>original: "in anfractis vexationibus"</note>. Every person sings much more resonantly the song that they approve of according to the natural quality of their own mind. 

You will not be able to remain ignorant of these matters if you stick to the aforementioned arguments through constant practice. Indeed, one must use these arguments while we still "know only in part," so that we may arrive at the fullness of knowledge. But because the brevity I have proposed does not require me to pursue these things at length—especially since many more points could be gathered from them—let these words on singing suffice. Now, let us briefly follow the rules of diaphony.

## Chapter XVIII.
### On Diaphony, that is, the rule of organum.

"Diaphony" <term>diaphonia: from the Greek for "sounding apart," a term used here for early polyphony</term> means a separation of voices, which we call organum <term>organum: a style of medieval music where a second voice is added to a plainchant melody at a fixed interval</term>, where voices separated from each other are harmoniously dissonant and dissonantly concordant. They use it in such a way that a "fourth string" <note>the musical interval of a fourth</note> always follows the singer, such as A to D. If you double the organum through the high *a*, so that it is D and *a*, then A will sound a fourth <term>diatessaron: the musical interval spanning four notes of the scale</term> to D, and an octave <term>diapason: the musical interval spanning eight notes, a perfect consonance</term> to *a*. D, however, to...

<summary>Guido discusses the subjective beauty of music and how practice refines a composition, before introducing the technical rules for "organum" (early polyphony) based on the interval of a fourth.</summary>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the aesthetic perception of music based on individual temperament and transitions into the technical definitions and rules of "diaphony" or early polyphony, specifically the practice of organum.</meta>

— 105 —

Moreover, you must know that just like pure silver, every song <note>original: "Gesang" / Latin: "cantus"</note> gains more beauty the more it is used. That which at first is less pleasing is later highly praised once it has been smoothed by use as if by a file. Similarly, depending on the diversity of nations and temperaments, what displeases one person is eagerly embraced by another; one finds delight in unity <note>meaning consonance or melodic smoothness</note>, while another finds more pleasure in sharp contrasts. One person, due to a sensual nature, requires continuity and softness; another, of the former serious character, is moved by simple songs; yet another feeds almost foolishly on intricate and complicated flourishes <note>original: "Schnörkeleien," referring to complex musical ornamentation</note>. Each person, however, performs that song much more melodiously which he finds to be in accord with the innate character of his own spirit. All of this cannot remain foreign to you if you occupy yourself with constant practice in these matters. It is, however, necessary to make use of such instructions as long as one only partially knows the subject, in order to progress toward the fullness of science. But because the brevity I have set for myself does not allow me to pursue this subject further—especially since much more could be brought forward regarding this—let what has been said about song suffice. We will now briefly take up the rules of diaphony.

---

## Chapter XVIII.
### On Diaphony, that is, on the rules of Organum.

Diaphony <term>diaphonia: from the Greek for "sounding apart," a term for early polyphony where voices move in relation to one another</term> means that separation of tones which we call organum <term>organum: the earliest form of Western polyphony, involving the addition of at least one voice to a plainchant melody</term>, because the tones, though separated from each other, sound together in concord and yet are heard as distinct tones; though sounding differently, they yet harmonize. Diaphony is applied in such a way that the fourth <note>original: "Quart"</note> always accompanies the singer below, such as **A** to **D**. If the organum is doubled by the high **a**, so that it is **D. a.**, then here **A** sounds with **D** as—

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of diaphony—early polyphony—from page 105. It provides a concrete musical example using the antiphon "Miserere mei Deus" to illustrate how different intervals (fourths, fifths, and octaves) are layered to create harmony, or what the author calls "vocal couplings."</meta>

— 106 —

both [the lower and higher] **A** and **a** [form] a fourth <term>diatessaron: an interval of a perfect fourth</term> and a fifth <term>diapente: an interval of a perfect fifth</term>; the high **a** to the lower ones [forms] a fifth and an octave <term>diapason: an interval of a perfect octave</term>. And because these three species mingle with such sweetness in the fellowship of organum <note>the early practice of singing in parallel or related melodic lines</note> that they were shown above to create a similarity of voices as symphonies,¹) they are therefore aptly called vocal couplings, whereas "symphony" is used for the whole of song.²) This is an example of the aforementioned diaphony:

->Octave **c d e c d e d c c c ♮ a g c d e d d c.**<-

->Fifth **F G a F G a G F F F E D C F G a G G F.**<-
->*Have mercy on me, O God.*<-
->Fourth **C D E C D E D C C C B A Γ C D E D D C.**³)<-

This clearly corrected figure contains the leading voices of this antiphon *Have mercy on me, O God* <note>original Latin: *Miserere mei Deus*</note>, with the following voices organizing by the fourth, which is commonly called organum above⁴) the voice—that is, beneath them—it retains the leading high voices organizing by the fifth, which is called organum above the voice. This antiphon is in the *tritus* tone <note>the third pair of modes in the medieval system</note>, that is, the sixth mode, and the organum in it is lowered all the way to low **C**, to which the organum sometimes descends. For just as the low voices descend, so the high ones ascend. You can double both the song with the organum and the organum with the song through the octave as much as you like; for wherever its concord exists, the aforementioned joining of symphonies will not cease.

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of early medieval polyphony, specifically focusing on the mathematical and harmonic relationships between intervals. Fourth, to **a** as an octave, while **D** to both **A** and **a** [forms] a fourth (**DA**) and a fifth (**Da**); the high **a**, however, relates to the lower tones as a fifth (**a D**) and an octave (**A a**). — Since these three types of intervals mingle with such an intimate fellowship in the *organum* <note>the early practice of layering voices to create harmony</note> and with such sweetness that they produce a similarity of the tone rows—as shown in the previously presented songs¹)—they are fittingly called "vocal couplings," while the term "symphony" applies to every song.²) An example of the aforementioned diaphony <note>two-voiced singing</note> follows below:

->Octave. **c d e c d e d c c c ♯ a g c d e d d c.**<-
->Fifth. **F G a F G a G F F F E D C F G a G G F.**<-
->*Have mercy on me, O God.* <note>original Latin: *Miserere mei Deus*</note><-
->Fourth. **C D E C D E D C C C B A Γ C D E D D C.**³)<-

This figure, completely free of error, contains as its principal tones those of the antiphon *Have mercy on me, O God*; in the tones placed below it, the accompaniment is in the fourth, which is commonly called "organum below⁴) the voice"—that is, below these [principal tones]; in the high tones placed above, the accompaniment is in the fifth, which is called "organum above the voice." — This antiphon, however, belongs to the <term>tonus tritus: the third of the four main pairs of church modes, encompassing the 5th and 6th modes</term>, that is, the sixth mode, and the organum in it descends as far as the low **C**, down to which the organum occasionally goes: For just as the deep tones descend, so the high tones ascend. One can also double the song with the organum, and the organum with the song, as much as one pleases through the octave; for wherever its concord appears, the melodies will always be fitting.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of diaphony (early two-part singing) from page 107. While the previous page provided a basic example of voices moving in parallel intervals, this page introduces a more refined "soft" style that allows for greater melodic flexibility by using thirds and seconds to avoid dissonances or forbidden intervals like the fifth.</meta>

— 108 —

Since, therefore, the doubling of voices has now been sufficiently explained, let us explain the deep accompaniment <term>succentum: a lower vocal part that supports the main melody</term> provided by the singer, following the custom we use. Indeed, the former method of <term>diaphonia: two-voiced singing, also known as organum</term> is "hard," but ours is "soft," in which we do not allow the semitone <note>a half-step</note> or the <term>diapente: the interval of a perfect fifth</term>; instead, we allow the whole tone, the <term>ditonum: a major third (two whole tones)</term>, and the <term>semiditonum: a minor third (one and a half tones)</term> in these lower parts, while the <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term> holds the primary place.

Thus, the <term>organum: the added voice in early polyphony</term> follows the chant with these four harmonies. However, some of the <term>tropes: here referring to the church modes or melodic classifications</term> are suitable, others are more suitable, and some are the most suitable. Those that are "suitable" are those that provide an organum only through the fourth, with voices at a distance of four steps from each other, such as the <term>deuterus: the second mode-group, encompassing modes starting on E or B</term> in **B** and **E**. "More suitable" are those that respond not only with fourths but also with thirds and seconds through whole tones and minor thirds—though rarely—such as the <term>protus: the first mode-group, encompassing modes starting on D or A</term> in **A** and **D**. "Most suitable" are those that do this most frequently and most sweetly, such as the <term>tetrardus: the fourth mode-group, on G</term> and the <term>tritus: the third mode-group, on F</term> in **C**, **F**, and **G**.¹) These²)

---

¹) <note>original: "Der Sinn des bisherigen ist folgender..."</note> The meaning of the text so far is as follows: Formerly, the lower voice of the *organum* always moved with the chant in fourths. We, however, allow other intervals as well; namely, alongside the fourth, also the major and minor third, and the major second. Conversely, we do not permit the minor second and the fifth. For example, if a song begins with **F G**, the *organum* below the voice must move from **C** up to **D**; it cannot remain on **C**, like this: <image-desc>Musical notation in bass clef showing two vertical dyads: the first is C3 and F3, the second is C3 and G3.</image-desc> because then it would form a fifth (**C-G**) with the tone **G** of the chant, which is not permitted. But if the chant moves downward from **F** to **E** and **D**, then according to the rule of the ancients, the *organum* would also have had to descend from **C** to **B** (the note H) and **A** in fourths, like this: <image-desc>Musical notation in bass clef showing three vertical dyads in a descending sequence: F3 over C3, E3 over B2, and D3 over A2.</image-desc> We do not require that, however; rather, the **C** can remain stationary here: <image-desc>Musical notation in bass clef showing three vertical dyads where the bottom note remains C3 while the top notes descend: F3 over C3, E3 over C3, and D3 over C3.</image-desc> where it then corresponds with the **F** of the chant as a fourth, with the **E** as a major third, and with the **D** as a major second. This occurs, however...

<summary>This page describes a transition from rigid parallel organum in fourths to a more flexible "soft" style that incorporates thirds and seconds to avoid prohibited intervals like the fifth. It also ranks the medieval church modes based on how effectively they allow for these harmonic variations.</summary>
<keywords>organum, diaphonia, diatessaron, diapente, ditonum, church modes, protus, deuterus, tritus, tetrardus, music theory, polyphony</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 108 regarding "soft" diaphony—an early form of two-part singing. It elaborates on which musical modes are best suited for this style of accompaniment, specifically focusing on how different intervals (the distances between notes) sound when a lower voice is added to a chant melody.</meta>

— 109 —

Since the doubling of tones has now been sufficiently explained, we wish to clarify the accompaniment provided for the chant in the lower register, following the manner in which we apply it. The aforementioned style of <term>diaphony: an early form of part-singing</term> is "hard," whereas ours is "soft"; in our style, we do not permit the minor second <note>a "half-step," like B to C</note> or the fifth. We do, however, employ the major second, the major third, and the minor third, alongside the fourth; among these, the minor third holds the lowest rank, while the fourth takes precedence.

The chant is accompanied in the lower voice with these four harmonies. Among the various melodic turns, some are suitable, others more suitable, and others most suitable. "Suitable" are those that form the <term>organum: the voice added to a plainchant melody</term> through the fourth alone, using notes that are four steps apart, such as the <term>deuterus: the second mode-group, typically based on E</term> in **B** and **E**. "More suitable" are those that correspond not only with fourths but also with thirds and seconds—using the major second and minor third, though rarely—such as the <term>protus: the first mode-group, typically based on D</term> in **D** and **A**. Most suitable, however, are those that do this very frequently and with greater sweetness, such as the <term>tetrardus: the fourth mode-group, based on G</term> and <term>tritus: the third mode-group, based on F</term> in **C**, **F**, and **G**.¹) These ²) indeed work best

especially in pieces of the 5th, 6th (<term>tritus</term>), 7th, and 8th modes (<term>tetrardus</term>). In pieces of the 3rd and 4th modes (<term>deuterus</term>) on **E**, in such passages as **E D C**, the chant with the organum—if the organum remained stationary on **B** <note>original: "H"</note>—would result in the intervals of the fourth (**E–B**), the minor third (**D–B**), and the minor second (**C–B**).

<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff with a C-clef on the fourth line. It shows three vertical pairs of notes illustrating the intervals described: a perfect fourth (E over B), a minor third (D over B), and a minor second (C over B).</image-desc>

We do not allow this last interval at all, and the former, the minor third, we allow only reluctantly. (As stated above, it occupies the lowest rank <note>original: "infimatum"</note>.) Therefore, chants of the 3rd and 4th modes are not well-suited for this type of organum. More suitable are those of the 1st and 2nd modes (<term>protus</term>) on **D** or **a**, because here, in such passages, besides the fourth and the minor third, the major second (a better interval) occurs.

<image-desc>Musical notation on a five-line staff with a C-clef, showing a series of note pairs that transition from a fourth down to a third and then to a second, demonstrating the more favorable harmonic movement in the Protus mode.</image-desc>

The most suitable are the chants of the 5th and 6th modes (<term>tritus</term>), where, in addition to the fourth, the major third and major second occur.

---

²) *Hae* <note>Latin: "these"</note> refers to the preceding letters **C**, **F**, **G**, and thus to the <term>tetrardus</term> and <term>tritus</term> modes.

<summary>This page evaluates the suitability of different church modes for "soft" diaphony, explaining that modes based on F and G (Tritus and Tetrardus) are most ideal because they avoid harsh dissonances like the minor second.</summary>
<keywords>diaphony, organum, church modes, Protus, Deuterus, Tritus, Tetrardus, musical intervals, medieval harmony</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of "soft diaphony" or early part-singing. While the previous page categorized different musical modes by how well they accept an accompanying voice, this page focuses on the specific movement of that accompaniment—the "organum"—particularly around the note called the "tritus" (the third position in the scale, usually F or C).</meta>

— 110 —

for they follow the <term>tone: a whole step</term>, the <term>ditone: a major third</term>, and the <term>diatessaron: a fourth</term>; from the <term>tritus: the third note in the scale system, often F or C</term> ¹), where either the end of a musical phrase occurs or which sits closest to the final note itself, the <term>follower: the secondary voice in the organum</term> must never descend, unless the singer has introduced lower notes. Indeed, it is never permitted to lower the <term>organum: the voice added to the main chant</term> from the lowest *tritus* or its immediate substitute. 

However, when the singer introduces lower notes in a suitable place, and the accompaniment is lowered by a fourth, then as soon as that low passage is finished and not expected to be repeated, the follower should return to the position it held before. It does this so that it may remain with the final note if the melody lands on it, or, if the melody is above it, it may meet it gracefully in an <term>occursus: the "meeting" or coming together of two voices at the end of a phrase</term>.²) This meeting is better achieved by a whole tone, less so by a major third, and never by a minor third. 

A meeting of voices hardly ever happens at the interval of a fourth, as a low <term>succentus: a lower accompanying voice</term> is more pleasing in that position. However, one must take care that this does not happen in the final section of the <term>symphonia: a sounding together of voices, or harmony</term>.³) Often, when the singer introduces notes lower than the *tritus*, we keep the accompaniment suspended on the *tritus* itself; in that case, it is necessary that the singer does not end the phrase on those lower notes. Instead, with the voices moving quickly, he should assist the waiting *tritus* by returning to it, and having reached a conclusion in the higher register, he restores both his own note and the accompanying note.

Here, "tritus" designates—as explained more fully earlier (Page 55, Note 4; Page 58, Note 3)—the third tone of the four main scale series, but specifically the notes C and F, which are also explicitly called "tritus" in the following chapter.

²) The last two sentences are merely a repetition or a more precise clarification of the preceding sentence. ...according to the <term>major second: a whole step</term>, the <term>major third: an interval of two whole steps</term>, and the <term>fourth: an interval spanning four notes</term>. A meeting of voices hardly ever takes place at the interval of a fourth, because in such a place a low accompaniment is more pleasing. # Chapter XIX.
## Proof of the aforementioned Polyphony through examples.

Therefore, we do not lower the <term>organum: the voice added as an accompaniment to the main melody</term> from the <term>tritus: the F-step of the scale</term>, whether it ends on that note or on the ones following it, in this manner:

Top Voice: &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;F F G G F F D E F E D C
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;| | | | | | | | | | | |
Bottom Voice: C C D D C C C C C C C C
Lyrics: &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To him a-&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;-lone <note>original: "Ip-si so-li"</note>

Observe here the end of the musical phrase on the <term>tritus C: the note C acting as the stable base for the accompaniment</term>. We do not lower the organum voice from this note because it does not have a <term>whole tone: a major second</term> or a <term>ditone: a major third</term> beneath it to create a <term>meeting: the "occursus," where the two voices converge on a single pitch</term>; instead, it has a <term>semiditone: a minor third</term>, through which a meeting does not occur. <gloss>3)</gloss>

Top Voice: &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;F G G a G G F
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;| | | | | | |
Bottom Voice: C D D E D D C
Lyrics: &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I keep the faith <note>original: "ser-vo fi-dem"</note>

Look at this other phrase in the <term>tritus F</term>, in which we follow the notes at the distance of a <term>fourth: an interval of a diatessaron</term> below, and this fourth-based accompaniment is more pleasing than the convergence of the voices:

# Chapter XIX.
## A closer look at the aforementioned Diaphony through examples.

First, therefore, we do not let the <term>organum: the secondary, accompanying voice in early polyphony</term> descend below the <term>tritus</term>, whether the phrase ends on this note or continues further, for example:

(1st Example:)
**Principal Voice:** F F G G F F D E F E D C
**Text:** To Him a-lone <note>original Latin: "Ipsi soli"</note>
**Organum (Accompaniment):** C C D D C C C C C C C

Above, one sees a <term>cadence: the concluding sequence of notes that brings a musical phrase to rest</term> on the <term>tritus</term> C, below which we do not allow the organum to descend. This is because that note has neither a whole tone nor a major third beneath it—the only intervals through which an <term>occursus</term> <note>the "meeting" or convergence of two voices onto a single unison note</note> can take place. It has only a minor third beneath it, which does not facilitate a proper occursus. <gloss>3) see Chapter XVIII, Note 2, page 110.</gloss>

(2nd Example:)
**Principal Voice:** F G G a G G F
**Text:** I keep the faith <note>original Latin: "servo fidem"</note>
**Organum:** C D E D D C

Above, one sees another cadence on the <term>tritus</term> F, which we accompany with <term>fourths: an interval spanning four notes of the scale</term> at a distance of four tones; here, the accompaniment in fourths is more pleasing than the occursus.

This would then be a cadence in the <term>deuterus: the second pair of musical modes, centered on the note E</term> on E, in which a meeting by a major third—either simple or <term>interrupted: "intermissus," where the voices do not move directly to the unison but pass through another note</term>—was preferred <gloss>1)</gloss>.

(6th Example:) C F F D F CD D C DF E C ED (Principal voice),
->Come to teach us the way of wis-dom. <note>original Latin: "Veni ad docendum nos viam prudentiae."</note><-
->C C C C C CC A C CC C C CD (Organum)<-

Above is a cadence on the first tone, A. In this cadence, notes in a lower position than the tritus C are permitted; specifically, that note (A) which is placed below the D standing at the conclusion (of the cadence at A); after the conclusion of the low position, the first place (C) is taken up again, where it says: *the way of wisdom*. Similarly also in the following cadence:

***

...makes no sense. It seems therefore that F F D E is indeed correct, and the "interrupted" <note>original Latin: "intermissus"</note> should be understood in such a way that a kind of "hidden" meeting occurs here, in contrast to the simple (*simplex*) one; the voice, in descending from F to D, lightly passes by E (as long as there is even a small <term>following: "obsecutio," a term used by Guido of Arezzo to describe how the organum voice follows the logic of the principal melody</term>, Chapter XVIII) and thus a meeting of a major third still takes place. According to this, a "simple" and an "interrupted" meeting would be comparable to our modern open and hidden fifths and octaves. — Also, the unclear passage referring to this (according to Note 3, Page 113) would receive a satisfactory explanation in this way. —
