<meta>Following the practical musical examples of diaphony and the use of the 'tritus' mode, this page shifts to a historical and theoretical foundation, explaining the legendary discovery of musical proportions by Pythagoras.</meta>

## Chapter 20.
### How music was discovered from the sound of hammers.

Regarding the origin of the art of music, because we saw that the reader was a novice, we initially remained silent; we have now assigned this history to the more practiced and knowledgeable <note>Manuscript 9921 reads: "and more thirsty" (meaning more eager for knowledge) instead of "knowledgeable."</note> reader. In ancient times, instruments were uncertain and the multitude of those singing was "blind": for no human was able to grasp the differences of voices and the discernment of <term>symphony: the harmonious sounding of different pitches together</term> by any systematic reasoning. Nor could anyone ever know anything certain about this art, until at last divine goodness disposed what follows by its own will.

When Pythagoras, that great philosopher, happened to be traveling, he came upon a smithy in which five hammers were striking upon one anvil. Marveling at their sweet concord, the philosopher approached. At first, hoping that the power of the sound and the melody existed in the variety of the hands, he switched the hammers among the men; once this was done, the same power of sound followed each specific hammer. Therefore, having set aside the one that was discordant with the others, he weighed the remaining ones. In a wondrous manner, by divine will, the first weighed 12, the second 9, the third 8, and the fourth 6—by whatever units of weight they were measured. He thus recognized that the science of music consists in the proportion and comparison of numbers.

That arrangement found in the four hammers was the same as what is now found in the four letters A, D, E, and a. Finally, if the low A has 12 <note>This refers to the numerical weight or string length representing the pitch.</note> and the high "a" has 6, the high "a" stands in relation to the low A in that proportion which, in Arithmetic, is called "duple" <note>original Latin: "dupla." The 2:1 ratio (12:6) creates the interval of an octave.</note>.

---

<vocab>Pythagoras, music, hammers, proportion, Arithmetic, symphony, discernment, 12, 9, 8, 6</vocab>

<summary>This page recounts the foundational legend of Pythagoras discovering musical intervals—the octave, fifth, and fourth—by observing the mathematical ratios of hammers in a blacksmith's forge. It marks the transition from music as an intuitive practice to music as a scientific discipline based on numerical proportions.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, blacksmith hammers, musical proportions, mathematical harmony, origin of music, intervals, octave, arithmetic</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the practical application of musical modes to the theoretical and legendary origins of music, specifically focusing on the discovery of mathematical proportions by the philosopher Pythagoras.</meta>

## Chapter XX.
### How Music Was Discovered from the Sound of Hammers.

Regarding the origin of music, we initially remained silent because we found the reader was not yet well-instructed; now, however, we wish to share this history with those who are practiced and have advanced in knowledge <note>The German editor notes that Manuscript 9921 uses a phrase meaning "more thirsty [for knowledge]" here.</note>. In ancient times, there were instruments not fully known to us and a great number of singers who practiced this art only blindly; for no person was able to grasp the variety of tones and the distinction of <term>consonances: musical intervals, such as the octave or fifth, that sound stable and pleasant together</term> through any systematic proof. Indeed, no one would have ever discovered anything certain about this art, if divine goodness had not finally brought about the following event at its own signal.

Once, when a certain Pythagoras, a great philosopher, happened to be traveling, he came upon a workshop where five hammers were forging upon an anvil. Marveling at their sweet concord, the philosopher approached. Since he first suspected that the cause of the tone and its harmony lay in the variety of the smiths' hands, he had them swap hammers. After this was done, the same musical effect followed each specific hammer. He therefore set aside the discordant hammer from the others and weighed the remaining ones. In a wondrous manner, by divine providence, the first weighed 12 units, the second 9, the third 8, and the fourth 6—of whatever unit of weight they were. He recognized from this that the science of music is based upon the proportion and comparison of numbers. 

The same mutual relationship existed between those four hammers as now exists between the four notes A, D, E, and a. If, accordingly, the low A corresponds to the number 12 <note>The number 12 here represents a mathematical weight or string length used to calculate intervals.</note> and the high "a" corresponds to the number 6, then the high "a" stands in a relationship to the low A that, in arithmetic, is called the— <note>The text breaks off here; the mathematical relationship between 12 and 6 is 2:1, which defines the musical octave.</note>

---

<note>original: "Von 'et a acuta' bis 'et si A habet XII.' incl. fehlt in Cod. 9921."</note>
<margin>The section from "and the high a" to "and if A has 12" inclusive is missing in Manuscript 9921.</margin>

<summary>This page recounts the legendary discovery of musical intervals by Pythagoras, who realized that the "sweet concord" of blacksmith hammers was due to their mathematical weights (12, 9, 8, 6) rather than the strength of the smiths.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, music theory, mathematical proportions, musical intervals, smithy hammers, harmony, history of music</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mathematical ratios discovered by Pythagoras in the blacksmith's shop, specifically applying the weights of the four hammers (12, 9, 8, and 6 units) to the fundamental intervals of music theory.</meta>

...in music, however, this is the <term>diapason: an octave interval, the most perfect consonance</term> consonance. But the low D, which is 9, with the low E, which is 8, maintains a relationship which in arithmetic is <term>sesquioctave: a ratio of 9:8, where the larger number contains the smaller plus one-eighth of it</term>, that is, <term>epogdous: the Greek term for the 9:8 ratio</term>; in music, however, this is the consonance of a whole tone. 

Likewise, the high "a" with the low E—just as the low D with the low A—maintains a relationship which in arithmetic is <term>sesquitertia: a ratio of 4:3, where the larger number contains the smaller plus one-third of it</term>, and in music is the <term>diatessaron: a perfect fourth interval</term> consonance. 

Likewise, the high "a" with the low D—just as the low E with the low A—maintains a relationship which in arithmetic is <term>sesquialtera: a ratio of 3:2, where the larger number contains the smaller plus one-half of it</term>, and in music is the <term>diapente: a perfect fifth interval</term> consonance. 

And if A has 12 and D has 9 as steps of three,¹) then A will have four groups of three in its 12, and D will have three groups of three in its 9. ²) 

Again, since A has 12 and D holds 9,³) A will have three steps of four, while E <note>represented by the number 8</note> will have two, and the fifth <note>diapente</note> is revealed. 

Let there be again 12 in A and 6 in the other "a". Six is half of twelve, just as the high "a" is gathered from half of the other A. Thus, the octave <note>diapason</note> is present. 

Thus, A itself⁴) yields a fourth to D, a fifth to E, and an octave to the other "a". D also sounds a tone to E, and a fourth or fifth to both "A" and "a". And E also sends a tone to D, and a fifth or fourth to both "A" and "a": while the high "a" sounds an octave with A, a fifth with D, and a fourth with E. 

A curious investigator will find all these things in the aforementioned numbers. For from this starting point, <term>Boethius: An influential 6th-century philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music theory textbook of the Middle Ages</term>, the revealer of this art, demonstrated the great, wondrous, and most difficult harmony of this art through the proportion of numbers. What more? Through the aforementioned species of the mono- <note>The text likely continues as "monochord," a single-stringed instrument used to demonstrate these mathematical ratios.</note>

<margin>3) Manuscript 9921 reads: "Again since A has 12 and E holds 9."</margin>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of musical intervals using the Pythagorean proportions (12, 9, 8, and 6), which were established on the previous page as the weights of the blacksmith's hammers. This page specifically maps these numbers to the notes A, D, E, and a.</meta>

-> — 123 — <-

...double, but in music it represents the consonance of the octave. The low D, which corresponds to the number 9, relates to the low E (equal to 8) in the ratio which in arithmetic is called <term>sesquioctave: a ratio of 9:8 where the larger number contains the smaller plus an eighth part of it</term> <note>original: "Sesquioctava"</note>, that is, containing one and one-eighth <note>Greek: ἐπόγδοος (epogdoos)</note>, but in music is designated as the consonance of the whole tone. Similarly, the high "a" relates to the low E just as the low D relates to the low A in the ratio which in arithmetic is called <term>sesquitertia: a ratio of 4:3 where the larger number contains the smaller plus a third part of it</term> <note>Greek: ἐπίτριτος (epitritos)</note>, but in music is designated as the consonance of the fourth. Similarly, the high "a" relates to the low D just as the low E relates to the low A in the ratio which in arithmetic is called <term>sesquialtera: a ratio of 3:2 where the larger number contains the smaller plus a half part of it</term> <note>Greek: ἐπιδεύτερος (epideuteros)</note>, and in music is called the consonance of the fifth.

Since A = 12 and D = 9 are both divisible by 3 ¹), A contains four groups of three in its 12, and D contains three groups of three in its 9 ²). Furthermore, since A = 12 and E = 8 ³) are divisible by 4, A contains three groups of four, while E contains two groups of four, and thus the fifth is revealed. Let A again be 12 and the other "a" be 6; six is half of twelve, just as the high "a" is obtained by the middle (halving) of the other A. Thus, the octave is produced.

So, A ⁴) itself creates a fourth with D, a fifth with E, and an octave with the other "a". D sounds against E as a whole tone, and against both As (the low A and high a) as a fourth (DA) and a fifth (Da). E likewise creates a whole tone with D, and with both As a fifth (EA) and a fourth (Ea); the high "a," however, sounds as an octave to A, a fifth to D, and a fourth to E. The curious researcher will find all of this in the numbers cited above. Proceeding from this, <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), whose work on music was the standard university text for over a thousand years</note> **Boethius**, the expander of this art, demonstrated the manifold, wonderful, and extremely precise correspondence of this art with the ratios of numbers. In short! With the aforementioned numerical ratios—

⁶) <note>Note referencing the variation in the text</note> Codex 9921 reads: <note>original Latin: "Cum igitur A"</note> "When therefore A."

Pythagoras first constructed the <term>monochord<note>A single-stringed instrument used in the Middle Ages as a scientific tool to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note></term>, in which—because it is no frivolous toy, but rather a clear representation of this art provided with great diligence—all scholars have commonly found delight. ## The following works by the same author have appeared and can be obtained directly from him, as well as through all bookstores:

**Gradual <note>original: "Graduale"; a liturgical book containing the choral chants used during the Mass.</note> arranged according to the use of the Cathedral Church of Trier. This has been most accurately transcribed from the old original codices and expanded with newly ordained or granted feasts. Published with the approval of the Superiors by Michael Hermesdorff, Priest of the Diocese of Trier. 53 sheets, octavo, 4 Marks 50 Pfennigs.**

The present Gradual is edited according to the best and oldest parchment manuscripts of the Trier choir books and therefore offers the highest interest for all friends of choral chant.

**Antiphonal <note>original: "Antiphonale"; a liturgical book containing the chants for the Divine Office, the daily cycle of prayer.</note> arranged according to the use of the Cathedral Church of Trier. This has been most accurately transcribed from the old original codices and expanded with newly ordained or granted feasts. Published with the approval of the Superiors by Michael Hermesdorff, Priest of the Diocese of Trier. 64 sheets, octavo, 4 Marks 50 Pfennigs.**

In this Antiphonal, also edited according to the oldest parchment manuscripts, are contained not only the Vespers chants for all days and feasts of the year, but also the chants for Lauds, Prime, Terce, Sext, None, Compline, and the complete Office of the last three days of Holy Week. It is therefore a complete <term>Diurnal<note>A book containing the daytime prayers of the Divine Office.</note></term> with musical notation and is thus equally interesting from a liturgical and musical perspective.

**Prefaces <note>original: "Praefationes"; the prayers of thanksgiving that introduce the most solemn part of the Mass.</note> in the Trier chant, most accurately transcribed and offered publicly by Michael Hermesdorff, Priest of the Diocese of Trier. 8 sheets, large folio. Black and red print, 1 Mark 50 Pfennigs.**

Edited according to old missal <term>incunabula<note>Books printed before the year 1501, during the earliest stages of the printing press.</note></term> with comparison to the last Trier Missal editions of 1608 and 1610, these also offer great interest for friends of the chant and its history. The format is chosen so that they can be bound into a Roman Missal of any size. The printing is produced with new, sharp types in red and black, on strong paper in an elegant finish.

**Kyriale <note>A collection of chants for the Ordinary of the Mass, such as the Kyrie, Gloria, and Sanctus.</note> or the Ordinary of the Mass for the various seasons and feasts throughout the year. Including the Masses for the Dead, Antiphons for the sprinkling of holy water, votive Masses of the Blessed Sacrament and of the Blessed Virgin Mary, and various sacred songs for the Elevation, the Benediction, etc. 21 sheets, large folio. Black and red print, 6 Marks, bound 9 Marks.**

This Kyriale contains the common Mass chants as they appear in the parchment manuscripts of the 13th, 14th, and 15th centuries, and partially in even older <term>neume<note>An early system of musical notation used before the invention of the five-line staff.</note></term> manuscripts.

**Harmony of Plainsong <note>original: "Harmonia cantus choralis"</note>, containing the Trier chant in four-part harmonization, edited according to the newly published Trier choral books by Michael Hermesdorff, Priest of the Diocese of Trier, Cathedral Organist and teacher of singing at the Episcopal Priestly Seminary there. Trier, Fr. Lintz’sche Bookstore. Six divisions with a supplement booklet. 11 Marks 80 Pfennigs.**

The above work forms a carefully prepared organ accompaniment to all parts of the Gradual and Antiphonal, which is so arranged that all choral pieces (Masses, Offertories, Hymns, Psalms, Magnificat, etc.) can be performed simultaneously by a four-part choir of singers. The individual divisions of this work are also available separately, specifically:

**Twelve Motets** by older masters, arranged as Offertories for the major feasts of the year in an easy arrangement, and dedicated in deepest reverence to His Episcopal Grace, the Right Reverend Dr. Mathias Eberhard <note>Mathias Eberhard was the Bishop of Trier from 1867 to 1876.</note>, by Michael Hermesdorff, Cathedral Organist and President of the Diocesan Cecilian Society of Trier. Score 1 Mark.

**First Vespers** for the Feast of Corpus Christi <note>original: "hl. Frohnleichnamsfeste"</note>, including Psalms, Hymn, and Magnificat for four-part mixed choir, along with a motet for the Holy Blessing <note>A motet used during the Benediction of the Blessed Sacrament.</note>. Score 1 Mark.

**Requiem Mass** harmonized for four-part men's choir by Michael Hermesdorff. Score 50 Pfennigs. Discounts available for bulk orders.

**Singing School** for the systematic instruction of church choirs, also suitable as a guide for singing lessons in all higher educational institutions. Composed and published based on his own long-standing observations and experiences by Michael Hermesdorff, Cathedral Organist and Music Director. 1 Mark.

Alongside the very clearly presented explanations of tone, notation, time, rhythm, tempo, and the study of intervals, the aforementioned Singing School contains a rich, well-organized collection of pure sight-singing exercises, as well as rhythmic-melodic vocal, sight-singing, and performance exercises. These will very quickly lead the student to a level of technical perfection and enable them to fully occupy their place in any choir.

<summary>This page lists the published musical works and educational materials of Michael Hermesdorff, specifically focusing on polyphonic masses, motets, and a vocal manual for choirs. It highlights the integration of 19th-century Trier's local church hierarchy with the broader Cecilian movement for musical reform.</summary>

<meta>This page serves as the title and advertisement page for the 15th volume of the journal *Caecilia*, continuing the catalog of Michael Hermesdorff’s efforts to document and reform Catholic church music in the late 19th century.</meta>

# CAECILIA,
## Journal for Catholic Church Music
->edited<-
->by<-
## Michael Hermesdorff.
->With an autographic supplement. <note>A supplement containing lithographic reproductions of handwritten musical scores or manuscripts.</note><-
## 15th Volume.

This journal, which also serves as the organ <note>The official publication or voice of an organization.</note> of the Choral Society <note>The Choral-Verein, dedicated to the study and promotion of Gregorian chant.</note>, is published monthly in the size of one sheet <note>A "sheet" (Bogen) was a standard printer’s measure, usually resulting in about 16 pages when folded.</note> along with an autographic supplement containing original compositions and highly interesting and valuable facsimiles <note>Exact reproductions</note> of ancient choral manuscripts from the 10th to the 15th centuries. It can be obtained through all bookstores, post offices, and directly from the publisher for an annual subscription price of 4 Marks. The exceptionally refined, educational, and highly interesting content gives this journal an enduring value. It is sent free of charge to members of the Choral Society in return for the annual membership fee of 6 Marks, along with an additional autographic supplement presenting more specialized research results on ancient choral manuscripts. Registrations for the Choral Society are to be sent directly to the editor of *Caecilia*, Cathedral Music Director M. Hermesdorff in Trier (148 Banthus Street). The supplements intended for members of the Choral Society, along with *Caecilia*, can be purchased through bookstores for 8 Marks per year.

->**The Eight Low Keys** <note>original: "Octo claves graves." These represent the fundamental notes at the bottom of the musical scale.</note><-

|---|---|

---

<margin>
**Tetrachord of the Middle Notes.** <note>original: "Tetrachordum meson = mediarum." A tetrachord is a scale of four notes spanning a perfect fourth, a concept inherited from Ancient Greek music theory.</note>
</margin>
<margin>
**Tetrachord of the Principal Notes.** <note>original: "Tetrachordum hypaton = principalium." In the Greek system, "hypaton" refers to the "highest" in importance or string length, though it sounds lowest in pitch.</note>
</margin>
<margin>
**The Added Note.** <note>original: "Proslambanomenos = Adsumta." The lowest note in the Ancient Greek Greater Perfect System, added to complete a two-octave range.</note>
</margin>

->**Octave.** <note>original: "Diapason." The Greek term for an interval of an eighth.</note><-

->**Two Octaves** <note>original: "Bis DIAPASON." A double-octave range.</note><-
->**Normal Range** <note>German: "Gewöhnlicher Umfang."</note><-

<note>This table demonstrates a medieval method of assigning musical pitches to vowels (a, e, i, o, u). The text used is the end of the hymn "Ut queant laxis," which was famously used by <term>Guido d'Arezzo</term>—a 11th-century music theorist—to teach singers the ascending scale. Each syllable of the hymn originally started on a higher note, giving us the names for the notes of the scale (Ut, Re, Mi, Fa, Sol, La).</note>
